# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum IV

## Funding proposal package for FP256

### Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:
- a) A funding proposal titled "Intensification of Agriculture and Agroforestry Techniques (IAAT) for Climate Resilient Food and Nutrition Security: Tombuctou, Gao, Mopti and Dogon regions of Mali";
- b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);
- c) Secretary's assessment;
- d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;
- e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and
- f) Gender documentation.

This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public website in that context, which does not imply a formal outcome. Decisions taken in relation to this document and updates to it if any will be made publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

# Climate change evidence and problem:

1. Mali is ranked 11th most vulnerable to climate change and 237th least ready for climate change adaptation and mitigation according to the 2018 World Risk Database Global Adaptation Indicator (ND-GAIN) index.
2. Over the last 60 years, Mali has experienced the following key climate trends: a mean annual temperature increase of 0.5°C; an increase in extreme temperatures with more frequent heat waves and fewer cold spells; an increase in intensity of climate hazards, particularly droughts and floods across the region, and related to this is increased heat stress on livestock and crops; and a decrease in rainfall over most parts of Mali.
3. According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), with a drought severity index of -5, which indicates severe drought conditions, Mali has been classified as one of 10 countries most vulnerable to climate hazard, making up 50% of all climate hazard occurrences from 1986 to 2003.
4. It is estimated that 70% of the population in Mali lives below the poverty line and more than half of them live in the Mopti and Gao regions in particular; and that 500,000 people are currently affected by floods each year.

## Under both Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5 and RCP 8.5 emissions scenarios, Mali is likely to experience a temperature increase of between 1°C and 3°C over the period from 2010-2039 under baseline conditions.
6. According to the IPCC, under both RCPs, the projected increase in mean annual temperatures for Mali shows that against a baseline historical period of 1981–2010, Mali is likely to experience temperature increases of between 1°C and 4°C over the period from 2010-2039 (RCP 4.5) or up to 6°C by 2070 under RCP 8.5.

### 6\. Mali's total green greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions are rising but remain very low relative to global averages. Emissions have been rising steadily with total emissions estimated at 44 million tons of Carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) in 2015, up from an estimated 38 million tons in 2010.* Emissions per capita worldwide and Mali's per capita emissions (in t CO2e per capita) are approximately only 4% of the global average.** The country has a low level of energy access and is heavily dependent on biomass for cooking, heating, and lighting. Mali’s population growth rate is projected to increase due to its population growth, the expansion of electricity access, deforestation for agriculture and mining, and urbanization.* Mali is also one of 195 countries that have committed to reducing their emissions in order to meet nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) in the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC)* by 2030 when they aim to reduce emissions by 25% in the agriculture sector.*

#### Purpose and Objectives

1\. The International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) will support the Government of Mali to implement the International Action Team (IAT) on Agriculture and Agroforestry Technologies (IAAT) to achieve the following objectives: a) increasing the climate resilience in agriculture systems and reducing GHG emissions, IAAT will achieve this by supporting the development of climate-resilient agricultural technologies and practices; b) addressing climate risk-informed decisions, including scaling locally Led Adaptation (LLA) actions to address vulnerability to climate change and its impacts on food security and livelihoods; c) promoting the adoption of Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) adoption; d) supporting the development of CSA and agroforestry value chains; e) reducing GHG emissions from agriculture, forestry, and other land use sectors through the promotion of sustainable agricultural practices and technologies; f) increasing access to climate information services for smallholder farmers.

2\. The IAAT will be implemented in 5 regions (Ségou, Koulikoro, Mopti, Segou, and Tombouctou) and highly vulnerable communities. It will be supported by a national steering committee, regional focal points, national and regional workshops, stakeholder consultations, and climate change vulnerability assessment. IAAT will

This project will establish circle-level LLA plan development committees (LLA-DCs) to develop, review, approve, and monitor LLA plans in collaboration with local government officials (e.g., commune chiefs, district officers), representatives from the local government, the communes will provide input into LLA plans, achieve equity in access to resources, and ensure that LLA plans are consistent with national policies. ### 9.4 ARTS AND TECHNOLOGY IMPLEMENTATION (ATI)

The ATI component will support the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices, technology transfer (e.g., solar pump and biodigester), and involvement of private sector value chain actors to promote the CSA value chain in the project area. The ATI component will also support the development of a community-based adaptation strategy that includes the identification of key stakeholders such as local government officials, farmers, women's groups, youth organizations, community and social institutions and community structures such as CAGs and VSILAs. IAT will use these stakeholder groups to identify and address potential barriers and challenges in implementing ATI activities.

### 10. ARTS AND TECHNOLOGY IMPLEMENTATION (ATI)

The ATI component will support the adoption of CSA practices, including youth and women agripreneurs, increase their resilience to current and future risks of droughts, floods, and heatwaves by building on existing community-based adaptation strategies. The ATI component will also support climate-smart agriculture farming. In addition, the IAT will indirectly support a further 705,980 beneficiaries, by working with local government officials, farmers' organizations, and other stakeholders to provide training for farmers across the project regions. Strengthening the capacity of public extension agents will create public goods and services that will benefit all smallholder farmers in the project area (Annex B 3.2). The land use impacts associated with the adoption of CSA practices across smallholder farms and public agricultural lands are expected to be minimal, as most of these practices are already being used.

### 11. THE IMPACTS OF CLIMATE CHANGE AND ADAPTATION STRATEGIES

The IAT will also support the implementation of a national climate change adaptation strategy in Mali. The Children Federation, Inc (CSU). Mali regional and local government authorities will also engage in the project implementation process to ensure that the LLA plans are consistent with national policies. The National Climate Change Management Committee (NCCMC) acts as the secretariat for the National Climate Change Commission (NCNCM). The NCNC plays a central role in coordinating climate change adaptation efforts at the national level and provides technical support to the NCCMC.

### 12. IN ADDITION TO DELIVERING THE 3 COMPONENTS, THE IAT WILL ALSO ACHIEVE THE FOLLOWING CO-BENEFITS AS LISTED BELOW:

- Empowerment of women and youth through increased access to resources and services.
- Enhanced enabling environment, and adoption of a range of CSA and agroforestry technologies will help smallholder farmers increase their productivity and income.
  - Co-benefit 1Gender: Increased gender equality and economic empowerment
  - Co-benefit 2 Environment: Improved soil health and water resources from land use improvements

# PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate Context (page: 1 of 2 pages, approximately 2 pages)

### Socio-economic Context

13. Mali has a young and growing population facing significant challenges to improve their livelihoods in the face of climate change. The median age is Mali is 16.3 years in 2025*, compared to the World average of 30 years.
* * *
14. More than 47% of the population is less than 15 years old.* and 40% are aged between 15 and 40. The rapid population growth rate (2.8%) is one of the highest in Africa, with a high dependency ratio. This age structure is particularly among youth aged 15 to 29.
* * *
Less than half of this age group's workforce is employed,* with an estimated unemployment rate of 40% for those between 15 and 24 years old. The youth are often considered.* * * This pervasive unemployment is a significant driver of poverty and poses a threat to social cohesion and security in Mali, particularly among young people living in urban areas.
* * *
The population faces specific challenges related to climate change, including increased vulnerability to extreme weather events (inputs, equipment, finance; etc.) and limited awareness of skills related to existing technologies and practices. The country's agricultural sector is highly vulnerable to climate change, with a decline in the share of arable land from 51% in 2019 to 43% in 2021*, further emphasized by declining purchasing power and leading to limited capacity to adapt to climate change.
* * *
Vulnerability to climate change is highly linked with the rural populations, which account for 90% of the country's population. The majority of these people live on less than $1 a day, and more than half are undernourished. In addition, the population density, reaching just one person per km² in remote eastern and northeastern regions.* * *
The country's and areas' vulnerability to climate change is also linked with its geography. Mali has a vast territory of 1240,000 km². The Niger river provides water resources; lands outside the Niger valley are often barren or desert, making agriculture difficult in these areas.
* * *
The population concentration on land along the Niger River, where more agricultural opportunities and livelihood prospects exist,* * * is a significant factor of vulnerability to climate change. This area also has a high population density, providing fertile agricultural soil and pasture for livestock.
* * *
These factors make Mali particularly vulnerable to climate change, threatening the livelihood resilience of these vulnerable populations in Mali, particularly its young and rural populations.* * *
Mali has been played by conflict and instability since 2013. The country's political situation is characterized by a weak state of governance, poor state of the country has weakened governance, increased inequality, and caused heightened insecurity, prompting large-scale displacement of people.
* * *
The country's population reached 19 million in 2020, with an estimated 45% living below the poverty line. ### Land tenure security and climate change adaptation

16. In many parts of Mali, land is concentrated among rural communities. Although the 2017 Agricultural Land Law aimed to provide farmers with increased access to land ownership, its implementation at the local level has been slow.
17. Studies indicate that in some locations, the lack of law enforcement and ambiguity regarding land ownership contribute to conflicts between sedentary farmers, pastoralists, and transhumant pastoralists, who have access to water and pasture.
18. But currently, land ownership rights are still executed by the Territorial Collectives (sub-national government structures in Mali) and local governments.
19. Limited knowledge of land registration processes, combined with high transaction costs, low literacy rates, limited knowledge of land registration processes, and financial constraints.
20. Women face additional challenges due to their lower access to land ownership rights compared to men.

### Financial inclusion

21. Access to formal financial institutions is highly uneven in Mali. While 83% of households have access to formal financial institutions, the expansion of financial services has mostly benefited urban areas and larger enterprises.
22. Microfinance institutions (MFIs) are established to address this gap by providing financial services to small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs).
23. Micro-finance institutions (MFIs) were established to address this gap by providing financial services to small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs).

### Climate change adaptation

24. Mali is ranked 157 out of 189 countries on the UNDP Gender Inequality Index.
25. Despite several reforms, significant socio-economic inequalities persist in Mali, hindering their health, education, participation in governance, and social and economic independence.

### Education and women's empowerment

26. Women's labor force participation is significantly lower than men, with 30% of the female population employed compared to 58% for males.
27. Educational attainment for women is among the lowest globally, with limited access to education in rural areas.
28. Women are predominantly viewed as farmers responsible for feeding their households, making decision power and labor allocation decisions within the household.

### Climate change adaptation initiatives

29. Droughts dominate Mali's climate, combined with inadequate infrastructure and services, further restricting their role for business and livelihoods.

Mal’s population is young; children urgently require assistance to address their food security, health, nutrition, and education needs. Mal’s Agriculture Extension System

The structure of the management of agriculture extension in Mali is presented below (Figure B 1.1). Mali has three levels of administrative offices: national, regional and local circle level. There are 8 regions, 49 circles, and 302 communes. The agricultural office manager (ACM) at the regional level works with various district directors such as the commune and village. The agriculture extension in Mali operates at the national, regional, and local (circle) levels. At the national level, the National Director of Agriculture (NDA) is responsible for the overall management of the agriculture extension service. At the regional level, the Regional Director of Agriculture (RDA) works with various district officers of agriculture, fisheries, livestock, book literacy, and farmer organization and the technical director of the Rural Polytechnic Training Center (RPTC). The RPTC is responsible for training extension activities at the national level. At the circle level, the agricultural office manager (ACM) and the Local Director of Agriculture (LDA) work with various district officers such as the commune and village. The LDA ensures the effective delivery of extension to the farmers in the villages. In addition, various private sector institutions are involved in agriculture extension activities at all levels. These institutions are determined by the National Directorate for Vocational and Technical Education under the Ministry of Education; For example, the National Institute of Agricultural Research (INRA) is responsible for training the Regional Director of Agriculture in the Sahel, and The Rural Polytechnic Training Center, offering trainings programs for agroforestry technicians, animal husbandry, fisheries, and horticulture. Other private sector institutions include consulting professionals and farmers. Other private sector actors such as agriculture input suppliers, equipment sellers, contractors, transporters, aggregrators, traders, importers, exporters, and others also offer extension and advisory services.

Figure B 1.1: Structure of agriculture extension system in Mali

## 21. The field agent and farmer groups are key elements of the agriculture extension system at the village level. The field agents, therefore, need to be well trained in diverse areas of agriculture/agronomy and constantly assisted by the national and regional committees of coordination.

The field agents set up experimental and control plots to demonstrate proven technologies to farmer groups. They also provide training on the use of new technologies, including the application of agrochemicals, crop contents and the environmental practices of producers. The agriculture extension, therefore, focused on organizing an extension system that is based on a network of field agents who are well trained in diverse areas.

The link between extension, research, and farmers' program planning, involvement of women in programs, and increasing access to credit for smallholder farmers is critical.

## 22. Training and knowledge transfer under the agriculture extension are planned, structured, organized, and implemented through a series of activities such as training workshops, field visits, practical demonstrations, review meetings, monthly technology review workshops, fortnight training sessions, specific trainings, and experience exchange visits. The field agents are responsible for organizing these activities in their respective areas and identifying and prioritizing issues to concern producers. The field agent is the essential link in this system because he/she is the implementer of all extension activities.

The field agents should be well trained in diverse areas such as business development, production, storage, processing, and marketing. They are also required to be trained in many areas and expected to transfer knowledge in diverse areas of agriculture to producers.

### Climate Context

#### Climate Change
At village level: Mali has high exposure to climate hazards and is ill-equipped to adapt to climate change. Mali is ranked 175 in vulnerability to climate change and 129th in readiness to adapt to the impacts of climate change, according to the World Bank's (2015) Climate Vulnerability Index.

The impact of climate change arises both directly through climate drivers and hazards (high drivers include temperature increase, rainfall decrease, and extreme weather events), as well as indirectly through resource-driven conflict. The following sections outline Mali’s climate trends including current and future climate data, their impacts on the country's economy, and the country's current GHG profile.

### Climate Context - historical and current trends

#### Climate Drivers
At village level: Mali is one of the hottest countries in the world with an average mean temperature of ~28°C. Temperature is seasonal with the coldest average temperatures of around 22°C in December / January in the dry season, and the highest average temperatures of about 35°C during the wet season (June to September). This results in widespread extreme heat events with the majority of Mali (in terms of geography).

Mali is ranked among the countries that are most vulnerable to drought according to the International Food Policy Research Institute (IFPRI). The Palmer index indicates that 2019 was the highest level of drought, based on the Palmer severity index. At present, it is estimated that four hundred thousand people are

## Floods
Floods are the most frequent climate hazards in Mali making up 50% of all climate hazard occurrences from 1980 to 2020. The flood season is generally from July to October, but can last longer and more severely in several locations in the inner delta of the Niger River, generally from August to October or early November. It is estimated that 500,000 people are currently affected by floods each year in Mali.

Figure B.15: Annual hazard occurrence in Mali (1980-2020)

| | |
|---|---|
| | |

## Heat stress
Average annual number of days with heat index > 35°C between 1960 and 2020 has significantly increased in Mali (Figure B.16 left). Similarly, the average annual number of tropical nights (T-min > 20°C) has also increased over this period (Figure B.16 right). The increase in heat stress is expected to continue and gradually increasing in Mali. This is gradually changing the suitability of crop and livestock production across Mali with increasing heat stresses.

## Climate context – projected trends of temperature, precipitation, and corresponding hazards

Climate change is expected to be a disruptive influence on development in many countries. This is in part due to data availability constraints including a lack of accurate domestic data sources, gaps in the historical record, and limited ability to project future changes with confidence. There are also uncertainties in temperature and precipitation, as well as a limited understanding of the impact of aerosols on projected climate change.

### Representative Concentration Pathways (RCPs) RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5

RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 are predominantly used to analyze climate trends in the period up until 2100, with a focus on the period from 2030 onwards. The projections for these pathways are subject to increasing variability and challenges, for example the 2080 projection.

### Temperature Projections

### Precipitation Projections

Alongside temperature, extreme heat is also projected to rise with the number of very hot days with a daily maximum temperature above 35°C expected increases across West Africa. The projected increase is most significant in the south of the country (namely Sikasso, Bamako, and parts of Kayes and Koulikoro) where >50 additional very hot days are projected by 2100.

Precipitation projections are inconclusive due to its variability across different regions and time frames. This is primarily due to the high uncertainty and year-to-year variability, which is demonstrated by the range in values for each region and pathway shown in table B.1.2. In the period up until 2030, precipitation is projected to increase in all regions of the MAT project with the exception of Sikasso.

## Droughts: Drought projections rely on precipitation projections, as well as projections of temperature evapotranspiration, and soil moisture. The inability to accurately project precipitation means that drought projections are also uncertain.

The projected increase in average annual temperatures across Mali is expected to lead to a reduction in the amount of water available for crops. This will be exacerbated by an anticipated change in the natural crop land area exposed to at least one drought per year in response to global warming, offers project up to 3 times more severe and longer duration droughts than those experienced during the baseline period (1981-2010). The distribution in Figure B.17 presents expected values of annual SPEI (SPEI-1) from 2006 to 2100. Panel (a) shows the distribution of SPEI-1 for Mali and its regions from 2006 to 1–01 in 2100**. This information shows increased severe drought vulnerability across the regions in Mali.

34. **Floods:** High temperatures make soil dry and reduce soil’s absorption rate, which increases the likelihood of floods, especially in the face of extreme rainfall events as it is Mali's wet season. With temperatures projected to rise, this will likely increase flooding frequency and intensity. In addition, with climate change leading to more frequent extreme events, the conditions suggest that floods will either stay the same or get more frequent. This is corroborated by the fact that in 2018, there were 35 flood events in Mali, up from just four in 2017.

35. **Pest and diseases:** Increasing pest and disease infection in crops and livestock has been observed in recent years across Mali with increasing temperature and changes in rainfall patterns (i). An assessment of the expected impact of climate change on crop production in Mali shows that under a high-emission scenario, the maize and wheat livestock types in Mali show the increasing risk of infestation or outbreak levels for many pests and diseases (ii).

36. **The impacts of climate change:** The impacts of climate change are likely to be felt across all sectors, including food security, water resources, human health, and economic development. These impacts will vary by sector, location, and population group. Factors such as the total requirements of each pest or disease, the endemic zone, the relative frequency of outbreaks within endemic zones, and the ability of farmers to adapt are among those that will influence the extent of impact. The factors can also be influenced through vector, and pathogen transmission, crop/pest management practices, and land use.

### Climate change impacts

37. **Impact on agriculture and resulting food insecurity:** Agriculture in Mali is largely reliant on rainfall and suitable temperature for the crops and livestock production which are sensitive to changes in precipitation patterns, temperatures, and the corresponding climate hazards. This presents a major challenge for Mali as agriculture accounts for 60% of GDP (iii) and >50% of employment (iv). The majority of farmers in Mali practice subsistence farming with mixed crop-livestock agropastoralism, with 86% of farmers being smallholders who work on small parcels of land (generally less than 1.2 ha) and produce a wide variety of crops for their own consumption as well as for sale to local markets (e.g., rice and rice) and livestock (predominantly cattle, goats, goats and sheep). representing ~45% and >30% of Mali’s agricultural output respectively (v). The majority of the population in Mali is rural (~72%) with a total population of 19.6 million people in Mali. Due to the significance of agriculture in Mali for employment and food consumption, the impacts of climate change on agriculture will have significant implications for food security.

38. **Precipitation impacts:** Dependence on rainfall has been a major constraint on crop and livestock production in Mali (vi). The average annual precipitation across Mali is 500 mm with a range from <200 mm to >1,600 mm. This has affected the flood potential of 4.2 million hectares of wetlands in the country and generated a negative impact on soil and water quality. In addition, the increased frequency and intensity of rainfall events have led to an increase in crop yields. The reduced period of heavy precipitation has resulted in a shortened growing period in Mali and correspondingly lower yields for crops such as rice and millet (vii). This has had a significant negative impact on livestock production by both reducing direct access to water for live stock consumption, and due to the decreased availability of pasture land. In addition, the increased frequency of rainfall events has led to soil erosion in Mali. The majority of smallholder farmers have been forced to remain near permanent water sources, disrupting their habitual migration patterns. This has had a negative impact on livestock production as well as increasing the risk of desertification and degradation.

39. **The impacts of water scarcity:** The impacts of water scarcity on livelihoods have been felt acutely in the North of Mali. For example, the population of 100,000 people living in the commune of Kidal (250 km from Timbuktu) and 40,000 people living in the commune of Gao (370 km from Timbuktu), which was the main source of livelihood for the communities. The depletion of Lake Faguibine has had a significant negative impact on the local economy as it is the only water source for livestock production, leading to disputes between farmers and livestock herders.

40. **Temperature impacts:** Temperature increases in Mali are projected to lead to an increase in temperature in Mali which is associated with an increased evaporation rate, leading to increased water requirements for crop and livestock production (viii). This has had a significant negative impact on the livelihoods of smallholder farmers attributed to the increased droughts in the 2016-2020 period. In 2021, drought-induced about a 10.5% decrease

## In cereal production in Mali, threatening the livelihoods of over three million people.

### Livestock production, rising temperatures cause heat stress which affects their feed intake, milk production, and reproductive efficiency and subsequently reduces meat and milk production. This has a negative impact livestock production through decreased fodder availability which is predicted to decline by 5%-10% in Mali due to the increasing frequency of droughts.

### Tensions in Mali, due to their predominance in farms and nations representing 68% of all daily caloric intake and 70% of food security. The impact of climate change on livestock production is expected to be more severe than that for crops because declining resource availability could generate an overall loss of welfare ranging from USD 2 to 142 million.

### Flood Impacts: Floods and excessive rainfall have created waterlogging and soil erosion in many places of Mali, leading to the destruction of agricultural infrastructure. These events have a direct impact on access to land for agriculture, crop damages, and reduce household ability to ensure food security. In addition to agricultural damage, the various impacts of floods include the destruction of dwellings, loss of assets, displacement of people, and increased vulnerability to diseases.

40. It is also forecast that land degradation will accelerate due to the forecast climate changes resulting in an increased negative impact on agricultural production. Figure B.1-8 models the impact of changing climate conditions and land degradation on crop yields for maize, wheat, rice, and sorghum in Mali except pearl millet. The extent of land degradation could lead to the incapacity to grow maize, beans, and other crops.
41. Resulting impact on food security: To date, the cycle of droughts, floods, and hotter temperatures, as well as the prolonged dry season, have led to a decrease in crop yields, reducing the quantity of food available for consumption and reducing the nutritional value of some crops. Droughts have contributed to severe food crises in Mali and the wider region. The 2019/2020 agricultural season was characterized by poor rainfall distribution, which reduced the production of crops and livestock led to reduced food availability and increased food prices in Mali. For example, the price of wheat flour increased from 350 CFA francs/kg (CFAF) in September 2019 to 470 CFAF/kg in March 2020, an average of 79% for maize for sorghum and 85% for millet compared to the same period in 2019. This is particularly worrying because the food security situation in Mali has been deteriorating since 2016. In 2020, the household budget reduced over 55%, and this figure did not take into account the food produced by a household's own production. The number of people living below the poverty line increased from 37% to 48% in children. The share of the population that is undernourished rose just over 21% in 2017 to almost 10% in 2020. In addition, the number of food insecure people has increased by more than 5 million from January 2019 to August 2022, reaching 1.2 million children September-2021 to August 2022. Food insecurity further increased in 2023. The number of food-insecure people is expected to increase from 48% in July 2022 to 56% in June 2023, projected to 24% at August 2023, up from 15% in early 2023.

### 43. In agriculture, crop production contributes 75% of total agricultural emissions and livestock production contributes 70%. Cultivation on degraded soils is a major GHG contributor, with savannah burning attributed to 21% of agricultural emissions. Within the livestock sector, enteric fermentation is a major emissions source (39%) of agricultural emissions. The conversion of forest areas into agriculture or pasture land for livestock grazing and food production needs are speeding the degradation of soils, natural resources, and increasing emissions trends. The GHG emissions from rice cultivation are also significant, with 20% of total agricultural emissions attributed to this activity.

| Crop synthetic fertilizers | 8% |
| Manure in soil | 36% |
| Manure management | 2% |
| Manure left in pastures | 19% |
| Enteric fermentation | 30% |

**Limitations in adaptive capacity to adjust to climate change**

- Smallholder farmers have limited capacity to adapt to the current and projected climate change impacts and risks because of their low level of education, lack of access to information, and limited financial resources.
- The adoption of new technologies and practices in Mali, implementation is limited at the household and community levels with four key themes: availability and accessibility of seeds; institutional capacity; availability and accessibility of CSA techniques; information availability.

**Adaptation strategies**

- Smallholder farmers have been able to adapt to climate change through a range of adaptation strategies. Some successful techniques include the use of improved and adaptive climate-seeds and breeds; and soil water conservation practices such as contour plowing, terracing, and mulching.
- However, they have been employed to avoid effect for extreme weather events such as droughts and floods. The 2015 flood in Mali has highlighted the lack of water and management as one the main constraints for the implementation of agricultural adaptation strategies.

**Constraints**

- Limited access to information on climate change and its impacts.
- Smallholder farmers (partially attributed to farmer illiteracy) and limited access to the financial inputs required to implement adaptation measures.
- Access to the market for climate-smart technologies and other inputs (e.g. climate resilient seeds/breeds, planting materials, tools, etc.) is a major challenge for smallholder farmers in Mali.

**Access to information**

- Limited information on early warning systems exists in Mali; but information is not consistently communicated to the farmers in good time, which undermines individuals' ability to deploy resources to act quickly and effectively. The same applies to climate-smart agricultural techniques. There are limited details available on the costs and benefits of different high-tech and low-tech options and how best to combine them in the context of Mali's

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | p.19

## Access to finance for smallholder farmers continues to be low in Mali and across West Africa.

The use of agroecological practices is limited by the lack of access to credit, which is predominantly due to the perception of high risk. Financial resources are even less accessible to women in large part due to their lower social status and weaker bargaining power within households. As a result, adaptive capacity is further reduced for female-headed households. In addition, market infrastructure challenges such as poor road networks and lack of access to markets also limit the adoption of agroecological techniques more attractive.

### Focus areas of IAT implementation

1. IAAT aims to target the most climate-vulnerable communities within Mali and its subsequently build resilience by providing them with the necessary resources to help 83,400 farming households and 172,650 farmers to adapt to climate change and support low-carbon agriculture development pathways.
Guided by the IPCC vulnerability framework, the project will use a multi-criteria decision analysis (MCDA) approach to determine vulnerability at the circle level for the five target regions of the project (Gao, Koulikoro, Mopti, Segou, and Tombouctou). The MCDA process is described in detail in Annex 1. See also Section VIII: Vulnerability and Targeting Assessment.
This analysis resulted in the selection of 12 circles across five regions of Mali as the target areas for the project (see Figure B.1.4). IAAT will select 45 communes within the 12 circles based on stakeholders' consultation in the first six months of the project.

### Related projects/Interventions

52. The high vulnerability to climate change and threatened livelihood in Mali have driven development partners to partner with the government of Mali (GoM) to implement a number of interventions aimed at enhancing adaptation and mitigation capacities, including the implementation of Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA). These interventions address the root causes leading to exacerbated exposure and vulnerability to climate change, including limited access to resources, poor infrastructure, lack of information on climate variability and trends, low awareness about climate risks, and limited adoption of CSA. The interventions aim at increasing farmers' resilience by raising awareness and adoption of Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) and agroforestry practices and technologies.

53. Notable projects include the Mali Climate Change Adaptation Program (Phase II), the Regional Sahel Pastoralism Support Project (RSPPS II) and the Africa Hydroclim Program (Phase 2) and the Regional Sahel Pastoralism Support Project (RSPPS II) and the Africa Hydroclim Program (Phase 2). These projects aim at increasing farmers' resilience by raising awareness and adoption of Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) and agroforestry practices and technologies. The Mali Climate Change Adaptation Program (Phase II), for example, aims to increase farmers' resilience by strengthening national extension services and developing and vocational education and training programs for smallholder farmers. Additional projects include the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) project on climate change adaptation in agriculture and agroforestry, and the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) project on climate change adaptation in agriculture and agroforestry.

54. The Mali Climate Change Adaptation Program (Phase II), for example, aims to increase farmers' resilience by strengthening national extension services and developing and vocational education and training programs for smallholder farmers. Additional projects include the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) project on climate change adaptation in agriculture and agroforestry.

### 54. The GCF IAAT project seeks to bring a meaningful contribution to the ecosystem already created in Mali, by working closely with existing projects and initiatives of the Government of Mali (GoM), National Civil Society Organizations (NGOs) and Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) to harness the synergies identified across the respective projects. Considering that the GCF IAAT project is focused on addressing climate change adaptation needs and resilience in the regions targeted by the project and capitalize on existing synergies to create economies of scale and leverage resources, it is important for the project to work closely with other initiatives and projects. The development partners, the adaptation and mitigation gap for smallholder farmers remains significant and requires additional support from the international community. This is especially true in the context of climate change vulnerability and displacement of populations in Mali. The GCF IAAT project aims to create value by adapting an end-to-end approach to the agricultural sector in Mali, which includes a focus on the following: (i) strengthening the capacity of public services, private sector, government, etc., A subset of 22 agriculture and agroforestry practices has been prioritized. In addition, the project will also support the development of an enabling environment for CSA and agroforestry technologies. This will be achieved through a number of activities.

### 55. IAAT will bring the Children’s global experience in Locally Led Adaptation (LLA) to address climate change challenges at the local level. The LLA approach is a participatory, community-based adaptation strategy that empowers communities and builds their capacity for resilience by strengthening local ownership to advance climate action. The Children used the LLA approach in the resilience food security project implemented in 2014-2015 in four countries: Ethiopia (Oromia), Kenya (Nakuru), Tanzania (Mtwara), and Uganda (Wakiso). Mali (Albarka), Nepal (Saptari), Tanzania (Lise-Ehinde), and Ethiopia (Transformation to Food Security) are the five countries where the Children will implement the LLA approach. The project will also focus on remote villages with presence in remote and last mile communities in Mali to advocate and raise awareness on children’s health and nutrition, water and sanitation, food security, and climate change adaptation. This will be achieved by addressing food and water insecurity.

### 56. The GCF IAAT project aims to collaborate with the Albarka project and build upon its results and experience while delivering a cross-cutting project aimed at low-carbon and climate resilient pathways. The Albarka project is a multi-country initiative that seeks to improve food security and resilience of communities in conflict-affected areas in Mali through strengthening local ownership, capacity building, and the development of community-based adaptation strategies for smallholder farmers. The project aims to improve the resilience of communities, especially families, women, and youth, in three of the five regions targeted by GCF IAAT. The Albarka project will also support the development of a network of Community Adaptation Strategy organizations for community development and enhanced adaptation and resilience capacities, including Water Users Associations (WUA) and Village Savings and Loans Associations (VSLA). The project will establish Climate Change Action Committees (CCACs) to foster ownership of decision-making processes by communities, and Early Warning Groups (EWGs) in the five regions targeted by GCF IAAT. The Albarka project will also support the development of a youth network for climate change adaptation and resilience.

Albarka also aims to enhance the business capacities of women and youth and create sustainable markets for products and services that are environmentally friendly, while ensuring that they are integrated into local communities and regions and emphasize the integration of climate change mitigation and adaptation into existing economic activities. The project will support the development of a Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) in Economic, Social and Cultural Development Plans (PESC) at the commune level, strengthening the capacity of VSLA and WUA to manage their resources for climate change adaptation and resilience, and supporting entrepreneurs involved in CSA and agroforestry, and creating linkages between multiple stakeholders involved in


---
**Page 28**
---

Alignment with national priorities

The project is consistent with Mali’s climate policies and frameworks specifically targeted toward climate-resilient agriculture and adaptation to climate change. These include National Adaptation Program of Action (NAPA-2017), National Climate Change Policy (NCCP-2015), National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS-2017), Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement, and Mali’s 2021 NDC. The project aligns with the national climate adaptation measures in response to the need to cope with adverse and irregular climatic conditions. The project also supports the implementation of the National Strategy for Climate Change Adaptation (NSCCA-2017) which commits to a 25% emissions reduction target for agriculture and stresses the value in conserving soils and nutrient management, agroecological practices addressing climate change. Mali’s 2021 NDC emphasized creating an enabling environment for sustainable natural resource management focusing on the following key actions for Climate Change adaptation and mitigation:

- Technical assistance to support the development of national and local level strategies at all levels (national and local levels in a way that can support local and sector planning, including identifying practical solutions).
- Technology and knowledge transfer to support monitoring, forecasting, analysis, early warning, and preparedness for natural hazards in agriculture and natural resource management sectors.
- Investment in the development, financing, and management of adaptation and mitigation measures within broader sectoral objectives and plans.

### 63. Barriers to Accessing Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) Practices

The adoption of CSA practices is hindered by a number of factors, including limited financial resources, climate change adaptation (seeds, fertilizer, labour, equipment) due limited financial resources, CSA and bio-essential services, such as extension services, are not available to smallholder farmers. In addition, the cost of inputs related to their total income and resources, examples resources includes seeds for new climate-resilient varieties and breeding programs, fertilizers, irrigation systems, and other equipment. Smallholder farmers also face barriers in accessing irrigation and biodigesters systems. This provides a barrier to smallholder farmers in Mali as they typically have limited access to these inputs and services.

Barrier 6: Market, technology, and cultural barriers to the adoption of low-carbon agriculture technologies such as agroforestry, soil irrigation, and biodigesters. High-potential low-carbon technologies that provide co-benefits for climate change mitigation are often not adopted by farmers because they require irrigation and biogas systems or practices such as agroforestry (see Annex 2: Feasibility Study, Section VIII). Sub-optimal market conditions, lack of access to finance, and limited availability of inputs and services experienced for adaptation practices, low-carbon pathways face additional market and technology barriers. In the case of biodigesters, a key barrier is the high upfront cost of installation. According to the African Biodigester Central Africa (ABi- WCA) estimating that by 2019 there were only 1,600 biodigesters in the country, and the market penetration rate was less than 5%. The ABi-WCA also notes that the high upfront cost of installation is a barrier. In addition to the high upfront capital costs, the lack of access to finance for biodigester technology in Central Africa provides a significant barrier; a high upfront investment cost makes the technologies inaccessible to most potential users. This is because the majority of smallholder farmers are unable to afford the high upfront capital cost and provide limited market demand for biodigesters. The low adoption rate of biodigesters can be attributed to the lack of access to finance, as well as the lack of awareness among smallholder farmers combined with limited installation, operations or maintenance services accessible by smallholder farmers. In addition, there is a lack of awareness about the benefits and co-benefits that are provided by using biodigester technology. The high upfront capital cost of biodigesters can be offset to some extent by using limited market demand for biodigesters. This is because the majority of smallholder farmers are unable to afford the high upfront capital cost and provide limited market demand for biodigesters. The low adoption rate of biodigesters can be attributed to the lack of access to finance, as well as the lack of awareness among smallholder farmers combined with limited installation, operations or maintenance services accessible by smallholder farmers. In addition, there is a lack of awareness about the benefits and co-benefits that are provided by using biodigester technology.

For biodigesters, legacy donor projects have also served to create an expectation for free biodigester technology. This has led to low adoption rates as many farmers are focusing solely on the energy production and excluding the other valuable outputs of the system such as fertilizer recovery and animal feed. For agroforestry, additional barriers are also present that limit its adaption. As agroforestry often requires multiple years for establishment before it becomes productive, it is difficult to attract investment from private sector actors who have a 10-year or more reach to maturity but then deliver stable output for a further 10+ years; certainty on land ownership is key to enabling the long-term investments required. Agroforestry also faces barriers related to its adoption by smallholders, which are often influenced by cultural norms that associate the act of tree planting with land ownership. However, this is less common when trees are planted as part of a larger agroforestry system and can be seen as an additional benefit rather than a threat to land tenure.

Barrier 7: Institutional barriers due to insufficient capacity, resources and sometimes trust within public institutions that have the potential to support low-carbon agriculture technologies and practices or adaptation practices. The presence of the centralized government-level governance structures and administration (e.g., national agricultural research systems) can be an obstacle for the adoption of new technologies as capacity building intervention and has been associated with the adoption of specific new adaptive techniques such as water harvesting, agroforestry, and soil conservation. However, this is not always the case; in some cases, communities from effectively leveraging the PSCs to foster improved adaptation and mitigation practices. This is important because it can be difficult for governments to provide adequate support to farmers when they are unable to engage with communities on a regular basis. The lack of trust between government officials and farmers can also be a barrier to implementing CSA and agroforestry supportive policies that are approved at the national level. Policies related to land tenure, water rights, and other aspects of agricultural production can be difficult for governments to implement effectively because they often require significant changes in policy or institutional arrangements.

Incorporating demand for resources also limits the ability of public sector institutions to support CSA and agroforestry practices. The lack of funding from national governments is a major barrier to implementing CSA and agroforestry practices, as well as other adaptation strategies. This can be seen particularly with regard to the National Response Plan (NRP), which is the main state response to support households in the areas most affected by climate change. The NRP aims to provide financial assistance for households that are facing demand for resources at both the national and local levels, with communities also citing a lack of budget to implement CSA practices as one of their major barriers. In addition, there can be significant delays between the time when new policies or institutional arrangements are approved by central government institutions and the time when they become operational in practice.

Demanding competing for resources also limits the ability of public sector institutions to support CSA and agroforestry practices. ### Outline of Project Components to Address Barriers

1. **Outcome 1:** Increase climate-resilient agricultural crop and food production in the targeted regions in Mali.
   - To achieve outcome 1, if the project:
     * Improves the technical capacities and utilization of extension services at the local level (Output 2.1) and supports smallholder farmers to adopt climate-resilient practices in their farming systems (Output 3.1).
     * Increases access to markets for smallholder farmers through improved market linkages and value chains (Outputs 4.1, 5.1, and 6.1).

2. **Outcome 2:** Improve and inclusive access to finance and markets for vulnerable communities to enhance sustainable livelihoods.
   - To achieve outcome 2, if the project:
     * Supports the creation of more connected value chains in Mali (Output 4.1) and improves market linkages and value chains at the local level (Outputs 5.1 and 6.1).
     * Supports smallholder farmers to adopt climate-resilient practices in their farming systems (Output 3.1).

### 74. Outcome 3: Adaptation and mitigation considerations and best practices are embedded in institutional arrangements and governance mechanisms.
   - To achieve outcome 3, if the project:
     * Increases institutional capacity in climate change adaptation and mitigation planning as well as knowledge of best practices to address agriculture and livelihoods (Outputs 2.1 and 3.1).
     * Improves access to advisory services on climate-resilient practices in CA and agroforestry systems (Output 6.1).

   - **Barriers:** 3.1) The integration of adaptation and mitigation best practices into institutional arrangements, second and local community governance mechanisms (i.e., the adoption of adaptation and mitigation practices). [Outputs 3.1 and 6.1] 3.2) The lack of knowledge and capacity base will be removed for institutional stakeholders. This will directly address barrier 7. **[Limited capacity of public and private extension services at the local level to provide access to inclusive and reliable advisory agro-advisory services on climate-resilient practices]** and barrier 1.

## Outcome 4: Reduced GHG emissions from the agricultural system

To achieve outcome 4 of the project (i) increased adoption of low-carbon agriculture technologies and practices (Output 2.3.1) and (ii) increases the land area under agroforestry within the project's target regions (Outputs 1.3 and 4.2). The project will support the direct adoption of these technologies and practices by smallholder farmers through a combination of approaches, including: (i) providing training to farmers on low emission technologies and practices will be deployed in replacement of higher emissions technologies; and (ii) supporting the development of value chains for agroforestry products such as timber, fruits, nuts, and carbon "seeds" will be created in the form of 31,250 Ha of agroforestry. The project will both support the direct adoption of these technologies and practices by smallholder farmers through a combination of approaches including supporting value chains and services. IAAT will collaborate with the private sector solar and biogas technology suppliers to provide training on the installation and maintenance of these technologies, as well as financing options through the PAVCO approach. Some of these companies have already started exploring this financial model in Malawi. The project will also support the development of a market for agroforestry products such as timber and systems in the IAAT location. Additionally, the installation of solar irrigation systems will contribute to enhancing productivity and profitability of smallholder farmers through increased water availability and reduced labor costs, enabling long distances from remote rural areas to purchase fuel, coupled with the support to effectively harness the potential of agroforestry products such as timber, fruits, nuts, and carbon "seeds" for sale.

Similarly, agrotechnology with shared smallholder farmers as part of the CSA techniques is component 1, will be the focus of this project. The project will provide training on CSA techniques to local extension services such as nurseries and transportation of saplings as well as increased capacities amongst regional governments to conduct trainings on these techniques. This will increase the adoption rate of low-carbon agriculture technologies such as "Market", technology and cultural barriers for the adoption of low-carbon agriculture technologies/ practices such as crop rotation, intercropping, agroforestry, and conservation agriculture. The project will address barrier 1 in addressing barrier 1: "technical know-how and capacity gaps amongst smallholder farmers to implement and integrate climate smart agricultural practices" through component 2.

When combined, the 4 project outcomes can create a systems-level change through re-enforcing outputs on the farm, market, and institutional level:

- On-farm: Increased resilience, low-carbon practices by smallholder farmers (components 1 and 2)
- Increased market capacity is key input for the adoption of CSA and low-carbon farming, and access to demand will increase the profitability of these technologies (component 3)
- Increased institutional capacity and interest in climate resilient and low emissions practices and therefore increased willingness to engage in national dialogues and policies, enabling increased interest at the market and on-farm levels (components 3 and 4)

## In order to achieve the paradigm shift through the above project components

The following assumptions are included:

Assumptions included in the Theory of Change:

- Vulnerable smallholder farmers and their families are willing to participate in the project and to alter food production and marketing practices as a result.
- Other stakeholders (e.g., local government, USAID Afrika), with which there are synergies and interdependencies, deliver according to their work plans.
- Local communities are supportive of the project's activities.
- All levels of government in Malawi remain supportive of project initiatives and enable working relationships with public institutions to ensure that projects are implemented effectively.
- Local or national conflict does not inhibit safe access of project implementation partners to target project regions during the project implementation period.

B.3 Project programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 5 pages)

Project Components and Activities

78. To implement inclusive consultations with regional and communal councils, community beneficiaries (i.e. farming cooperatives and unions, community leaders, youth and women, women’s committees, local government officials), and other relevant stakeholders on the proposed interventions (climate-smart agriculture, agroforestry, solar pump irrigation, biodigester included in the activities of Component 1) to identify the most appropriate and feasible climate-smart agricultural interventions for smallholder farmers, community leaders, and local farmers (also include smallholder, marginal, and vulnerable populations). IAT will conduct further consultations with these stakeholders during the second phase of the project to integrate the interests and needs of the Indigenous People (if identified during the implementation of Component 1) into the proposed interventions. The project will also ensure that vulnerable people during the selection of 48 communes that will ensure application of free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) procedures.

Component I: Improving Extension Services and Increasing On-Farm CSA Adoption

79. Component I will focus on i) increasing the awareness of climate risks and ii) the understanding and technical capacity to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices among smallholder farmers through advisory services, the key educational service for smallholder farmers, and secondly by supporting the on-farm adoption of CSA techniques. The project will also support the development of a curriculum for extension officers that includes lessons in climate-smart agriculture and agroforestry production* and output 1.2 *Increased use of climate resilient practices in the project area.

Output I.1 Improved technical capacities and inclusivity of extension services in climate-smart agriculture and agroforestry production

80. Activities under Output 1.1 will be delivered through activity 1.1.1: “Build technical capacity and reach of extension services on CSA and agroforestry techniques” which will directly address barrier 1.1.1: Limited development of public and private extension services in climate-smart agriculture and agroforestry production, and barrier 1.1.2: Lack of awareness among smallholder farmers about the importance of climate risk practices*. This activity seeks to expand the reach of extension services and to improve the technical capacity of extension officers to provide advisory services on CSA techniques. The project will also develop a tailored curriculum for extension services that address climate risks and deliver practical advice on how to adopt CSA techniques in their work with smallholder farmers. The activity will strengthen public and private extension services. It will also involve local organizations and leverage digital platforms to increase the reach of extension services. The activity will be implemented through two sub-activities: 1) Strengthening of extension services in climate-smart agriculture and agroforestry among smallholder farmers. Beneficiaries of activity 1.1.1 will be extension officers, local organizations, and public and private extension agencies across Goba, Koulikoro, Tombouctou, Segou and Mopti.

80. Activity 1.1.1 will be delivered through two sub-activities:

a) Strengthening of extension services in climate-smart agriculture and agroforestry among smallholder farmers and families through rural and family farming project regions (Goba, Mopti, Koulikoro, Segou and Tombouctou) and tailor to each level via workshops.

b) Strengthening of extension services in climate-smart agriculture and agroforestry among smallholder farmers and families through the IAT project regions (Ségou, Mali), Koulikoro, Tombouctou and Mopti) and tailor to each level via workshops.

| Priority CGI Technique | Focus Area | Description of the technique |
|---|---|---|
| Compost burning | Crop Farming | A form of micro-catchment technology, a simple and cheap form of water control that involves burning compost in a pit or trench. The heat generated from this process is used to kill weed seeds and other pests. |
| Culturing straw technique | Crop Farming | An innovative method for straw management to enhance agro-climate conditions. |
| Application of manure and/or of farm compost | Crop Farming | A form of micro-catchment technology, a simple and cheap form of water control that involves burning compost in a pit or trench. The heat generated from this process is used to kill weed seeds and other pests. |
| Management of flood and river erosion | Crop Farming | Management of flood and dewatered areas. |
| Development of inland valley rice culture | Crop Farming | A form of simple, low-cost water management structures in the rice fields (e.g., check dams, small weirs, etc.) that are used to control flooding and reduce soil erosion. |
| Half-moon technique | Crop Farming | A water harvesting intervention that consists of half moon shaped bags dug in the field. These bags are filled with water from rainwater or irrigation. |
| System of rice intensification (SRI) | Crop Farming | Rice cultivation practice for the early establishment of healthy plants, low soil fertility and high rainfall conditions. |
| Zai technique | Crop Farming | Small basins as which seeds or small parts of perennial crops are planted. They are used to improve water infiltration in dry areas by creating a micro-hydrological environment with a higher water infiltration when it rains. |
| Crop diversification | Crop Farming | A form of simple, low-cost water management structures in the rice fields (e.g., check dams, small weirs, etc.) that are used to control flooding and reduce soil erosion. |
| Integrated pest and disease management | Crop Farming | Focus on long-term prevention of pests and diseases through a combination of cultural practices, biological controls, resistant varieties and judicious use of chemical pesticides. |
| Feeding of birds by animal compost | Livestock farming | Providing livestock in fields to eat soil that was fertilized the field with manure. |
| fodder crops | Livestock farming | Planting leguminous plants such as cowpeas or soybeans, which are rich in protein and trace minerals. |
| Feed supplements for animals | Livestock farming | Feed supplements are agrochemicals, calcium and trace minerals that can be added to animal feed to improve the nutritional value of the diet. |
| Dual purpose crops (Food and fodder) | Crop Farming | Crops grown as food source for livestock and people. These crops can also be used in human diets. |
| Assisted natural regeneration of forests | Agroforestry | Increasing the number of trees by protecting and pruning living tree stumps, and by planting new seedlings or saplings. |
| Hedgerows | Agroforestry | Planting of wind and stress screens, typically bordering a road or field boundary to provide shelter for livestock and crops from wind and other environmental stresses. |
| Tree nursery and transporting | Agroforestry | Establishment of tree nurseries in the vicinity of the project area where seedlings are grown and maintained until they reach an appropriate maturity, trees are then transported to the destination location. |
| Training Services | Service Delivery | Training services for farmers on the use of improved agricultural practices as well as public officers to anticipate and prepare for climate risks. |
| Crop insurance | Service Delivery | Insurance schemes that provide financial compensation in case of crop failure due to adverse weather conditions, pests or diseases. |
| Solar irrigation systems (Pumps & Irrigation) | Water Resources & Environment | A system that uses electricity generated by solar panels to pump water to extract groundwater or surface water, water connects to an irrigation system for use on crops and livestock. |
| Biogas systems | Mostly livelihoods & environment | A system that uses anaerobic digestion to convert organic matter into biogas which can be used as a source of energy. The process also results in the creation of liquid fertilizer, which is rich in nutrients such as nitrogen, phosphorus, and potassium. This fertilizer can be used on crops or sold for income generation.

Through the extension agents trained as part of 1.1.1, the finalized and tailored learning program will be disseminated across the twelve circles, focusing on the 48 project targeted communes, by using a variety of dissemination methods including training of trainers, field visits, workshops, and mentoring of cooperative leaders in the new curriculum who will then train their members.

### 2.1.2 Sub-activity: Strengthening Early Warning Group (EWG) Capacity

#### 2.1.2.1 Sub-activity: Building EWG capacity to support the implementation and dissemination of the early warning system (EWS)

Existing and new Early Warning Groups (Gap-RUs) will be supported to improve their understanding of and capacity for implementing the EWS, including the use of mobile phones and other communication tools.

Informed farmers and communities in general. Clear guidelines regarding the procedures and measures to be taken is required to ensure that the early warning system can be effectively implemented at the community level by high-risk vulnerable groups (e.g., women, youth, and older people). The contingency plan will aim to enhance the readiness and resilience of Gap-RUs in the face of challenges such as climate change. This will include training on how to use mobile phones and other communication tools.

The procedures will include protocols for communication, coordination, and response mechanisms, as well as the education and awareness raising activities that will be carried out at the community level. The EWG members will be trained in their respective roles and responsibilities and will receive kits to enhance the timeliness and effectiveness of early warning dissemination.

#### 2.1.2.2 Sub-activity: Strengthening Community Action Cycles (CACs) established by Albarika to ensure their sustainability beyond the project period

Albarika has established 30 CACs in Gao, Mopti, and Koulikoro, with 1 CAC in each village targeted through the project. IAWT will select 250 villages based on the commune’s priorities within 48 communes to establish CACs.

The CACs are established as a community-based approach to empower communities and support them to autonomously identify, plan, implement, and evaluate their own projects. The CACs are also used for conflict resolution and environmental management. This approach facilitates community empowerment by fostering their active involvement in decision-making, problem-solving, and resource allocation.

Albarika has effectively used the CACs approach to minimize and eliminate conflicts within communities and between communities and local authorities. The CACs have been used as a model to transfer and apply to the IAWT. See Annex 23.

CACs will be strengthened by building their management capacities and ensure sustainability beyond the implementation of Albarika. This will include providing management kits to each CAC and supporting them to develop action plans for their communities, including land use planning and climate change adaptation. Implementing farmers’ training and capacity building by the extension staff and agents, farmers field schools, and other stakeholders.

### 2.1.2.3 Sub-activity: Extending the Albarika CAC model in other communes in Gao, Mopti, and Tombouctou, and strengthening their management capacities

GOF IAWT will replace the same model of CACs launched by Albarika (Sub-activity 1.2.1) in all other communes within the project area where CACs have not been established yet.

This sub-activity will include establishing a CAC in each village and strengthening their management capacities by providing management kits, training on land use planning, and capacity building for farmers’ field schools and other stakeholders.

### Output 1.3: Increased land area under agroforestry systems

#### Activity 1.3.1: Promote Agroforestry Systems to Develop land-use mapping at the regional level?

and activity 1.3.2: Plant agroforestry trees on community and state-owned lands.

These activities will collectively contribute towards increasing the land area under agroforestry systems in the project area, with a focus on promoting practices such as agrosilviculture, solar irrigation, and biodigesters along cultural borders. Activity 3.2 will also promote agroforestry trees on community and state-owned lands.

The activities will be implemented through participatory approaches that include adaptation and mitigation considerations and best practices in their work, particularly with regards to planning for the tenure of unenclosed areas and levels with other stakeholder groups.

### Output 90: Develop a land-use database and mapping at the regional level?

Activity 1.3.1 will contribute towards developing a land-use database and mapping to support the development of agroforestry and inform the implementation of locally relevant CSA and agroforestry practices. The database will be developed in collaboration with local stakeholders, including farmers’ field schools, to enhance their understanding of land resource distribution, enabling improved land-use planning and decision-making at the commune level.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 [Page 35]

## Activity 1.3.1 will be delivered through two sub-activities:

### 1.3.1.2 Sub-activity: Establish a project land use database.
A project land use database will be established to collect specific data on the agricultural land in the targeted region, including land use categories, soil characteristics, and other environmental features. This information will help identify changes over time, environmental features, soil characteristics, etc., which can provide valuable information for the implementation of agroforestry systems.

### 1.3.2 Activity: Seeks to promote enhanced adoption of agroforestry by supporting the on-farm planting of 91,285 ha of agroforestry, targeting specific value chains including acacia Senegal and shea.
The capacities of several stakeholders will be strengthened in order to ensure that the right seeds and saplings are available for planting. This includes training farmers on seed production and distribution of inputs, regional and common local authorities for the identification of suitable land, farmers for planning and implementation of agroforestry systems, and extension agents for providing technical support.

### 1.3.2 Activity will be delivered through three sub-activities:

#### 1.3.2.1 Sub-activity: Support the production and distribution of agroforestry tree saplings (a).
The project will establish a seed bank in each region to collect seeds from local trees, shrubs, and other plants that are best suited for each area targeted by the project. This information will be leveraged to identify and produce the seeds and tree saplings that are best suited to each area targeted by the project. The project will also support the production of seedlings in nurseries located on private land dedicated to agroforestry in five regions. Agroforestry activity in IATV will only include (a) in private plots, (b) public land - plantation of fodder and other useful trees in degraded lands for reforestation and soil and land management, (c) public land - plantation of fodder, fruit and other useful trees in degraded lands for reforestation and soil and land management, (d) public land - planting of fodder and other useful trees in degraded lands for reforestation and soil and land management. The project will also support the production of seedlings to enhance existing agroforestry systems on private lands that will cover 21,585 ha.

#### 1.3.2.2 Sub-activity: Support the distribution of tree saplings to farmers in targeted regions.
The project will establish a seed bank in each region to collect seeds from local trees, shrubs, and other plants that are best suited for each area targeted by the project. This information will be leveraged to identify and produce the seeds and tree saplings that are best suited to each area targeted by the project.

#### 1.3.2.3 Sub-activity: Support the production of agroforestry tree saplings in collaboration with smallholder farmers.
The land use maps developed in 1.3.1 will support the identification of the areas allocated for agroforestry systems in each region. The project will establish a seed bank in each region to collect seeds from local trees, shrubs, and other plants that are best suited for each area targeted by the project.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 [page] | 54

### 1.3.2 Sub-activity: Develop plans to manage saplings and growing trees in crop, grass/pasture, and community land areas, including the development of a Community Action Cycle (CAC) for sustainable livelihoods and food security.

This sub-activity will ensure sustainable management of cultivated areas, especially in community lands. Community Action Cycles will be developed with communities to manage saplings and growing trees. They will also participate in the allocation and management of pastures, to avoid conflict between crop farmers and pastoralists. The CACs will provide a framework for communities on how to ensure the development of plants, efficiently manage shared resources, and handle conflict.

### Component 2: Supporting the development of CSA and agroforestry value chains

#### Sub-activity 1: Support the development of CSA and agroforestry value chains that are more connected and ultimately more diverse and sustainable livelihoods for the target communities by fostering the development of connected value chains (e.g., through CCAFS) and increasing access to markets.

This sub-activity will address the disproportionately faced by women and youth entrepreneurs for starting and running businesses, including access to finance, land, inputs, and extension services. It will also support the development of a CSA and agroforestry value chain that is more connected and ultimately more diverse and sustainable livelihoods for the target communities.

**Outcome 2.1:** CSA and agroforestry VCA are more connected and reach more smallholder farmers.

* **Activity 2.1.1:** Support the creation of inclusive private sector value chains (e.g., via CCAFS) through activity 2.1.1 "Support the creation of inclusive private sector value chains" (see page 54).

**Outcome 2.1.2:** Smallholder farmers have access to markets and value chain services.

* **Activity 2.1.2:** Facilitate market linkages for smallholder farmers, including through the development of a CSA and agroforestry VCA, and will it give the foundations to address barrier 5 "Limited connections and value chain services" (see page 4). Lack of access to markets and value chain services is one of the most significant bottlenecks for their empowerment (e.g., Limited access to finance and land, exclusion from decision-making, lack of information on market prices, etc.). This activity will also support the development of specific and distinct needs of smallholder farmers, women, and youth. The activity will mainly target businesses that deliver added productivity and diversity in the CSA and agroforestry value chains (e.g., through the development of a CSA and agroforestry VCA). It will provide opportunities for women and youth, and/or are connected to the mitigation and adaptation technologies prioritized by smallholder farmers.

**Benefits:** 20% of the beneficiaries of activity 2.1.1 will be farmers with access to services (leveraging with the beneficiaries of 1.1.1, 1.1.3, and 1.1.4). The target number of farmers is 5,000. This sub-activity aims to reach at least 22,000 farmers (5% of farmers receiving the CSA and agroforestry training), 100 private sector companies providing services to smallholder farmers, and 30 agro-processing technology suppliers (one per each commune) and 24 producer associations (5 in each commune).

* **Activity 2.1.1:** Will be delivered through 4 sub-activities:

    * **2.1.1.1:** Facilitate market linkages for smallholder farmers, including through the development of a CSA and agroforestry VCA.
    * **2.1.1.2:** Support the creation of inclusive private sector value chains (e.g., via CCAFS).
    * **2.1.1.3:** Facilitate market linkages for smallholder farmers, including through the development of a CSA and agroforestry VCA.
    * **2.1.1.4:** Support the creation of inclusive private sector value chains (e.g., via CCAFS).
