Linkages will be developed across crop and livestock value chains to increase farmers' access to markets and information for CSA and agripreneurial technologies and related support from suppliers. Establishing linkages with the private sector is critical in order to ensure that the innovations and solutions created through existing digital platforms in Mali, focusing on value chain connectivity (e.g., Sugu Bud Kourou) will be scaled up and replicated across other regions of West Africa. The innovative companies created by Alibaba will be extended to include more stakeholders and will be leveraged to increase the number of farmers and service providers that can access these innovations and solutions through the initiative resulting in a target of 22,000 farmers and 100 service providers through this platform. However, the expansion of the digital platforms will also require scaling up the number of users on the platform. The numbers of the rural population during the 5-year project period. IAT will showcase the use of digital platforms to increase access to markets and information for CSA and agripreneurial technologies and related support from suppliers. This will be achieved by ensuring that the same cohort of businesses identified and supported in 2.1.1, ensures seamless continuity and alignment with the objectives of the project.

### 2.1.1.2 Sub-activity: Deliver technical assistance to private sector businesses that are involved in CSA and agripreneurial technologies and related support from suppliers

The sub-activity will focus on providing technical assistance to private sector service providers offering business plans, 100 private sector companies providing CSA and agripreneurial technologies and related support from suppliers. The technical assistance will be provided through the development of specific guidelines for each company based on their inclusive services and marketing strategies to specifically target women and youth. The technical assistance will be provided by IAT's technical team in collaboration with the private sector companies, thereby defining their service offering and therefore identify the specific support and/or adjustments to the business model needed to ensure that they are able to provide these services effectively. These guidelines will be documented and reviewed to identify patterns that would lead to the development of specific guidelines across all 100 private sector companies.

Workshops will be conducted to share the new guidelines and provide them with clear instructions on how to implement each guideline, as well as to review their progress in implementing these guidelines and additional support required. The 100 companies will be selected for inclusion in this activity on the basis of their ability to provide a range of services that are relevant to CSA and agripreneurial technologies and related support from suppliers. These services include seed supply, pest control inputs providers for modern seed varieties, pest reduction services, and services related to the processing of agricultural products. The selection process will also take into account the level of innovation being provided by each company as well as their capacity to provide these services in a sustainable manner.

### 2.1.1.3 Sub-activity: Develop technical guidelines for private sector technology companies working in solar irrigation and biodigesters, including informal providers

Private sector technology providers working in solar irrigation and biodigesters, especially the informal providers, will be supported through the development of specific guidelines to ensure that they are able to provide a range of services relevant to CSA and agripreneurial technologies and related support from suppliers. These services include providing information on the benefits of using solar irrigation systems and biodigesters as well as providing training for farmers on how to use these technologies effectively.

The sub-activity will include grouping technology providers into Economic Interest Groups in each community to support the scaling up of their activities, including the development of specific guidelines for each group. The guidelines will be developed based on the needs identified by the informal providers and as per the tried and tested approach taken by IAT's consultancy biodigester project MERRY. The guidelines will also include information on how to access financing from MFIs and other financial institutions.

Koulouba and Kouegno, the IAGT's targeted 12 circles avoid overlapping with MERIT's locations. Workshops will be conducted in each of these communities to share the new guidelines and provide them with clear instructions on how to implement each guideline as well as review their progress in implementing these guidelines and additional support required.

### 2.1.1.4 Sub-activity: Implement training to producer associations to support them to deliver agglomeration and value addition services to their members

Producers' associations will be supported with the necessary knowledge and build the capacity of their members due to the proximity and accessibility of these associations. The activity will leverage cooperatives and producer's associations to support access to broader innovations and solutions by providing producer associations to provide services such as cleaning, grading, and packaging in order to ensure that they are able to provide a range of value-added services for their members. These workshops will be conducted in each community where the IAGT is targeting 5 communities. The workshops will be used to transfer the knowledge to their members and support them in implementing best practices. These workshops will aim to reach 340 producer associations, corresponding to 5 in each commune.

### Output 2.2: Smallholder farming communities, especially women and youth, can more easily overcome barriers to entrepreneurship in CSA and agribusinesses.

#### 94 Objective 2.2.1: Support local financial institutions to increase access to finance for smallholder farmers, especially women and youth, for CSA/agroforestry investments

#### 96 Activity 2.2.1.1: Establish a network of microfinance associations in the project region

This activity will collectively address barrier #4 "mobility for smallholder and subsistence farmers to invest in inputs for climate-smart agriculture" by increasing access to finance, particularly for women and youth.

Limited consideration of the specific and distinct needs of women and youth in the development and management of rural financial institutions has been a major obstacle to their inclusion. This activity will address this issue through increased access to finance and land, exclusion from decision-making processes.

Activity 2.2.1.1 will be delivered through 3 sub-activities:

#### 96 Sub-activity: Extend and replicate the Albaraka-VSLA model (particular in Seguio and Koulikro communities)

This sub-activity aims to extend the Albaraka-VSLA model, which has been successfully implemented by IATTA, to other communities. The Albaraka-VSLA model is a microfinance initiative that provides small loans to women farmers for CSA investments.

The Albaraka-VSLA model will be extended and replicated in Seguio and Koulikro communities through the following steps:

1. Establishing new VSLAs: IATTA will establish 20 new VSLAs in Seguio, 5 in Koulikro, and 3 in Gao, Mopti, and Tombouctou.
2. Training VSLA facilitators and members on best management practices and income opportunities.

#### 96 Sub-activity: Extend the Albaraka-VSLA model (particular in Seguio and Koulikro communities)

This sub-activity aims to extend the Albaraka-VSLA model, which has been successfully implemented by IATTA, to other communities. The Albaraka-VSLA model is a microfinance initiative that provides small loans to women farmers for CSA investments.

established in 1997 provides services in IATF’s regions. A detailed assessment of financial institutions available in Mal and IAT locations is presented in the Feasibility Report (Chapter III, Section 3.1.4). Such an assessment will be used to develop a set of investment guidelines that will allow them to reach women and youth entrepreneurs, even in remote rural areas. The project will also work with financial institutions and other stakeholders to overcome the barriers preventing microfinance institutions to reach and serve more women and youth entrepreneurs. This will include providing training on gender equality and gender risk management techniques to reduce the risk profile of smallholder farmers, such as crop insurance. Workshops will be held in collaboration with financial institutions and other relevant stakeholders to identify institutions and provide them with clear directions regarding how to develop and implement inclusive policies.

The project does not expect to make significant investments in providing specialized technical services to financial institutions. However, it is expected that the project will leverage existing resources and there are opportunities for leveraging on other projects in IATF that are designed to increase access to inclusive financial services. These include the Microfinance Project (MFP) under the Inclusive Growth and Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGIF); G4: Growing Agricultural Banks; A Financial Sector Transformation Strategy (FASTS); and the Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment in Agriculture and Rural Development (GEWARD) project under the Global Water Partnership (GWP) countries - Phase I. The project will hold in-depth conversations with project representatives and specific stakeholders to identify opportunities for leveraging existing resources, as well as effective opportunities for leveraging and develop the detailed activities to support this collaboration.

### Activity 2.2.2 aims to enhance the skills and connectivity of women youth smallholder farmers and/or agricultural entrepreneurs in Malawi and IATF regions through training and capacity building, access to financial resources, thus strengthening their role within a sustainable supply chain. This initiative will provide comprehensive training on business management, marketing, and finance for women and youth beneficiaries and local financial institutions mentioned in section 2.2.1 as well as leveraging funding opportunities towards the development of an inclusive financial service delivery model. The project will work with 2.1.1 PRIPS II (the World Bank Funded Project) targets women and youth to support provision in agriculture and related sectors. This initiative will also provide training on business management, marketing, and finance for beneficiaries. In addition, the project will collaborate with PRIPS II and other similar projects to access financial resources. Beneficiaries of activity 2.2.2 will include women and youth smallholder farmers and/or agricultural entrepreneurs in Malawi and IATF regions.

### Activity 2.2.1 will be delivered through 3 sub-activities:

#### Sub-activity: Selection of youth entrepreneurs, 1,500 young entrepreneurs will be selected by Alitana in Gwag, Mopoti, and Tondoloto to enhance their business capacity and connect them with financial institutions. The selection process will focus on the most enterprising and resilient approach. These entrepreneurs will be trained in core business skills, including business plan development, marketing, finance, and risk management. They will also receive training on loan applications to improve access to finances, which is one of the main barriers hindering youth entrepreneurship (20–prospective young) to start and expand their business activity after receiving the training. The project will provide a comprehensive package for women and youth entrepreneurs in the business proposals including scope, rate of return and possibility of drawing additional and alternative funds. This initiative will also provide training on business management, marketing, finance, and risk management to deliver the capacity building. These business proposals will provide information about the type of product or service that is being offered, the market demand for this product or service, supply (e.g., seed, feed, irrigation), market tools/equipment, and livestock health services. farm input supply, e.g., seed, feed, irrigation, market tools/equipment, and livestock health services.

#### Sub-activity: Strengthening the business capacity of women entrepreneurs, including women-led agricultural enterprises in Malawi and IATF regions through training and capacity building covered by Alitana, 3,750 women will be targeted and provided with the same support as youth entrepreneurs. Inclusion of women in this initiative is critical to ensure that they are not left behind. The project has a strong focus on women-led processing units, which grant the opportunity to women in rural areas to lead revenue-generating activities. This initiative will provide training on business management, marketing, and finance for innovative women to startup new business with some financial support. Like sub-activity 2.2.1, the provision of processing units will be provided based on the co-evaluation of the business proposal


---

including scope, rate of return, and possibility of drawing additional and alternative funds. Women’s entrepreneurial potential exists in the processing and value addition of agricultural products (e.g. fruit juice and jam production), as well as in the production of agro-based products such as palm oil, coconut oil, butter; seeds for condiments and nuts/seeds for soaps are the most frequently mentioned products collected by women. The sub-activity will be implemented through a combination of activities aimed at enhancing women’s entrepreneurial skills and their access to finance, production services and distribution networks through the promotion of improved agroforestry systems as well as women’s business skills to enhance their enterprise development.

2.2.2.3 Sub-activity: Connect youth and women entrepreneurs with local financial institutions and private sector service providers (e.g., input suppliers) in order to improve access to finance, production services and distribution networks. This sub-activity will be implemented through a combination of activities aimed at improving access to finance and integrate sustainable value chains. This support will be provided through connections between youth and women entrepreneurs with local financial institutions (banks and microfinance institutions), and private sector service providers (e.g., agro-input suppliers and CSA technology suppliers). The target group for this sub-activity is women and young entrepreneurs trained and supported by Aisha’s CCF YAT that are targeted for this sub-activity.

IAAT project will collaborate with local financial institutions to address gaps in knowledge, information, and capacity building of the financial institutions on how to provide credit to youth and women farmers. These activities would include (based on assessed need and demand) orientation on specific financial products provided by the financial institution, training for financial institution staff on how to identify and train clients based on anticipated business opportunities.

The activity will also engage with private sector companies such as agro-input suppliers or CSA technology suppliers to provide technical assistance and capacity building workshops to these companies to the farmers or in partnership with financial institutions vetted farmers. If meaningful financing opportunities are identified, the project will support the development of a revolving fund for small-scale farmers.

Output 2.3: Increased adoption of climate-smart agricultural technologies by smallholder farmers

99 Output 2.3 will be delivered through activity 2.3.1 "Install and support the productive use of biodigester technology in smallholder farms" (see Table 4.1). The project will install 500 biodigesters on smallholder farms to directly address barrier 6: Market, technology and cultural barriers for the adoption of low-carbon agriculture technologies. This will increase the productivity and revenue of smallholder farmers whilst reducing GHG emissions from livestock production.

This activity will increase the productivity and revenue of smallholder farmers whilst reducing GHG emissions from livestock production. The project will install 500 biodigesters on smallholder farms to directly address barrier 6: Market, technology and cultural barriers for the adoption of low-carbon agriculture technologies. This will increase the productivity and revenue of smallholder farmers whilst reducing GHG emissions from livestock production.

The activity will install 1,000 solar irrigation systems to install new and replace existing diesel generator irrigation pumps on smallholder farms in order to directly address barrier 7: Market, technology and cultural barriers for the adoption of low-carbon agriculture technologies. This will increase the productivity and revenue of smallholder farmers whilst reducing GHG emissions from livestock production.

The activity will ensure that the full potential of these technologies is harnessed by (i) developing the understanding of the benefits of these technologies among smallholder farmers, (ii) providing training to support their long-term productive use. In order to enhance the development of the project, the project will organize a series of awareness raising and capacity building workshops for smallholder farmers on their resources to provide tailored and high-quality services to smallholder farmers. Beneficiaries of activity 3.1 include 500 biodigester owners, 250 solar irrigation system owners, 400 crop farmers, and 40 Energy Interest Groups.

101 Activity 2.3.1 will be delivered through 2 sub-activities:

2.3.1.1 Sub-activity: Install 1,000 solar irrigation systems for smallholder farms from the suppliers identified in Table 4.1. The project prioritizes replacing diesel pumps with solar irrigation systems wherever possible to reduce emissions and increase water productivity. This will be achieved by installing a new solar pump or replacement of existing diesel fuel-based water pumps (small sized 5-7 hp) by solar pumps which is economically feasible for smallholder farmers. The project will also provide training on the installation, maintenance, location and size of the solar pumping systems during the initial preparation phase of the project. Current cost estimation includes 9.4 and 5-hp total solar pump irrigation systems. This size may very depending on the

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 [Page] | 59

location and farming type. A detailed profiling of solar pumps during the initial stage of the project will provide this information.

At 100 villages, farmers will not replace diesel fuel-based pumps. IAT will target at least 35% solar pumps installed by the project to replace existing diesel fuel-based pumps that provide higher economic return than solar pumps (e.g., in terms of water yield per kilowatt-hour and cost benefit analysis) that varies with pump size and locations. In addition, IAT will support the installation of new solar pumps at 100 villages where farmers are willing to replace existing diesel fuel-based pumps.

The potential and viability of solar pump irrigation exists within the five project regions using the PAYG model. IAT will also provide financial assistance for the purchase of solar pumps (see below for details on contributions). The additional 300 solar pumping systems will be promoted by using Pay-As-You-Go (PAYG) technology, which is a prepayment system that allows farmers to pay for electricity in advance and receive electricity supply in kilowatt-hours. IAT will only cover the initial start-up cost of all target beneficiary systems under this subactivity. The PAYG model will be implemented by using solar pumps from ECOFARM, ECO-TECH, ENDev, EcoEnergy, and PEA Group to scale out solar irrigation systems in the project locations. Farmers will pay for electricity supply in advance through a mobile phone or other electronic means.

The PAYG model will allow farmers to prepay for electricity by using their mobile phones. IAT will cover only the initial start-up cost of all target beneficiary systems under this subactivity. The PAYG model will support private sector investment in scaling up solar pumping systems in Mali (see scaling approach in Feasibility Study). Farmers will pay for electricity supply in advance through a mobile phone or other electronic means. Suppliers and financial institutions based on their outreach regions are listed in the first part of the project document.

IAT will also provide financial assistance to private sector suppliers and financial institutions in the location projects. The investment from private solar technology suppliers and financial institutions will be used for the installation of new solar pumps at 100 villages where farmers are willing to install the solar pump through IAT. Key selection criteria will be households currently using diesel powered generators to irrigate their crops, willingness to replace existing diesel fuel-based pumps with solar pumps, and ability to pay for what they can afford to use as they need. Similar to the project scaling of solar pump systems, the PAYGO model will be used to reach the target number of biodigesters. The PAYGO model will provide incentives to private sector technology suppliers and investors to scale up the remaining 4,000 by using the PAYGO model. IAT will cover the index costs for the first 12 months of operation of the biodigester system. This includes training and capacity building of biodigester users and private sector technology suppliers. Like solar irrigation systems, this will be a one-time cost of the biodigester system to the private biodigester technology suppliers on an installment basis.

| SCI Mali, as the Executive Unit, will implement activities 2.3.1.1 and 2.3.1.2 through private sector suppliers who have been selected for the biodigester system procurement. Once the suppliers have been selected, they will implement under SCI Mali's supervision.

#### 2.3.1.3 Sub-activity: Deliver targeted training for those receiving new technologies in 2.3.1 and 2.3.2

| IAT will deliver training to beneficiaries of biodigester systems (2.3.1) and solar pump systems (2.3.2). The training will be delivered by the same extension agents supported in component 1. IAT will invest in training activities to ensure that the beneficiaries of these technologies are able to use them effectively, safely, and efficiently. This includes avoiding breakdowns by providing tailored training through farmer field schools and extension agents. Training will include: (i) awareness raising on the benefits of biodigester systems; (ii) training on requirements during installation; (iii) requirements for production use (e.g., identification of appropriate food crops to be used as feedstock, selection of suitable sites for biodigesters); and (iv) establishment of business relations between the system e.g., compost/fertilizer sale, improved livestock management, etc. The training will also include a service support component in activity 2.3.1.4 (development of service support system). The training will create the opportunity for beneficiaries to share their experiences with other farmers and provide feedback on the operation of biodigester systems. Farmers who are biodigester users will be trained as trainers to train other farmers, especially those who do not have access to repairs that they can directly perform to maintain continuity of activities. All farmers who are biodigester users will be trained in 2.3.1 and 2.3.2.

#### 2.3.1.4 Sub-activity: Generate technical guidance and deliver commune-level workshops on the installation and operation of biodigester systems and solar pump systems for local government officials and construction companies

| The project aims to create a sustainable market for solar irrigation systems and biodigesters by emphasizing the development of a strong supply chain for the technologies. Informal operators, including local technicians and repairmen, will be trained in 2.1.3 and 2.1.4 (training on technical requirements for the installation of O&M for the long-term productive use of solar irrigation systems) as well as training on technical requirements for the installation of biodigesters of 2.1.3 as well as develop the required networks to disseminate the technologies in the future. The training will be provided by SCI Mali and IAT.

#### Component 2: Increasing Institutional Capacity and Knowledge

| 102 | *Component 2* aims at strengthening institutional capacity at government entities at the local, regional, and national levels, as well as communities through Community Action Cycles (CACs), improving their adaptation to climate change. This will be achieved by building the capacity of government institutions for collaboration for knowledge-sharing for scaling out CSA, enhancing the impacts and reach of components 1 and 2. Component 2 consists of three sub-components: 2.2.1 *Enhanced institutional capacity*, which includes planning and best practices to address agriculture-related climate risk; and 3.2 *Enhanced knowledge sharing and coordination of best practices in CSA and agroforestry across sectors*.

| This is a conservative estimate as biodigester systems may benefit multiple households

### Output 3.1: Increased institutional capacity in climate change adaptation and mitigation planning and best practices to address agriculture-related climate risks

#### 3.1.2.3 Output 3.1 will be delivered through activity 3.1.1 “Strengthening institutional capacity in local climate change adaptation and mitigation planning” and sub-activity 3.1.2.3 “Technical capability building of Main Climate Fund in prioritizing and targeting investment in CSA and agroforestry”

This activity is designed to build the technical capability of the Main Climate Fund (MCF) in prioritizing and targeting investments in climate change adaptation and mitigation planning, with a focus on CSA and agroforestry. It will also support the MCF in incorporating climate considerations into its investment decisions.

The activity will be implemented through two sub-activities:

1. **Sub-activity 3.1.2.3.1: Strengthening institutional capacity at the local level** This sub-activity aims to enhance the technical capability of local governments and communities to design, implement, and coordinate adaptation and mitigation activities in CSA and agroforestry.

    - **Community Action Groups (CAGs)** will be established as a key mechanism for community engagement. These groups will work with local government authorities to develop action plans that address specific needs or challenges related to climate change.
    
    - **Training sessions** will be organized at the local level, focusing on CSA and agroforestry adaptation planning.

2. **Sub-activity 3.1.2.3.2: Support new and existing Community Action Groups in five regions for prioritization of investments in CSA and agroforestry and climate change adaptation planning**

    - **Community Action Groups** are essential for the empowerment of communities and their involvement in decision-making processes at all levels.
    
    - The project will aim to strengthen the capacity of these groups, particularly those that have been established as part of the Community Action Plan (CAP) process. These groups will be supported through training sessions on CSA and agroforestry adaptation planning.

The activity will also support the MCF in prioritizing investments in CSA and agroforestry by incorporating climate considerations into its investment decisions. This will ensure that funding is directed towards activities that are most effective in addressing climate change impacts at both the local level, as well as their integration into community action plans. Community Action Groups will be supported through training sessions on CSA and agroforestry adaptation planning.

The activity will also support the MCF in prioritizing investments in CSA and agroforestry by incorporating climate considerations into its investment decisions. ## Activity 1.2: Strengthening the implementation of the Mail Climate Fund in CSA and agroforestry systems

### 3.2.1 Sub-activity: Enhance the capacity of the Mail Climate Fund to target adaptation and mitigation activities

#### 3.2.1.1 Sub-sub-activity: Enhance knowledge sharing and coordination of best practices in CSA and agroforestry across stakeholders

**Outcome:** 3.2.1 will be delivered through activity 3.2.1 “Enhanced learning, conversion and contribution to national databases for CSA and Agroforestry,” which will directly address barrier 7: “Public sector institutions and community governance mechanisms are not sufficiently informed about the latest developments in climate change adaptation and mitigation.”

The activity will compile the insights gained from the IAMT project and disseminate them to national and regional stakeholders through a series of workshops, training sessions, and knowledge networks. A special emphasis will be placed on building an enabling environment supporting the exchange of knowledge across different levels (national, regional, local) and with other stakeholder groups in order to enhance the learning process.

Activity 3.2.1.1 will contribute to building an enabling environment supporting the exchange of knowledge among stakeholders at national and regional levels, especially regarding the adaptation and mitigation objectives and strategies. The insights gained from these events will be documented and discussed with relevant stakeholders to interpret their relevance for climate change adaptation and mitigation in the areas of interest.

The activity will also aim to contribute to building an enabling environment supporting the exchange of knowledge among stakeholders at national and regional levels, especially regarding the adaptation and mitigation objectives and strategies. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 [Page 43]

## 3.1.3 Sub-activity: Organize annual knowledge sharing event to report best practices on CSA learned from the project

Annual meetings will be organized to share the knowledge generated through the CGIAR-CCAFS project, including lessons and experiences of smallholder farmers in communities (including women, youth, smallholder farmers and agroecoprotectors and critics), NGOs, CSOs, private sector actors, and other stakeholders. The meetings will also provide an opportunity for participants to share their experiences with the challenges they face and impact risks and potential mitigation, and identify solutions to improve project implementation.

Important: The meeting will be organized in a way that it is open to all communities supported by the project and where relevant, neighbouring countries, to allow for a broader reach of the lessons learned.

## 1.1 By acting within the same communities and regions but across different areas of the agricultural system, the components are complementary and mutually reinforcing

Examples of how the project components are interrelated and intertwined are outlined below:

### Component 1: In support of component 2
- **Component 1** and **Component 2**: within the same target communes, component 1 will work to first increase access to climate-smart agricultural technologies (CSATs) for smallholder farmers in order to provide them with access to value-added services and to buyers. In addition, component 2 will increase access to finance to fund the inputs needed by smallholder farmers to adopt CSATs.
- **Component 1** will also support the development of a database of climate-smart agricultural technologies for smallholder farmers delivered in Component 1, by actively supporting the planting of 31.285 ha of agroforestry trees and shrubs (AFS) and conducting maps to identify suitability factors, cultivation of fire suppressions and their distribution across project regions.

### Component 2: In support of component 1
- **Component 2** will support the building that sell prioritized climate-smart technologies (polyculture and biodiversification) in order to increase smallholder farmers' market share.
- **Component 3**: In support of component 1 and 2, component 3's institutional support will support an overall shift in the decision-making process by involving all stakeholders in the project. This will be achieved through a participatory approach that will help build local interest and investment in the techniques advocated as part of the training for smallholder farmers in components 1 and 2.

### As outlined in section B.1, the project will focus on the 12 communes, and within them 48 communes, in the Tombouctou, Mopti and Sikasso regions. The target communes are those that have been identified as having a high potential for agroforestry systems. Within these regions, communes' beneficiaries will include smallholder farmers and their households, women and youth groups, private sector business owners, extension services members and members of public institutions.

### Output 1.2
- **Output 1.2**: Increase CGIAR-CCAFS project services in target communes to reduce the loss due to changing climate impacts and increase the resilience of smallholder farmers' livelihoods through implementation of CSA (e.g., by increasing access to extension services).
- **GCF Target**: Vulnerable people.
- **MRA**: Vulnerable people.
- **AAR**: Vulnerable people.

### Output 2.1
- **Output 2.1**: Increase CGIAR-CCAFS project services in target communes to increase farm productivity and security through improved decision-making by smallholder farmers, supported by extension and online platforms.
- **GCF Target**: Vulnerable people.
- **MRA**: Vulnerable people.
- **AAR**: Vulnerable people.

### Output 2.3
- **Output 2.3**: Increase CGIAR-CCAFS project services in target communes to minimize the increasing risks of climate change by using budgetary inputs (e.g., breeding 2-4 cows) and having access to water, willingness to engage in the project.
- **GCF Target**: Vulnerable people.
- **MRA**: Energy generation and jobs sector.

### 117. For further details on the project beneficiaries and the selection criteria, please refer to section E of the funding proposal and Annex 2, Section IV Vulnerability and Targeting Approach.

#### B.4.3. Project Beneficiaries (max. 1000 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

This project will have four Executive Entities (EEs): Save the Children Federation, Inc. (SCF), and Save the Children International [SG] in Mali. SCU will channel a WEI with responsibilities for supervision and oversight of the project implementation. The EEs will be responsible for the overall management of the project. SCU will provide technical support in designing, implementing, monitoring, evaluating, and reporting. SCI Mali will be responsible for the execution of the project activities and will work closely with the local communities in collaboration with the CGI. As implementing EE1, SCI Mali will be responsible for the execution of the selection of technical and minor interactive activities (e.g., solar irrigation pumps, water storage technologies). The project implementation will be carried out through a series of phases: 1) Project preparation; 2) Project implementation; 3) Project evaluation. The CGI will provide support to SCI Mali in MES and AED projects as well as Mali’s NDA to the GCF. Save the Children (SC) as an Accredited Entity (AE), will maintain reporting channels to the GCF for all project activities. SCI Mali will be responsible for the implementation of the project, and will maintain reporting channels to MEF & S to maintain and increase the country project ownership. SCU will provide accountability and oversight on behalf of the CGI. The CGI will also ensure that Save the Children will save the Children’s Moment to ensure compliance and high-quality delivery of the project and financial management.

#### 109. All field-level activities which need direct engagement with farmers and communities, local government and civil society organizations (CSOs) will be implemented by SCI Mali. The CGI will provide technical support to SCI Mali. Local NGOs will be procured through the pre-bid process. SCI Mali will be the procuring entity, and the procurement will be carried out in accordance with the relevant national laws and regulations. SCI Mali will also engage local biodigesters, and other CSAs (technologies) SCI Mali will follow an open bid process to purchase from the private biogas digesters.

#### 120. A high-level Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be responsible for the project's strategic direction and oversee implementation of the project. The PSC will consist of representatives of CGI, SCU, MEF & S, SCI Mali, which will manage day-to-day operations. Representatives of SCI Mali and the ECO will chair the PSC, which will also include representatives from the Ministry of Environment and Sanitation, Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Livestock and Fisheries, Ministry of Water Resources, Ministry of Health, Ministry of Education, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Planning and Development, Ministry of Trade and Industry, Ministry of Energy and ## Advisory Committee

The advisory committee will include subject matter specialists in agriculture, climate change, agribusiness, gender and social inclusion, conflict management, market system development, and other experts from SCA Mali, SCSDS, and AGRO-Mali.

### Project Implementation Unit (PIU)

122. The PIU will monitor and oversee technical and financial implementation, fiduciary processes, auditing, and reporting on the GCF-funded activities in Mali. It will also be responsible for ensuring that all projects are in line with the GCF and provide it with guidance and direction while receiving regular reports on project implementation. The PIU will work collaboratively with the SCA Mali, SCSDS, and AGRO-Mali to ensure efficient use of resources.

123. The management oversight of the PIU will be held by SCI Mali, which has the legal standing necessary to implement GCF activities in Mali. It is also legally and organizationally responsible for implementing GCF activities in Mali. The PIU will collaborate closely with national ministries and implementing partners to ensure efficient use of resources.

124. To meet the fiduciary requirements and standards of the GCF, legal and management oversight of the PIU will be held by SCI Mali, which has the legal standing necessary to implement GCF activities in Mali. It is also legally and organizationally responsible for implementing GCF activities in Mali.

resources and co-financing to manage project governance and implementation will manage of downstream

The PIU will deliver results through a combination of direct and indirect interactions with the 48 communities in 12 operations of IATF activities in the field. They will organize training, meetings, conferences; base with service providers, government agencies, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders to ensure the smooth implementation of project activities. While the PIU will deliver technical and practical support for the delivery of project activities, the communities will be responsible for the planning and delivery of output specific outputs (as depicted in Figure 8.4.1).

All activities will be implemented in line with the project's Gender Assessment and Action Plan (see Annex E), which aligns with the Government’s policies on gender equality, development, and child safeguarding policies and Prevention of Sexual Exploitation and Abuse policy guidelines. Per the Gender Action Plan, all project activities will be designed to ensure that women are fully involved in the project (e.g., smallholder farmers, youth). Activities will contribute to gender transformation and ensure community participation.

All activities will be implemented in line with the project's Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP), which is aligned with the GCF’s safeguard policies and includes a Grievance Redress Mechanism.


### 8.5.3 Climate Change Vulnerability in Malawi

There is a significant gap in climate finance in Malawi that the GCF is well-positioned to fill and address critical areas of climate change vulnerability that the Government of Malawi has prioritized and committed to, however is unable to fund from its own resources. The country’s limited access to international capital markets means that it cannot raise funds for GCF investment – Malawi has very limited public resources, and as a country recovering from a series of increasing droughts, floods, and cyclones, the Government of Malawi does not have the ability to invest in its agriculture and natural resource management sector. At the same time, the environment is increasingly vulnerable due to climate change, with 80% of the population living on less than $2 per day, smallholder farmers (75%), and youth.

The country has a total population of approximately 19 million people, of which about 36.4% are farming households) and 700,580 people directly, all of whom are among the most vulnerable to climate change in Malawi. There is a significant gap in climate change adaptation and mitigation finance for smallholder farmers and youth in Malawi. The country has experienced extreme poverty and food and water insecurity amongst households. The current and future needs of smallholder farmers and youth are exacerbated by the impact of climate change, which will continue to increase as the Government, Also, the perceived poor economic returns and high-risk nature of investment in smallholder farmers are unlikely to attract private sector investments. This is further compounded by the lack of a legal framework to enable the IATF to catalyse five regions with scalable results for a full parallel shift in other regions, which will be critical to achieving the Government’s climate change objectives.

Ongoing conflict within domestic resources: the impact of COVID and sanctions from the ECDOWA has hindered Malawi’s ability to access international capital markets. The country is currently unable to meet its GCF commitments as a result. As only USD 25 million and $10 million are respectively dedicated to agriculture and environmental projects, there is an urgent need for additional climate finance to support the Green Climate Fund, which will be critical in addressing Malawi’s climate change vulnerability.

The estimated funding gap for climate-smart agriculture with IATF is USD 39.2 million. The estimated funding gap for climate financing through IATF is USD 29 million. The estimated funding gap for climate finance through the AFD is USD 108.5 million. In 2022, Malawi’s Public Treasury raised only 7.9% of its resource objective and the AFDB identified new opportunities to support Malawi in the areas of agriculture, energy, water resources, and disaster risk reduction for elections, the lack of security, and the impact of climate change as headwinds for the country's economic development.

The GCF has been a key player in supporting Malawi’s efforts to address climate change. Since 2015, the GCF has provided USD 39 million in funding to support Malawi’s climate action plan. However, the amount of money that has often been limited. The Government's National Strategy on Climate Change has not been fully implemented due to lack of resources and capacity. In addition, despite the country’s strong commitment to access to finance, the investment it has received is viewed insufficient with a budget of USD 29 million largely focused on climate change adaptation rather than mitigation. This is further compounded by the limited focus in climate financing through IATF 4.1.5. Although the exact level of funding needed for climate-resilient smallholder farmers and youth remains unclear, estimates suggest that an additional investment of US$307 million will be required to improve land management strategies

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 [P] | [48]

## Infrastructure, increase access to technologies, structured partnerships, and advisory services, especially for women: The National Agricultural Sector Investment Plan (NISP) estimates implementation for agricultural transformation in the sector at US$ 15 billion.

Similarly, the total estimated cost of Mac's NDC implementation is USD $27 billion, which includes water management and irrigation infrastructure (US$ 3.8 billion), climate-resilient agriculture and food security solutions (US$ 500 million); sustainable crop nutrient management (US$2.5 million; and GHG emissions reduction from the livestock sector (US$14.6 billion). Mac's NDC also includes a USD $790 million investment in renewable energy building to address systemic issues related to the design and delivery of climate-resilient interventions that fit local contexts, including the development of an enabling environment for private sector participation in climate solutions. The implementation of Mac’s NDC is conditional on technical and financial support from international donors. In addition, Mac's NDC includes a USD $10 million investment in capacity building and technical expert support to close the funding gap; and the complex political and conflict situation in Mal also pose challenges.

For example, the International Finance Corporation (IFC) has provided USD$ 250 million in financing for Mac’s NDC, which is estimated at EUR$7 billion between January 2013 and September 2017. In addition, there are some projects that have been identified as potential sources of funding for Mac's NDC, including blended finance; low participation in the development of climate change strategies; and limited access to international financial institutions (IFIs) and other sources of financing. The IATF has developed a detailed Feasibility Study, Chapter IX, Section 8.1.18. The IATF is seeking to address private-sector partnerships through blended finance.

### Climate finance to smallholder agriculture in Mal is also disproportionately low when compared with the opportunity cost of climate change impacts on agricultural productivity and food security. The main barriers limiting climate financing in this area include the low investment readiness of agri-businesses; the need, at the national level, for a more comprehensive approach to climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies; the difficulty of engaging assets to make them attractive to large scale investors; the lack of access to finance for smallholder farmers; and the limited capacity of financial institutions to create new products. The alternative funding options for supporting investment at the scale proposed by the IATF project for smallholder agriculture in Mal are limited, including blended finance (see Section 8.1.20), public-private partnerships (PPPs) and microfinancing through OIFD 2.3.

The IATF project is designed to complement existing climate change interventions in Mal, particularly those implemented by the World Bank. The project aims to build on the success of these interventions; USAID Abassa commenced implementation on October 01, 2020, and is expected extend to 2026; research and development (R&D) projects are being implemented through the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI); and the Food Security and Resilience in Mal project is being implemented by the World Bank. The IATF project will build on these interventions by strengthening local systems for climate change adaptation and resilience, including by supporting the capacity of strategic organizations for community development and enhanced adaptation and resilience capacities as well as enhancing the capacity of smallholder farmers to access finance.

The project has been designed to build on and complement the existing work of the Abassa project. More information about this project can be found in Section 8.1.20. For more information, please refer to section B.3., and for the corresponding breakdown of the split of the co-financing by component please see Annex A. The IATF project is also designed to complement the work being implemented under the IATF in recognition of the value it brings to the climate resilience of vulnerable Malians. There is a strong alignment between Mac’s NDC and the IATF project, which will be reflected in the IATF project's advice progress on its national climate change adaptation and mitigation commitments that IFAD focused on local and social dimensions.

### Progress on climate action in Mal, including gender equity, clean water and sanitation, and sustainable agriculture, is likely to be compromised if Mac’s NDC is not implemented. The lack of implementation will not only the climate change agenda is compromised; but any progress made at wider SDGs is likely to fail to achieve ambition as the country vulnerable population is likely to increase without extraneous financial support.

#### B.5 Exit strategy (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
**Page 55**
---

135. Project strategies and activities include a strong focus on sustaining and continuing results and benefits beyond the implementation period, enabling long-term ownership, operations, and maintenance of investments; and supporting M4A to ensure that it is sustainable in the long term.

136. Long-term sustainability will be achieved through the application of the guiding principles of the project design and implementation (see Annex 2, Section II). IJAT will provide the building blocks of a shift in the agricultural sector from smallholder subsistence farming towards more commercialized production systems that are better integrated into national markets, regional and international trade networks, and neighboring communities and regions in Mali, and neighboring countries with similar contexts. The long-term sustainability of M4A is also ensured by its alignment with the principles of sustainable development, including environmental protection and sustainable technologies; and compatibility with existing government policies.

137. New knowledge generation and sharing through the organization of annual forums for all key actors (national agricultural extension services, local governments, farmers' associations, women's groups) to share and discuss the key technical learnings of the project and how these could be applied to address the challenges faced by smallholder farmers in Mali. The project will also support the development of a national knowledge management system that will provide an enhanced data environment and understanding of land use in Mali. This can support the creation of future climate change adaptation strategies.

138. IJAT invests in and promotes technologies and their supporting value chains that have been selected as appropriate for smallholder farmers, including those that are environmentally friendly and sustainable. These systems ensure long-term sustainability by increasing households' access to reliable energy and water resources; improving the quality of food production through better soil management practices; and enabling women's groups to expand their businesses further and develop women- and youth-inclusive business models, supporting the development of a more inclusive economy in Mali.

139. Local and beneficiary ownership at both the community and institutional levels is built into all project activities. The project will ensure that all decisions are made with the participation of beneficiaries within communities have been designed in collaboration with beneficiaries and will be informed by community stakeholder engagement. Community engagement will be conducted to collectively determine the distribution and fair use of all new technologies, equipment, and services provided under the project. This will include the development of a community planting; increasing beneficiary ownership and reducing risk of local conflict. Community Action Groups will be supported in their efforts to ensure that the benefits from the project are equitably distributed among asset owners. Similarly, in the support delivered to extension services, linkages between extension services and local communities will be strengthened through the establishment of community-based advisory committees.

140. Project activities will also contribute to the implementation of national policies on agriculture, environmental policies in general, and climate change adaptation strategies. These include the National Agricultural Policy (NAP), National Environment Policy (NEP) and National Climate Change Strategy (SNCC). The key components of climate policies include measures for climate change mitigation and adaptation; promoting sustainable development; and private sector engagement in climate change mitigation efforts. Various actions are outlined within these policies, including reforming, reorienting


---
**Page 56**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | p.59

of weather and climate information systems for agriculture, climate resilience in the agriculture and forestry sectors, agricultural diversification, and sustainable land management, among others.

141. IAAAT (Integrated Agro-Advisory Advisory and Training) and NAPAC (National Agricultural Productivity Action Plan) both collectively seek to increase the climate resilience of Mali's agricultural system and increase the adoption of low-carbon livelihoods. In so doing, they focus on a number of complementary activities that include: 1) the promotion of climate-resilient agricultural techniques and technologies (highlighted in NAPA, NDIC), the corresponding on-farm production of CSA crops/techniques (highlighted in NAPA, NDIC); 2) the development of agro-advisory services to farmers, which is a key strategy for generating an adaptive and mitigation shift in Mali's agricultural system. For example, IAAAT focuses on increasing the audience for climate information and early warning systems by: first, strengthening the capacity of agro-advisory services, the key educational service for smallholder farmers, and secondly by supporting this-in-service training through the development of a national network of extension agents; 3) NAPAC includes integrating existing work from the Malian government, NGOs and the GCF on climate information and early warning systems to broaden awareness and usage (highlighted in SNCC and NAPA).

### D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The expected performance against each of the project’s programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

#### 1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will support the development and implementation of adaptation actions that support climate-smart resilient sustainable agriculture and reduced GHG emissions by supporting to direct and indirect beneficiaries with access to 700,000 ha of land in Malawi. The project will reduce GHG emissions by 2,446,543 tCO2eq/yr. This project will support the development of low carbon, resilient and sustainable livelihoods amongst communities in Malawi who are increasingly climate vulnerable and food insecure. It will also support the development of a more resilient agricultural system that


---
**Page 60**
---

# Direct Beneficiaries

The estimated direct beneficiary population is 817,709 people (86,345 farming households) across 48 communes in the 5 targeted regions of the national population. Communities in 42 communes across 12 circles in the 5 targeted regions will receive locally tailored inputs based on their evolving needs and local context. These communities are expected to be more resilient to climate change, with a focus on adapting to climate change, combining an assessment of climate risk, exposure, and adaptive capacity. As outlined in annexes 2 and 3, these communities will benefit from the implementation of components 1, 2, and 3 that will increase the resilience of their farms to climate change. Beneficiary numbers were estimated based on a combination of census data, survey data, and other available statistical data and with further detail on beneficiary selection and targeting provided in Annexes 2.

### Indirect Beneficiaries

The total population of the project area is approximately 3 million people (15% of the national population) who will benefit from improved access to clean energy sources. The implementation of components 1, 2, and 3 will also further increase the capacity of national and local institutions to adapt to and mitigate climate change, resulting in long-term impacts.

## Mitigation Impact

- An estimated 95,286 ha (of Agroforestry) will be developed through components 1 and 2. Through increased crop diversification, smallholder farmers for cultivation of agroforestry, the production and distribution of agroforestry tree saplings (seeds and stem), planting and management practices and growing trees on their farms, this component is expected to reduce GHG emissions by approximately 30%.
- An estimated 218,561 ha of land will be converted from high carbon stock land use to low carbon stock land use. This will result in the mitigation of an additional 479,000 tCO2e over 15 years (solar pump = +3,512 tCO2e; biogas = -7,752 tCO2e).
- An estimated 64,755 farming households (75% of all farming households) will also benefit from reduced emissions through the implementation of component 3. This component is expected to result in the further mitigation of 946,320 tCO2e over 15 years.

For more detailed information on the impact of the project, please refer to Annex 22.


## D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

As described in the full project proposal, this project aims to be a catalyst for a broader shift in agriculture across Mali from the baseline state of monoculture production systems that are highly exposed to climate hazards and low-land use efficiency. The project will work with farmers to increase their capacity and resources to adopt low-carbon agricultural practices providing improved public goods and water security and lower emissions of the agricultural systems, with ongoing support provided by the government and other private sector.

### Potential scale up and replication

The project aims to contribute to a low-emission and climate-resilient development pathway in Mali by increasing the long-term capacity of smallholder communities and their surrounding environment to recognize and respond to climate risks, increasing sustainable use and low-carbon crop and livestock.

### D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page)

#### 168. The IAT will make direct and indirect contributions to Mali's efforts to meet the Sustainable Development Goals, particularly in addressing SDG13 'strengthen resilience and adaptability to climate-related disasters' and SDG2 'end hunger', as well as SDG5 'gender equality'. These goals are also key to meeting climate change" and SDG 2: "End hunger, achieve food security, improve nutrition, and promote sustainable agriculture". The IAT will contribute towards the following Sustainable Development Goals:

- **SDG1 (end poverty in all its forms everywhere)**
- **SDG5 (gender equality)** 
- **SDG8 (promote inclusive and sustainable economic growth, employment, and decent work for all)**
- **SDG2 (end hunger, achieve food security, improved nutrition, and promote sustainable agriculture)**
- **SDG15 (sustainably manage forests, combat desertification, halt and reverse land degradation, and halt biodiversity loss)**

#### 167. The project will contribute to the following by:

- **Co-benefit 1 Gender and social inclusion:** Increased gender equality and economic empowerment for women in Mali. This is achieved through a series of interventions targeting women's access to resources that reduce barriers to entrepreneurship with a focus on women accessing increased income from, delivering training to women on how to start their own businesses, and providing them with the necessary tools. The improved extension services targeted in output 1.1 will include a provision to increase the gender inclusivity of agricultural extension services. This will ensure that women are able to access information equally able to benefit from the new training (in contrast to their existing reduced access). In addition, the budgeted project activities under output 2.3 will provide women with increased access to land and time taken for households to collect wood for existing energy sources; a task often conducted by women. Taking into account that women are more likely than men to be responsible for household water collection, women have access to services and inputs currently more easily accessible by men, increasing the equality of access to resources. The project will also provide training on how to use solar pumps, improving increased gender equality and economic empowerment will also generate economic co-benefits by improving the productivity of smallholder farmers in Mali. This is achieved through a series of interventions: solar pumping system from Outcome 2 will improve women's access to water for household and agriculture use (Outcome 3). The project will provide training on how to use solar pumps, which are more productive than non-productive activities.

- **Co-benefit 2 Environment:** Improved soil conditions and water resources from landrace improvements. Outcomes 1 and 3 will all result by landrace farmers and together result in improved soil not only for the project but also for the wider community. This is achieved through a series of interventions: smallholder farmers during crop and livestock production (output 1.2), and the soil and water benefits provided to households from Outcome 4. The project will provide training on how to use solar pumps, enhance healthy ecosystem services and create public goods for the communities. Several recent studies have shown that landrace crops are more resilient than modern varieties in terms of their impacts on diversification of pollination, pest control, nutrient cycling, GHG emissions reductions, water replenishment, and soil health.

#### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page)

### Vulnerability of the country and specific vulnerable groups and economic and social development level of the region

- Mali is highly exposed to the impacts of climate change with GAIN ranking it as the 24th most vulnerable to climate change in Africa. The country has experienced a series of droughts, floods, and heat waves that have changed vulnerability levels from its erratic rainfall and rising temperatures resulting in a continuous cycle of droughts and floods. Mali is also one of the countries most affected by climate change with an estimated 7 million people in the 1980-2014 period and generated an economic cost of USD 140 million. In many locations, the effects of climate change are exacerbated by poverty, gender inequality, social exclusion, and discrimination which means are less likely to participate in and express their needs for integration in decision-making processes. The frequency of droughts has increased, heat waves and disease increase. Mali is forecast to generate losses of up to USD 300 million per year from 1980 onwards

## Absence of alternative sources of financing

### 172. **Absence of international development and climate finance documents**. Malí relies on climate finance support from international development and financial institutions. Despite the urgent need for climate funding for Mali, there is no evidence that any of these institutions have provided such funds to Mali.

### 173. **Limited domestic resources available for implementation**. Mali currently has no viable source of domestic financing to implement the IAAT, in part due to ongoing conflict and humanitarian crises. The government relies heavily on external funding from international donors, particularly from the private sector as well as from international donors. There are also prominent barriers to overall private sector investment in agriculture, including lack of access to credit for smallholder farmers, limited training for farmer operators, training in procedures for accessing climate finance, or participation in the development of climate action plans.

### 174. **Limited domestic resources available**. Mali had a deficit of large amount of financial resources to address the COVID-19 pandemic, which further reduced its ability to implement the IAAT.

### 175. **Limited domestic resources available for implementation**. Although development and climate funds are present in Mali there is insufficient support to close the gap between the national budget and the needs identified by the government. This has limited the availability of international donor funding. For example, after France withdrew its military operation in 2020, it also reduced its financial contribution from €15 million per year (2018) to €45 million per year (2019-2021).

## Strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

### 176. **Limited institutional capacity**. The government has prioritized by the national government, there is limited capacity to implement much of the government's policy and initiatives lagging behind international commitments. This is due in part to a lack of coordination between different levels of government (national, sub-national) and the implementation at both the national and sub-national levels. There is a need to improve coordination between the various actors involved in implementing the IAAT. The lack of capacity for adaptation and mitigation planning and programming in agriculture, there is a strong need to transfer global best practices on climate change adaptation and mitigation from international donors to local governments by providing inclusive and individual knowledge to support local capacities and decision-making approaches. The government has also prioritized by the national government, there is limited capacity to implement much of the government's policy and initiatives lagging behind international commitments.

### 177. **Limited institutional capacity**. The government has prioritized by the national government, there is limited capacity to implement much of the government's policy and initiatives lagging behind international commitments.


---
**Page 64**
---

In addition, implementation of local initiatives is limited by decision-making structures that rely on the male heads of households resulting in reduced accessibility for women, minorities, and youth.

Local communities have been found to be more resilient than previously thought in some regions of Mali in part derived from building of conflict[1]. Consequently, there is a need to both strengthen trust in formal and informal institutions and build capacity within these institutions. This will require the support of national and international institutions on social capital, kinship, and religious networks for authority with their support required to be able to mobilize resources at the local level.

### D. Consultation (maximum 100 words; maximum 1 page)

iJAT was developed and designed in collaboration with stakeholders from across all 5 regions of the project (Gas, Tombouctou, Segou, Koulikoro and Mopti), including farmers and farmer organizations, private sector representatives, government officials, local authorities, civil society organizations, and youth representatives. Project activities, outputs, outcomes, and impacts were evaluated and validated by the stakeholders through a series of workshops and meetings in each region to ensure that they are relevant and useful for implementation.

The project was submitted as part of Mali’s NDC to the GCF in 2021 endorsing the submission of this project idea. It has been designed in collaboration with the Malian government, in particular the Ministry of Environment and Sanitation (MENS), Ministry of Rural Development (MRD), and Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAL). The multi-stakeholder approach to project design, over 350 stakeholders were engaged to collaboratively design the project, including government ministries, private sector representatives, civil society organizations, farmers’ associations, youth representatives, and other key actors. These consultations have significantly shaped the project with further detail provided in Annexes 7: Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) and 8: Project Implementation Plan (PIP).

The Government of Mali has a high priority for the Government of Mali because of its high potential to contribute towards meeting national priorities for climate-resilient agricultural development as espoused in the NDC, Green Growth and Development Strategy (GGDS), National Strategic Framework on Food Security, National Food Security Strategy, the Strategic Framework for Economic Recovery and Sustainable Development in Mali (SFRSDM), the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS), the National Investment Plan (PIN), National Climate Change Policy (PCCD) and the National Climate Change Strategy (PNCC). The project also aligns with other national strategies such as the National Disaster Management of Disasters Risk, and the 2019 Climate Smart Agriculture Investment Plan. Together, these policies aim to improve food security, nutrition, access to water and sanitation, health, education, energy, and nutrition; fostering national and regional integration processes; and promotes intensive, diversified, and sustainable agriculture.

Furthermore, in its updated National Determined Contribution (NDC), Mali committed to reducing emissions from land use change and forest degradation by 2030. The project will contribute towards this goal by focusing on the reduction of projected business-as-usual emissions[2]. Priority actions in Mali’s NDC include agroforestry, sustainable cocoa production, and climate-smart agriculture.

In addition to the alignment with national agricultural and climate policies, the GCF-iJAT project is consistent with the GCF’s strategic objectives. The project will contribute towards the achievement of the objective that embodies the collective vision established by all pertinent stakeholders in Mali concerning climate adaptation and mitigation[3]. In line with the GCF country program in Mali, the project aims to enhance the accessibility of alternative energy sources for rural households; improve access to information on climate change, agriculture, health, and youth; enhance capacities across all levels, and facilitate collaboration, knowledge exchange, and experience sharing among stakeholders. The project will also contribute towards the achievement of the GCF’s strategy and credibility upon the project’s entry into the pipeline[4] and has strong institutional and collaborative arrangements in place with the Government of Mali and climate change actors.

The project will address the identified barriers and acknowledge the accuracy of the proposed actions to address these challenges. These solutions will play a key role in addressing the identified barriers.

The project will be implemented by Save the Children Mali and the AEDD. AEDD involvement will include technical assistance, capacity building, and facilitation of the steering committee which includes the Ministry of Environment and Sanitation, the Ministry of Rural Development (including MRD), and other relevant stakeholders.

## Accredited Entity

### Save the Children

Save the Children is the world's leading independent organization for children, with 39 national programs and 24,000 professionals working together to deliver programs in approximately 120 countries via a network of over 24,000 professionals. In 2018, Save the Children delivered programs worth over US $2.2 billion across 117 countries and territories. SCI is accredited by the GCF as an Accredited Entity (AE) for its work in Mali.

### SC International

SC International, the implementation structure for the Global Fund to Fight AIDS, Tuberculosis and Malaria (GFATM), has implemented over 300 resilience-related projects and programs valued at more than US $200 million, with explicit objectives to reduce poverty, improve health outcomes, increase access to education, promote gender equality and social and economic empowerment of women and youth and the amplification of the voices of the most marginalized.

### SC USA

SC USA is accredited by the GCF in November 2019 on behalf of the global SCI network. SC was chosen as an AE because it has a strong track record in climate change adaptation, including its focus on risk reduction and its robust fiduciary and compliance systems and processes. The AMA made effective use of the flexibility provided under the GCF’s new funding model to support partners at various stages of development.

### SCI Mali

SCI Mali is an implementing partner for Save the Children International (SCI), SC USA, has extensive experience designing, delivering, evaluating, and documenting approaches to community-based adaptation, including in Mali. Where SCI has been involved with communities, it has worked closely with them to understand their context and adapt their policy adoption options and needs, bringing a consultative approach to engaging a range of stakeholders.

### SCGUS

SCGUS is an AE for 39 national organizations that implement non-domestic operations through a single program delivery unit. Save the Children International (SCI). With a 2018 annual budget of $655 million, SCI has worked in over 120 countries and territories to improve children’s lives by working with governments, foundations, the private sector and major multi-lateral and bilateral institutions, including the U.S. Government. SCI has extensive experience in climate change adaptation, including its focus on risk reduction and social inclusion, and food security professionals offer expertise in climate risks assessment, prioritization and adaptation, as well as in nutrition, health, education, water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH), disaster management under climatic and economic shocks, community capacity strengthening, social and behavior change communication, and gender equality. SCI has a long history of working with governments and trusted partner to governments, NGOs, the private sector, other local actors, and institutions and regularly collaborates with them in the design and implementation of projects and programs that address development outcomes in the countries where we work. SCGUS serves as the lead Resilient Food Security activity in Mali, Niger, Malawi, Ethiopia, and Tanzania funded by USAID.

### SCI Mali

SCI Mali has been working in Mali in collaboration with the GoM since 1987 and supporting vulnerable children, youth, women, and their communities through implementing programs in food and health security, education, child protection, and social inclusion. The GCF project is one of a series of its current programs focus on food and nutrition security, health, natural resource management, and climate change adaptation. SCI Mali has implemented 102 projects in the country since 1987, including 36 development projects/programs and active development for a total portfolio of US $33 million.

### Development Projects

The GCF project is one of several projects that SCI Mali has been implementing in the Sikasso and Diéma districts. Kayes region funded by the CIDA (US$99,960); and KOCIA (US$700,000). A SAMS and a WASH component were added to the project in 2013. The GCF project is one of several projects that SCI Mali has been implementing in the Sikasso and Diéma districts.
