## D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 300 words; approximately 1 page)

The project's total cost is US$24,879,000 of which 78% (US$19,36 million) is proposed from the GCF grant, and 11% (US$5 million) is the co-financing component from USAID in (grant instalment), and 11% (US$2.5 million) co-financing from USAID in-kind over the project period.

### Financial structure

The project's proposed concessionally lent total is rationalized through the lack of alternative funding mechanisms for smallholder farmers, who are currently dependent on extension services provided by government extension, social and ecosystem services and so on). And the IAAT focuses on climate vulnerable beneficiaries who have limited access to credit and other financial services. The project will provide a new source of financing while being unable to increase their adaptive capacity. The project will contribute to improving the management of natural resources and increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers in the region, directly benefiting 730,862. Furthermore, the project will result in the planting of 19,285 ha of agroforestry trees, which will provide additional carbon sequestration benefits. The adoption of the IAAT support in private land will result in the reduction of 1,668,836 tCO2eq net through improved agronomy, a further 40% reduction from the baseline scenario. This is achieved by the project's focus on the adoption of low-carbon technologies (solar pumps and biodigesters) in its 5-year lifetime. The public sector will be able to replicate this model with the support of the private sector through an existing market, meaning that it is only through grant financing that the agroforestry component can be implemented.

The IAAT will provide a successful model for enhancing climate resilience and reducing emissions in targeted areas, offering opportunities for replication in other regions or Mali and countries with similar contexts. Based on the high financing needs and vulnerability to climate change in Mali, and the public


---
**Page 67**
---

nature of goods and services provided through the project, the IAT will act as a catalyst for transforming the agriculture and agrosector industries in Mali, generating economic benefits for smallholder farmers and creating new opportunities for them to access markets and improve their livelihoods. The Government of Mali or its' local communities to fund this transformation currently, therefore conceivably is requested through the Green Climate Fund (GCF) as a means of financing the project. This will be done in line with current and future climate change. For a detailed financial assessment of the key interventions in the project, please refer to Annex 3 Economic and Financial Analysis.

### Cost effectiveness of mitigation

193. The total emissions mitigated through IAT's activities are 2,448,543 tCO2e over the next 15 years (potential range: 2,076,000 to 2,821,000 tCO2e). This represents a reduction of 9.5% in total emissions from Mali and is funded by the GCF. The economic public value of the negative carbon balance (reduction of GHG emissions) is estimated at USD 34 million (potential range: USD 28 to 40 million), based on the average social price for one ton of CO2. Assuming for both additional production and GHG co-benefits, the economic public value of IAT's activities over the next 15 years is estimated at USD 63 million (range of the social value of carbon recommended for use by the World Bank Group in USD per t metric ton of CO2). This represents a return on investment of approximately 40% and an internal rate of return (IRR) of about 8% to 10% in real terms by 2036. On this basis, it is relevant to propose a simulation of economic analysis for the project's financial viability over its lifetime. The results of such an analysis are presented at USD 9.8 million. The analysis below now provides an EIRP of up to 59% and an ENPV of over USD 58.6 million, which is more than sufficient to ensure the financial viability of the project.

### Expected financial internal rate of return and long-term financial viability

194. The assessment of these three components demonstrates promising financial viability for the project over its lifetime. The average expected financial internal rate of return (EIRP) is 58% (IRR 130%, IRR NPV USD 146), and Shura Better 68% (IRR and NPV USD 52). A use of solar irrigation – Margin (IRR 194%, and NPV USD 7.5), and a use of solar irrigation – Marginal (IRR 300%, and NPV USD 10) are also presented in the table. The average expected financial internal rate of return (EIRP) is 68% (IRR 229%, and NPV USD 65). The average expected financial internal rate of return (EIRP) is 73% (IRR 410%, and NPV USD 10.5). These results highlight the strong financial returns of the various value chains within the project, before considering any potential co-benefits or other externalities. The analysis also provides a comparison with the return profile for the farmers after project support, showing the competing financial case for delivering the improved technologies and services to smallholder farmers in Mali. The results presented below present a strong return on investment of 58% (IRR 130%, IRR NPV USD 146), and Shura Better 68% (IRR and NPV USD 52). The analysis also provides a comparison with the return profile for the farmers after project support, showing the competing financial case for delivering the improved technologies and services to smallholder farmers in Mali. The results presented below present a strong return on investment of 73% (IRR 410%, IRR NPV USD 10.5). Additionally, as shown by the sensitivity analysis, the project's design presents a strong return on investment for the farmers over its lifetime and across all levels of yield and crop risk. The results presented below present a strong return on investment of 73% (IRR 410%, IRR NPV USD 10.5). Additionally, as shown by the sensitivity analysis, the project's design presents a strong range in the potential return for farmers, with uncertainties in climate as well as political and security context resulting in a wide range of potential return scenarios.

### Expected economic cost of mitigation

196. This economic analysis is based on cash flow analysis with a project budget of USD 43.7 million and Economic return of agriculture productions as benefit. The project budget of USD 43.7 million is divided into three components: (i) IAT's activities, (ii) the cost of mitigation, and (iii) the co-benefits (18% in year 5). It results in an Economic Net Present Value (ENPV) of USD 4.4 million and an Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRP) of up to 59%, which is more than sufficient to ensure the economic value of GHG mitigated by the project.

### Application of best practices and degree of innovation

197. IAT will strongly rely on innovation and technology to drive change across the agricultural landscape, increasing productivity and profitability for smallholder farmers in Mali. The project will support best practices and extension services will be trained and encouraged to use digital platforms to extend their reach. In addition, the project will provide training and capacity building for local service providers and other players and enablers of the value chain, including service providers, processors, financial institutions, input suppliers, and traders. The project will also support the development of a land database and authorities to effectively map and classify public assets. A land use database will also be created, with further information on soil quality, water availability, and other factors that are relevant for farmers, implying more accurate and specifically tailored advisory services for smallholder farmers in Mali, and setting up a system for the registration of land rights.

## E.6 Project programme activities and deliverables

### Activities
- Build technical capacity and reach of extension services on CSA and agroforestry techniques

### Description
The project seeks to improve and expand extension services to support the transfer of knowledge and skills from larger farmers to smallholder farmers, including the adoption of best practices that address climate risks and deliver benefits for both soil fertility management and water resources. It will involve local organizations and networks in the design, implementation, and evaluation of these activities with a view to enhancing the effectiveness and accessibility of extension services on CSA and agroforestry among smallholder farmers.

### Sub-activities
1.1.1 Develop an updated curriculum for public and private extension service providers at the district level to tailor CSA and agroforestry techniques.
   - Benefits and implementation methods of 22 CSA practices in the project area (Bong, Kourouba, Segu and Toumodiéké) through the circle-level workshops.

### Deliverables
- Training program developed on climate risks and impacts of climate change.
- Analysis of existing data to provide advisory services for farmers and improved decision-making.
- Analysis of existing data at the district level to tailor CSA and agroforestry curriculum development.
- Importance of analysis and interpretation of data in providing tailored advisory services to farmers and stakeholders, including local NGOs and stakeholders in the workshops on traditional knowledge holders.

Activity 1.3.1 Develop land use mapping at the regional level

This activity aims to facilitate the adoption and effective utilization of regional-level land use planning in support of agricultural intensification and agroforestry, and improve the implementation of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) interventions.

By assisting regional authorities in mapping and utilizing land-use data for CSA activities across regions, it seeks to enhance their understanding of land resources and promote more sustainable land management at a regional level. Additionally, a land-use database will be established to provide information on land tenure systems, services regarding land characteristics, allowing farmers to make informed decisions about suitable crops and techniques for enhancing productivity and resilience.

The data collected from this activity will also be shared with national authorities to promote the adoption of CSA interventions across regions.

Activity 1.3.2 Plant agroforestry trees in community and state-owned lands

This activity seeks to promote enhanced adoption of agroforestry practices, as defined by FAO (1985) as agroforestry, targeting specific communities in Senegal and Gambia.

The capacities of several stakeholders will be strengthened through the implementation of this activity (e.g., nurseries for production and distribution of trees, training of local communities for the management of trees and associated resources). The successful establishment of these planted areas by communities will be critical to their long-term sustainability. This will be implemented in partnership with CACs - Alburka.

# E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (500 words, approximately 1 page)

## 198. The Monitoring and Evaluation Plan of the GCF IAT is designed to closely align with GCF’s integrated results management framework (IRMF) for existing projects/programs, monitoring and evaluating core indicators such as reduced emissions, increased resilience, and systemic change, which the IAT will ultimately aim to achieve.

## 198. The M&E arrangements are based on the IRMF and will be developed in close collaboration with the Project Management Unit (PMU) of the Project Management Unit. However, the M&E arrangements will be further developed at the onset of project implementation by the project M&E staff, to better define the roles and responsibilities for data collection and management, project components’ impact chains, information flows and reporting systems.

### Monitoring

## 198. The project monitoring plan includes two levels of evaluation: GCF level performance and project level performance. The IAT M&E team will conduct surveys to establish baseline levels for GCF core indicators, project-level results, and natural-resource-related factors impacting food security and livelihoods. The monitoring process will consider diverse population segments and aim to capture effects based on gender, age, household head status.


---
**Page 99**
---

and vulnerability. The monitoring structure allows flexibility for unforeseen incidents, with regular monitoring of project indicators, objective progress reporting, and alignment of project components with higher-level results and GCF goals.

200. The project will use a variety of tools to collect data on project performance and impact. Surveys and questionnaires will enable broad engagement with stakeholders, encompassing farmers, consumers, and community representatives, to gather valuable insights and accurately measure the project’s progress toward its objectives. Data collection methods include direct interviews, focus group discussions, and surveys through field visits and meetings, to ensure meticulous tracking of progress, such as land use changes and tree plantations, while pre- and post-interview evaluations will be conducted with farmers and consumers to assess their perceptions on the project’s impact on food security, livelihoods, and emissions reduction, avoidance, or sequestration. The data collected through these varied sources will contribute to the development of a comprehensive project monitoring plan.

201. The project team is committed to regular monitoring of indicators outlined in the project results framework, providing quarterly and annual assessments to ensure alignment with GCF goals and objectives. These evaluations will be conducted by the project management unit (PMU) and reviewed by the AEI and Executive Entities (Save the Children Email). During these reviews, the PMU will provide a detailed report on progress against indicators, while the AEI and Executive Entities will evaluate the project’s performance using the Project Monitoring Plan. The review process is structured to ensure consistency in monitoring across baseline, midterm, and final evaluations, involving the AE.

202. The evaluation plan for the IATI project will ensure consistent methodology and quality across baseline, midterm, and final evaluations, involving the AE. Executive Entities (Save the Children Email) will provide a detailed report on progress against indicators, while the PMU will evaluate the project’s performance using the Project Monitoring Plan.

Evaluation

203. The evaluation plan for the IATI project will ensure consistent methodology and quality across baseline, midterm, and final evaluations, involving the AE. Executive Entities (Save the Children Email) will provide a detailed report on progress against indicators, while the PMU will evaluate the project’s performance using the Project Monitoring Plan.

204. Performance evaluations will occur at baseline, midterm, and endline, while impact evaluations will occur at baseline and endline, involving quantitative data collection from both direct and indirect project beneficiaries. The sample design will be carefully designed to encompass key subgroups of interest, such as women, youth, and vulnerable groups.

205. Save the Children will engage an independent consultant firm for the baseline, midterm, and final performance evaluations, as well as the impact evaluation at endline. This consultant will provide a detailed report on progress against indicators, while the PMU will evaluate the project’s performance using the Project Monitoring Plan.

206. # RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures (pages 3-5)

The IAAT will be implemented in 12 across five regions. These circles were selected based on their vulnerability to conflict, limited access to markets, and the presence of transhumance corridors. Additionally, there is little to no conflict or competition between agriculturalists and agropastoralists in these areas, making them ideal for implementing the initiative.

### Selected Risk Factor

| Category | Probability (Impact) (before mitigation measures) | Probability (Impact) (after mitigation measures) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low |

#### Description
Active conflict reduces the ability to successfully implement the project. All regions of Mali are vulnerable to conflict, either directly or through an increase in internally displaced people. The conflict has previously impacted the ability to access specific areas of Mali, resulting in the closure of markets and limited access for humanitarian actors. For the IAAT, this could result in reduced access to project locations, a reduced pool of financial institutions to work with, and ultimately a potential reduction in the number of communities involved in the implementation of the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The project will mitigate the risk of conflict by:
- Minimizing exposure to conflict
- Minimizing the risk of infringing on local security concerns

#### Minimizing Exposure to Conflict
- Criminal and civil security risk have not been selected for this project (based on the assessment by SQ2 Mail security expert).
- Project implementation will also be designed to require minimal travel for the implementation team, reducing the risk of conflict exposure due to travel.

#### Minimizing Risk of Infringing on Local Security Concerns
- A local (community-level) security and political economy analysis will be undertaken to identify and then limit the potential for project activities to infringe upon local security concerns. This will be done under the lens of "no harm" and "conflict sensitivity" to ensure all activities are adapted to local contexts.
- To further reduce the risk, a conflict-sensitive approach will be used in the implementation phase. This approach as the Albaraka project which is: "a community-led participatory process, which involved community members in the planning of the project."
- Utilisation of feedback mechanisms and community feedback to allow any tensions arising to be swiftly resolved.
- Ensuring that all project activities are adapted appropriately.

#### During the implementation phase, contingency plans will be developed to identify the activities that can occur or be halted during different levels of conflict. These will be updated as appropriate. The IAAT will use online methods of project activity delivery.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability (Impact) (before mitigation measures) | Probability (Impact) (after mitigation measures) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low |

#### Description
Limited engagement or interest in project activities from local communities leads to non-realization of project outputs. In order for the project outputs to be achieved, smallholder farmers need to voluntarily implement new CSA practices. Limited engagement or interest can lead to a lack of uptake of these practices by smallholder farmers. For example, reducing the adoption rate of CSA techniques in Component 1 or limited use of the new technologies such as drip irrigation in Component 3 underpinned project outputs.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)


---
**Page 101**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | pg: [9]

- **Smallholder farmer engagement has been key to design the project and CSA practices selected for promotion by the project have been prioritised based on their suitability for smallholder farmers in the project locations and their ability to be scaled up.**
  - Smallholder farmers cite one of the biggest barriers to adopting existing CSA practices is the lack of technical support, training, and extension services. The project will address this barrier by increasing capacity through the creation of a new CSA curriculum for delivery and delivered by extension agents.
- **Smallholder farmers show reduced interest in CSA practices after project implementation closes.**
  - To ensure it is supported beyond the project period, e.g., through government level support, the project will engage interested groups.
    - The project will support interest in the adoption of new technologies that can add and support the economic return of CSA practices by supporting the relevant sale of the new goods in component 2. and for training biodigester and irrigation system users about the additional income opportunities from the technologies.

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability/Impact (before mitigation measures) | Probability/Impact (after mitigation measures) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description
Some beneficiaries farmers revert to pre-project (climate-vulnerable) farming practices and/or stop using new technologies after project implementation.

Smallholder farmers show reduced interest in CSA practices after project implementation closes.
- Bodigester and solar irrigation systems are not used by farmers due to challenges with operations and maintenance after the project period.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- This risk, thoroughly considered during design project, is mitigated by ensuring that project promoted CSA practices are financially sustainable through a public-private partnership with the access to more reliable project support value chain, so that farmers will be incentivized to continue using these practices beyond project implementation.
- In addition, the new extension service curriculum created during the project will continue to be utilized after the project period. This will ensure that there is a continued interest in CSA practices and also increase the adoption of CSA practices after the project implementation period.
- For the biodigester and solar irrigation systems, a business model will be built out in project communities to ensure that there are accessible service providers after the implementation period.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability/Impact (before mitigation measures) | Probability/Impact (after mitigation measures) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Low |

### Description
Limited interest from and high administrative burden on the government.
- Limited implementation of commitments by political decision-makers in the implementation of the project. Continuous lack of commitment from the government to support CSA practices results in a high and potentially administratively burdensome for the government organisations involved in the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- AEEO and the Department of Agriculture actively participate with the project with co-financing and this project largely works with local organizations.
  - A high level of commitment from the government to support CSA practices, building procurement, and delivery of technologies will be carried out by third party on a competitive basis.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability/Impact (before mitigation measures) | Probability/Impact (after mitigation measures) |
| --- | --- | --- |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | Page: 92

## Governance
**Level:** Low

### Description
Political instability or elections result in a change of central authorities during the implementation of the project. Although delayed vs 2022, presidential elections are scheduled to be held during project implementation (currently Fall 2023). This could lead to changes in the composition of the government implementing entity. Alternatively, a major internal government reshuffle could lead to a change of key stakeholders.

- The risk that new officials and/or new members of the AEDG will not support the implementation of the project will be mitigated through information sessions to update any new stakeholders on the project interventions and subsequent engagement with them.
- Additionally, the locally led, commune and circle-level implementation plans and agreements will be prioritized so as to ensure that the local government is fully aware of the Project. This will establish a permanent and continuous work agenda regardless of the national government.

## Selected Risk Factor 6
### Category: ML/CT
**Probability/Impact (before mitigation measures):** Low
**Probability/Impact (after mitigation measures):** Low

### Description
Risk of project funds being used for money laundering, terrorist financing, prohibited practices

### Mitigation Measures
- Completed assessment of the financial management and capabilities of the governmental implementing entity.
- AEDG (Annex 20: AMI/KYC).
- Set up a dedicated unit within the government to specialize in the IAT Project, including procedures for verifying financial transactions and annual audits of implementing partners.
- Include anti-money laundering measures in all agreements to be signed between SCI and the government.
- Actively monitor and evaluate the effectiveness of anti-money laundering measures in place in order to make adjustments where necessary and continuously improve anti-money laundering practices.
- Provide training on money laundering for all relevant personnel.
- SCI Mali uses compliance monitoring and verification measures for procurement process, service providers and other partners as part of its anti-corruption strategy, including due diligence procedures.

## Selected Risk Factor 7
### Category: Other
**Probability/Impact (before mitigation measures):** Low
**Probability/Impact (after mitigation measures):** Low

### Description
Climate hazard events (such as prolonged extreme drought or flood), and/or macro-economic events (such as higher or volatile market prices for agriculture inputs in international markets or tightened financing conditions from the banks due to global financial conditions, etc.)

### Mitigation Measures
- The project is designed to operate within the context of recurrent droughts or floods. The occurrence of such events will not have a significant impact on the government implementing entity but will also strengthen support for the role of the project in mitigating such risks in the long term.
- Project beneficiaries are provided with climate risk management tools, such as early warning systems to improve soil quality and enhanced information and early warning systems will reduce the impact of droughts.

# G. CP Policies and Standards

## G.1. Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 207. Eight performance standards of the GCF Environmental and Social Policy were evaluated during the project preparation phase to ensure that the project complies with these standards in all aspects except for No. 6 on land use.

The project is assessed as low risk due to small-scale infrastructure which may create short-term disturbances to the environment, but no significant environmental impacts are expected from this project. The assessment is limited to no significant impacts and will be readily mitigated through appropriate measures as reflected in the ESMS. In addition, the results of the Environmental and Social Screening (ES) conducted during the assessments and consultations, it has been confirmed that there are no transboundary concerns interacting with other projects or activities in the region. The project is also assessed to have low risk for social impacts initiated or utilized by Indigenous Peoples. Therefore, the IAT project activities will not impact transhumance routes and cultural sites of indigenous peoples.

### 208. As part of their AE status, SCA implements all programs in accordance with their own Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) policy. The ESA is a comprehensive risk assessment tool that includes an environmental categorization table within the SCSA has been tailored to this project and includes expanded aspects of the GCF ES Policy. The SCA’s ESA process will be used as part of the project preparation phase, and it will further developed continue to capture all potential impacts that are outside the Category C rating. The GCF ES policy is also aligned with the SCA’s own policies.

### 209. SCA has zero tolerance for any abuse and exploitation committed by representatives against adults or children in their programs. ## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 218.
Women in Mali play a pivotal role in the agricultural sector, environmental management, and biodiversity conservation. They are responsible for food production, including the cultivation of staple crops such as millet, sorghum, cowpeas, and groundnuts. Women also engage in activities like animal husbandry, fish farming, and poultry rearing. Despite their significant contributions to food security and economic development, women face numerous challenges related to access to resources, land ownership, credit facilities, technology, underdeveloped markets, and the disproportionate impacts of climate change. Despite the progress made by the Government of Mali (GoM) in promoting gender equality through various policies focused on removing barriers for women and youth still outside formal education, traditional social structures influenced by religion and culture continue to hinder women's access to resources and opportunities for empowerment.

### 219.
A gender analysis conducted in Mali reveals a range of challenges and disparities faced by women, highlighting the need for gender equality and empowerment. Women’s illiteracy, limited access to climate change information, and lack of decision-making power exacerbate their vulnerability. For instance, while men dominate decision-making positions, women are relegated to roles that do not provide them with economic benefits or opportunities for advancement. Women also face significant limitations in accessing land, resources, and agricultural equipment, curtailting their productivity and economic empowerment. The gender analysis underscores the need for targeted interventions aimed at empowering women by providing them with access to information, education, training, credit facilities, and technology. These measures can help women gain control over household assets and increase their income-generating activities. Women must have access to benefits provided by governments and other organizations and income generated by the households they manage. Empowering women is crucial for achieving gender equality in Mali.

### 220.
The gender assessment conducted in Mali reveals a range of challenges and disparities faced by women, highlighting the need for gender equality and empowerment. Women’s illiteracy, limited access to climate change information, and lack of decision-making power exacerbate their vulnerability. For instance, while men dominate decision-making positions, women are relegated to roles that do not provide them with economic benefits or opportunities for advancement. Women also face significant limitations in accessing land, resources, and agricultural equipment, curtailting their productivity and economic empowerment. The gender analysis underscores the need for targeted interventions aimed at empowering women by providing them with access to information, education, training, credit facilities, and technology. These measures can help women gain control over household assets and increase their income-generating activities. Women must have access to benefits provided by governments and other organizations and income generated by the households they manage. Empowering women is crucial for achieving gender equality in Mali.


---
**Page 105**
---

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | p.95**

221. Women are also involved in agricultural production downstream. They are present to varying degrees throughout the value chain of food and cash crops, including as producers, processors, traders, or workers producing. However, they are not paid, nor do they control the income from this production. In addition to requiring women’s labor for subsistence agriculture, women also produce a significant amount of food, enabling them to generate their own income, which they manage and control, even if they have to inform the family about it. Women’s access to land is limited in many countries, and they are often not allowed to control all income linked to agricultural production.

222. Women are also negatively affected by climate change impacts, particularly on women and girls in Mali. Women and girls are the most negatively affected due to their traditional responsibility for water collection. Repeated droughts have led to a decline in food production, which has had negative effects on women’s access to economic activities, school, and participation in local organizations. The energy supply burden is also directly increased by climate change impacts such as droughts and floods. Women are the most affected because it mainly affects women because they are usually responsible for providing energy for the households and must spend a lot of time collecting firewood or charcoal. In addition to this, women and girls are negatively impacted through flooding (losses in crops, livestock, and agricultural waste). In addition, women and girls were impacted much by the severe droughts and floods through loss of income from their small-scale enterprises and livelihoods.

223. Promoting women’s empowerment and addressing gender disparities in Mali is essential for their active participation in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes that determine how resources will be used to adapt to the impacts of climate change. This ensures women’s access to resources, increasing their representation in decision-making positions, and improving their ability to influence policy decisions. The government has taken steps to bridge these gaps, including recognizing specific roles within households and communities to drive positive changes such as land tenure and natural resource management. Additionally, initiatives like improved access to agricultural inputs and training for women can help increase their productivity.

Benefiting from innovation and private sector engagement will empower women through activities such as processing and marketing of agricultural products. This will also provide them with the necessary resources needed in their work. Women’s empowerment requires providing them with necessary resources, land access, training, and resilience building to ensure that they are able to adapt to climate change impacts.

224. The IAT aims to bring a meaningful contribution to the effective creation of opportunities for women through increased access to resources and decision-making power in Mali. This will be achieved by empowering women, emphasizing specific services for women’s empowerment, especially through support to enhance access to credit, land tenure, and training. Women are also encouraged to participate more actively in generating activities. Equal representation of women and men is targeted in all capacity building and awareness-raising activities related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

The Climate Action Coalition (CAC) will focus on increasing the participation of women and equal contribution in developing CACs. The IAT will support the development of a Women’s Empowerment Strategy, which will be dedicated specifically to women and 3,750 women entrepreneurs will be supported to ensure empowerment in their respective communities. This will be achieved through capacity building, awareness raising, and action plans acting towards CACs and adaptation and mitigation planning at the commune level. Furthermore, private sector collaboration with women’s cooperatives will be supported to integrate women (at least 60% men and women) in their service offerings.

| Financial structure: |
The project's financial structure draws lessons learned from SCI Mail's decades-long experience of implementing projects in the region. The structure has been designed to be simple, effective and efficient. As described above, funds will flow from WCA to SCI Mail, then will be managed through a single account at the national level. This allows for greater flexibility in managing the circle and commune levels; all activities can be carried out in conjunction with government partners (AEDO, local communities, etc.) and other stakeholders. The structure also ensures that SCI Mail achieves its outcomes. This structure will allow the project to balance support to national and subnational levels of government, while ensuring that SCI Mail is able to deliver on its mandate for improved service delivery, while minimizing potential financial and procurement risks. While implementation in close partnership with the government partners will be key, SCI Mail will also engage with other stakeholders such as community members and the project steering committee.

| Reporting and procurement cycle: |
All goods and services to implement IATF activities will be procured according to
SCI Global Procurement Policy, GCF policy, applicable Mal law, sustainability, and value for Money principles. The procurement process is designed to ensure that SCI Mail complies with all relevant laws and regulations. SCI Mail strives to meet and remain compliant with the highest ethical, legal, and professional standards. SCI Mail ensures that its procurement processes are transparent and fair. In addition, SCI Mail will adopt a risk management and procurement approach following SCA's approved processes to which they were accredited and certified in 2015. The procurement process is described below. For more information on the procurement selection process is provided in Annex 10 (Procurement Plan). At all procurements activities will be incurred, SCI Mail will ensure that it complies with the relevant laws and regulations. In addition, SCI Mail will manage its own procurement cycle to ensure that it meets the requirements of the project. SCI Mail will manage procurement for co-funding activities based on its own procurement policy and not liable for GCF's procurement policy.

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |

| **The Intensification of Agriculture and Agroforestry Techniques (IAAT) project targets the agriculture sector, critical for Mali’s food security and livelihoods in conflict-affected regions impacted by conflict and climate change. The accredited entity, Save the Children Australia, is well positioned to implement this project and address urgent climate challenges, given its strong experience in implementing agricultural climate projects in conflict-affected countries with solid conflict-sensitive analysis.** | There is a risk of active conflict upholder affecting public service delivery, business activities, and humanitarian operations leading to reduced access to project locations and limited availability of extension services, as well as delays in implementation and adaptation of the project. ## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

1. Mali is highly vulnerable to climate change, ranking 13th in vulnerability and 23rd in readiness for climate change adaptation and mitigation out of 192 countries according to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative country index, with significant impacts from rising temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and droughts up to 500,000 people annually. The socioeconomic context in Mali, as a least developed country with high poverty rates (48% of population), low levels of education, and one of the fastest-growing young populations, further increases its vulnerability to climate hazards.
2. Large-scale deforestation for fuelwood collection, combined with poor land management and contributing 36 per cent of gross domestic product in particularity threatened, with projected decreases in rainfall by up to 40% by 2050, increasing the risk of desertification and associated stressors, including heat and reduced fodder availability for livestock, exacerbate food insecurity, with severe food shortages impacting up to 24 per cent of the population and malnutrition rates reaching as high as 38.7%.

### The goal of the Intensification of Agriculture and Agroforestry Techniques (IAAT) project is to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture and increase soil organic carbon (SOC), while reducing methane (CH₄) and nitrous oxide (N₂O) emissions, and increasing resilience against climate change.

1. The goal of the Intensification of Agriculture and Agroforestry Techniques (IAAT) project is to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in close collaboration with the Government of Mali, civil society, the private sector and communities by increasing capacity and providing support for climate-informed decisions on land use, soil management, crop selection, livestock management, water use, and water insecurity.
2. The focus will be on improving extension services for climate-smart agriculture (CSA) adoption; supporting the development of CSA and agroforestry value chains; reducing GHG emissions from agricultural systems; and increasing institutional capacity and knowledge for climate change adaptation and mitigation in Mali. Specifically, the project will support vulnerable circles covering 48 communes were selected as target areas on the basis of national and regional vulnerability assessments.
3. In targeting Mali, a country highly vulnerable to the impacts of drought and climate change, the IAAT project holds significant strategic value. The AE, Save the Children Australia, is well positioned to lead this initiative, leveraging its strong track record of implementing projects in Mali that have had positive impacts on communities and livelihoods. Specifically, the project focuses on the agriculture sector, which is critical for food security and livelihoods in Mali; therefore, it will contribute significantly to reducing vulnerability to climate change using an assessment based on climate risk, exposure and adaptive capacity.
4. The proposed funding for the project is around USD 83.7 million, utilizing grant instruments from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and co-financing from the Albarka Project Co-Financing of USD 10 million will be made, consisting of USD 5 million by the Environment and Sustainable Development Agency (EDES) of the Government of Mali and USD 5 million by the


---
**Page 111**
---

# United States Agency for International Development through its Ahbarka project (USD $60 million grant) on project on resilience and food security in Gao, Mopti and Tombouctou

## Component-by-component analysis

The project is structured into three components:

### Component 1: Improving extension services and increasing on-farm CSA adoption
Activities under this component include:
- Building the technical capacity of agro-advisory services.
- The key educational service for smallholder farmers to adopt CSA techniques.

### Component 2: Supporting the development of CSA and agrifoodery value chains (total cost: USD $10,718.13; GEF cost: USD $698.58 in grants)
Activities under this component include:
- The creation of inclusive private sector value chains for key CSA/agrofoodery crops and technologies.
- Enhancing capabilities and connectivity of youth and women CSA/agrofoodery enterprises.
- Installing and supporting the use of biodigesters systems and solar irrigation systems among smallholder farmers.

### Component 3: Increasing institutional capacity and knowledge (total cost: USD $2,768.69; GEF cost: USD $104.57 in grants)
This component aims to strengthen the institutional capacities of government entities at the local, regional and national level, as well as communities through the Community Action Clubs system. It also focuses on improving policies and procedures.

10. Activities include (i) strengthening institutional capacity in localized climate change adaptation and mitigation planning (ii) building the technical capacity of the Mali Climate Fund in prioritizing and targeting investment in CSA and agroforestry; and (iii) Strengthening national databases for CSA and agroforestry.

Monitorable indicators: current cost: USD 2,746,294; GCF cost: 1,716,204

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

- Mali faces heightened vulnerability to climate change owing to frequent climate hazards, conflicts over water resources, and a fragile economic base. In particular, the agriculture sector, which is a critical industry for the country for food security and livelihood, faces severe challenges due to drought and floods, impacting the overall economy. Mali requires urgent mitigation and adaptation support to address environmental, social and economic vulnerabilities.

### 3.5 Country ownership

- No objection letters have been issued by the Government of Mali.
- The national designated authority actively participated in major consultations with GCF and the project team. This was done through a joint meeting between the national designated authority, in addition to over 350 other stakeholders from across all regions of the project, including farmers and farmer organizations, private sector businesses, local and international NGOs, government agencies, and representatives of civil society and indigenous representatives.
- The project is fully aligned with a high priority of the Government of Mali as outlined in the nationally determined contribution, the Growth and Poverty Reduction Strategic Framework 2012 to 2017, and other laws and strategic documents.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

- Scale: N/A
- The project incorporates an innovative Pay-As-You-Go financing mechanism for solar irrigation systems, reducing upfront costs for farmers. The alignment of repayment schedules with seasonal cash flows ensures that the system is affordable to smallholder farmers.
- Complementary measures, such as financial literacy training and partnerships with microfinance institutions, ensure that farmers have access and can manage credit effectively.
- The adaptation budget (USD 96 per direct beneficiary) and USD 12.2 per indirect beneficiary represents a net cost of USD 3,443/CO2 eq., equating to high efficiency competitive within the GCF portfolio for both mitigation projects and benchmarks for adaptation in agriculture. This is due to the fact that the project has been designed to address the financial constraints of the implementation context in Mali.
- An analysis of the project shows that it is economically viable, with a net present value (NPV) of USD 104 million and an economic internal rate of return of 12 per cent without valuing the economic value of GHG emissions mitigated by the project. If the economic public value of the negative carbon balance were to be included in the analysis, this would further increase consideration.
- When using USD 30 as the social price for 1 tCO2 eq., the economic net present value reaches USD 458 million and an internal rate of return of 17 per cent, reflecting robust economic viability and societal value.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The project is categorized as C for environmental and social impacts owing to minimal environmental and social risks. The categorization is in accordance with the GCF Environmental and Social Policy (ESP) and the GCF Project Preparation Document (PPD). The activities primarily emanate from the small-scale installations under component 2, involving rainwater harvesting systems, water storage tanks, and irrigation systems. The project will support the following activities to be supported by the project include awareness-raising and capacity-building on CSA and agroforestry practices, soil conservation, and planting agroforestry trees on community-owned State-owned lands. The project will utilize best practices for the use of genetically modified organisms, and use minimum soil disturbance and loss technologies and practices. The project will also ensure that the livelihoods and well-being of people and the activities within private lands will be voluntary in nature. The identified environmental and social risks are low to medium, and the project will implement mitigation actions as outlined in the environmental and social assessment and residual risk management plan. This categorization is based on a review of the potential environmental and social risks and impacts. The plan has an exclusions list that would serve criteria for the exclusion of areas from the project area and the project’s final categorization. The conflict sensitivity assessment prepared by the AE reflects that low-risk areas for conflict have been selected. Nevertheless, the conflict situation in the target areas will continue to be monitored through regular meetings with key stakeholders and representatives of stakeholders. The project interventions, along with continuous community engagement, are expected to contribute positively towards social cohesion within communities and between communities.

The project’s environmental and social co-benefits include improved soil conditions from reduced erosion and increased water storage usage; GHG emission reductions; and increased gender equality and women empowerment.


#### Project Implementation Plan (PIP)

A Project Implementation Plan (PIP), including an Environmental and Social Safeguards Specialist, will be developed in close collaboration with the project team on a rolling basis. The Project Steering Committee and the AE will provide oversight functions. Stakeholder consultations will include representatives of government agencies, local communities and unions, community leaders, youth and women, community committees and local farmers in the targeted areas; indigenous peoples’ organizations; national civil society organizations and civil society organizations, international civil society organizations, academia, private sector representatives, and regional and national government agencies. Working with communities and other stakeholders will be critical to ensuring that project activities are accepted by the communities and ensuring acceptance, ownership and sustainability, and in strengthening social cohesion through community engagement and participation. A grievance redress mechanism will be implemented during implementation and an activity-level grievance redress mechanism will be


---
**Page 115**
---

### Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore, conforms to the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy. The gender assessment highlights significant barriers to gender equality in Mali and the agriculture and agroforestry sector. These include: women's limited access to land and other productive resources; their central and strategic gender needs and interests. Among others, the AE found that some of these challenges are exacerbated by the following factors: women's lower level of education than men across sex; literacy levels particularly in women; women's lack of access to sources of information on climate change adaptation and mitigation; limited knowledge about climate change adaptation; inadequate knowledge on sustainable management and use of natural resources; low representation of women in conflict prevention and management frameworks and mechanisms.

## 4.3 Risks

### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)
- **Political risks**: Mali is rated Ca2 with a stable outlook by Moody's. Based on Moody’s latest report, the country is highly susceptible to event risk which may have impacts on public service delivery, business activity and private sector operations. The project will need to access government services such as extension services, and financial institutions and private companies to work with, with delays in payments or other disruptions being possible.
- **Operational risks**: Some beneficiaries revert from climate-resilient farming practices they have adopted due to lack of access to finance/replicable income for adoption of solar irrigation, thus resulting in reduced sustainability and paradigm shift. The AE identified the risk and outlined mitigation measures in the funding proposal as an acceptable level of residual risk.
- **GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)**: In the case of approval, the impact of this proposed project on GCF’s overall portfolio will be low. This is reflected in the risk appetite statement in terms of results area, single proposal or A1 concentration.

### Compliance risks (low risk)
- Many of the project activities that focus on the improvement of institutional and financial management systems are considered low risk, including technical assistance/capacity-building and enabling the development of CSA and agroforestry value chains. The AE has assessed the potential for corruption and other compliance risks under infrastructure activities, such as the installation of solar irrigation and biodigestion systems, present at a low level due to the nature of these projects and their implementation risks.
- Preventive measures under the project include the following:
  - The AE has assessed the money-laundering and financing crime risk as low on the basis of the project’s activities. The financial control mechanisms specific to the IAAT project, which includes financial transaction verification procedures and annual audits, are in place to mitigate this risk.


---
**Page 117**
---

processing. Moreover, the AE will also monitor and evaluate anti-money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism control effectiveness on an annual basis.

(ii) SCI Mail and Save the Children Federation, Inc., will co-execute as executing entities in collaboration with an implementing entity, MAI’s EDA. The capacity assessment of AEDD will be used to determine the level of support required for SCI Mail and Save the Children Federation, Inc.’s anti-money-laundering and countering the financing of terrorism management meets the requirements.

(iii) On the basis of the relatively low inherent money-laundering and financing terrorism and other prohibited practices risk posed by most of the proposed project activities and the specific measures that will be implemented to mitigate these risks, as well as the elevated risks associated with certain infrastructure activities, the overall compliance risk is deemed to be low.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

Save the Children Australia, as the AE, will be responsible for project supervision, oversight, and adherence with GCF standards. SCI Mail and Save the Children Federation, Inc., will act as executing entities, jointly managing implementation. AEDD will serve as an implementing partner, coordinating with local non-governmental organizations and solar and biodigester suppliers.

The project will establish a PMI within SCI Mail, dedicated to project coordination, fund disbursement, and reporting of GCF resources. The PMI will be responsible for hiring financial and procurement staff to ensure adherence to fiduciary standards. The PMI will report to a Project Steering Committee chaired by AEDD and SCI Mail. The Project Steering Committee will meet regularly to monitor progress against project milestones and objectives.

SCI Mail will establish an internal audit function to ensure that all funds are in compliance with GCF global financial procedures and GCF standards. All project funds will be held in segregated accounts to facilitate precise tracking and reporting of GCF resources. SCI Mail will utilize the services of a third-party auditor for annual independent external audits, including financial reviews, financial reconciliations and internal audits to ensure transparent fund utilization.

The PMI will provide quarterly submissions to GCF according to the funded activity budget and accreditation master agreement. Annual independent external audits will be conducted in line with international standards and best practices to ensure transparency and accountability, improving the project’s financial integrity.

- Procurement activities must adhere to the SCI Global Procurement Policy, which mandates adherence to adding multiple levels approval and transparent documentation to prevent conflicts of interest and ensure value for money. For local procurement, SCI Mail will follow relevant national laws and regulations. All procurement activities and related documents will be monitored by the PMI to ensure alignment with both GCF and GCF procurement standards.

- To maintain transparency and compliance, SCI Mail will conduct procurement training for staff on a regular basis. The PMI will also provide guidance as needed. Independent reviews of procurement processes and an annual audit of procurement records will further support accountability and mitigate risks associated with procurement activities.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP256

Proposal name: Intensification of Agriculture and Agroforestry Techniques (IAAT) for Climate Resilient Food and Nutrition Security: Tombouctou, Gao, Mopti, Koulikoro and Segou.

Accredited entity: Save the Children Australia

Country: Mali

Project size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview
The Intensification of Agriculture and Agroforestry Techniques (IAAT) project is a five-year initiative targeting five regions in Mali: Tombouctou, Gao, Mopti, Koulikoro and Segou. Its overarching objective is to improve food security and nutrition by increasing agricultural productivity while reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. This cross-cutting, small-scale project pursues a dual objective of enhancing livelihoods through climate-resilient agriculture and forestry practices that reduce droughts, floods and extreme heat; and by mitigating climate change by reducing GHG emissions from agricultural systems.

With an implementation lifespan of 15 years, the proposed project focuses on four core outcomes: (a) increased climate-resilient agricultural crop and food production in the target regions in Mali; (b) improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers through enhanced access to markets and services that enhance sustainable livelihoods; (c) adaptation and mitigation considerations are embedded in institutional frameworks, policies, and practices; and (d) reduced GHG emissions from agricultural systems, including agroforestry practices.

To achieve these outcomes, the proposed project will deliver the following eight outputs: 1.) increased adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) and agroforestry practices by smallholder farmers; 2.) increased use of climate-resilient practices in CSA and agroforestry production; 3.) increased land area under agroforestry; 4.) CSA and agroforestry value chains are reconnected and reach more smallholder farmers; 5.) smaller farmer farming communities, especially women’s groups, are strengthened to enhance their capacity for adoption of CSA and agroforestry; 6.) increased adoption of CSA technologies by smallholder farmers; 7.) increased knowledge sharing among smallholder farmers on climate-smart agriculture practices and best practices to address climate-related risks in agriculture; and 8.) enhanced knowledge-sharing and research-based extension services.

The environmental and social category of the project is G, reflecting minimal potential environmental and social risks.

The total cost of the project is USD 41.7 million, of which USD 33.7 million is requested from GCF as a grant. The remaining USD 10 million will be co-financed by national and international partners (USD 5 million in-kind contribution from the Government of Mali and


---
**Page 120**
---

### Impact potential

#### Adaptation impact potential

The IAT project targets 431,700 direct beneficiaries, including 172,680 smallholder farmers, with an emphasis on vulnerable groups such as women and youth. Additionally, Potentiel de l'adaptation represents approximately 5% of the total population will benefit from strengthened public and private extension services.

This comprehensive reach addresses the most climate-vulnerable populations and significantly enhances their resilience to climate change impacts. The inclusion of marginalized groups, particularly women and youth, remains a key challenge in fully realizing the expected impact potential.

To enhance the inclusive participation and empowerment of such marginalized groups, the project should build on its existing mechanisms by establishing dedicated gender and youth initiatives. These initiatives would leverage the project's targeted measures, such as gender-sensitive training programmes, Village Savings and Loans Associations (VSLAs), and other financial services that address barriers to participation while ensuring equitable representation in decision-making processes.

The proposed project aims to avoid the lock-in of vulnerable infrastructure by promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices through the adoption of sustainable management practices. These interventions reduce reliance on conventional, unsustainable farming methods and help to ensure that investments are not rendered unviable if institutional and technical support diminishes post-implementation. The project's strategy of integrating LLA plans and fostering private sector partnerships creates a foundation for long-term commitments with financial and institutional partners who would continue to support the project.

While the project provided a list of prioritized CSA practices selected for inclusion in the updated extension services curriculum, it offered few details on how their resilience to future climate change impacts was assessed. This is particularly important given Mali's vulnerability to food production. Given the drought conditions in Mali, it would be important to consider CSA practices that are more appropriate and resilient to changing climatic conditions.


---
**Page 121**
---

12. The project reduces vulnerability by enhancing adaptive capacity through CSA, agroforestry and renewable energy solutions such as solar pumps and biodigesters. These interventions stabilize livelihoods and are likely to provide resilience against recurrent climate risks such as droughts and floods.

13. Along with the proposed CSA and agroforestry practices aiming to enhance resilience, the project will also support the adoption of other climate-smart agricultural practices in the target regions, particularly in regions like Mogot and Gao. These regions often experience tensions between farmers and pastoralists over land use. The project will promote landscape-based management practices that expand agricultural land use without inadvertently encroaching on established transhumance routes, ensuring sustainable livelihoods for both farmers and pastoralists. This approach should incorporate multi-use landscape planning approaches, balancing the needs of farmers and pastoralists. Specific measures, such as delineating and preserving transhumance corridors within landscapes, will be developed in consultation with stakeholders.

14. Institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning are strengthened through capacity-building initiatives. A Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) is being developed with stakeholder input, aim to embed adaptation considerations in planning processes.

15. The project increases the generation and use of climate information through training and agroforestry practices. However, there remain significant gaps in access to reliable climate data and stakeholders-tailored information could limit the effectiveness of these interventions. This is particularly true for smallholder farmers who lack adequate climate data and adequate information systems. The project takes a clear strategy on this issue.

16. To address this, it should foster long-term commitments from national and international stakeholders in order to integrate into a long-term investment strategy.


### Mitigation impact potential

17. The project is expected to achieve a total reduction in GHG emissions of 2,446,543 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over 15 years through the adoption of CSA practices, agroforestry and renewable energy solutions (solar pumps and biodigesters).

18. The project's projected GHG emission reductions are based on robust methodologies and tools, such as EX-ACT (Environmental Xternality Assessment Tool) of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). These methods use climate change adoption models, assuming consistent and widespread uptake of CSA, agroforestry practices and climate-smart renewable energy solutions. The rate at which these technologies are adopted could be slower owing to economic or climatic barriers. To address this, the project should implement a monitoring system that will provide data on the actual rates of climate change emission reductions. This system will enhance accuracy and provide insights that could inform any necessary adjustments to interventions, ensuring realistic and verifiable mitigation outcomes.

19. By promoting solar irrigation systems and biodigesters, the proposed project avoids the lock-in of high-emission energy infrastructure solutions. These renewable energy solutions offer sustainable alternatives for agricultural activities.


### Agroforestry initiatives under the project will cover over 91,000 hectares, significantly improving soil fertility and water retention in degraded landscapes. These agroforestry systems are expected to contribute substantially to reducing land-use emissions. A key consideration is ensuring land tenure clarity to sustain the mitigation benefits of these activities. To ensure long-term sustainability, the project will work with local communities, governments, and other authorities through land-use management plans, supported by ongoing capacity-building initiatives. Additionally, establishing community-led monitoring committees to oversee


---
**Page 122**
---

rehabilitated lands post-project would help to maintain engagement and prevent disputes or underutilization.

20. The project also fosters low-carbon value chains by strengthening market linkages for CSA-compatible crops and promoting low-emission agricultural practices. Robust market infrastructure, including the development of a private sector-led commodity exchange, is essential in establishing and maintaining these structures to ensure long-term success.

The International Technical Assistance Facility (ITAF) considers the proposed project's impact potential to be medium.

### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: Medium to high**

20. The ITAF project introduces innovative CSA and agroforestry practices, including the deployment of solar pumps and biodigesters, to enhance agricultural productivity and reduce GHG emissions in Mali. These solutions target smallholder farmers, emphasizing women's youth, creating opportunities for income generation and food security.

To ensure sustained financial support and market-driven incentives post-project, the ITAF initiative should operationalize the long-term commitment strategy mentioned in paragraph 20. This can be achieved through partnerships with private sector entities, financial institutions, and government agencies through memorandums of understanding and embedding financial incentive measures, such as a co-financing scheme linked to VSLA, at national policies and frameworks.

24. The project's theory of change provides a clear pathway for scaling up its impact within target regions in Mali. By building on existing CSA models that are already being implemented through community-led approaches and LLA plans, the project creates replicable models that can be adapted to other contexts. However, while these models have been shown to be effective and robust, ensuring the adaptability of these models into diverse contexts will require a focus on knowledge-sharing and capacity-building frameworks that prioritize inclusivity and local context.

25. Therefore, to further tailor the project's knowledge-sharing and capacity-building mechanisms, it is recommended to establish regional or sub-regional knowledge hubs within target regions. These hubs would focus on collecting and sharing region-specific data, traditional practices, and challenges, while adapting training modules to reflect the unique socio-economic contexts of each region. Engaging local communities and community organizations in co-designing these models would ensure cultural relevance and increase their likelihood for successful implementation.

26. The security situation in Mali, particularly in the target regions of Tombouctoue, Gao, Mopti, Koulikoro and Segou, remains relatively precarious. These conditions pose significant risks to project staff and beneficiaries, including potential delays or disruptions in project execution and potential safety threats to project staff and beneficiaries. The current risk assessment framework is based on the most recent available data as of 2019 and may need to be updated. Furthermore, to mitigate these risks, the project, which is critically important for Mali's food security, must ensure that it has a robust security strategy in place, establishing secure sites, strengthening coordination with local authorities, and integrating adaptive measures to sustain operations during periods of heightened insecurity. Additionally,


---
**Page 123**
---

contingency plans should be developed to ensure that project outcomes are safeguarded even under adverse security conditions.

20. A comprehensive monitoring and evaluation (M&E) framework ensures that lessons learned from the IATF project are documented and shared. The use of baseline, midterm and endline evaluations will provide a clear picture of progress made against targets, measuring progress and identifying areas for improvement. These insights will not only inform future iterations of the M&E framework but also contribute to the overall sustainability of the project. However, ensuring sustained funding for the M&E post-project remains a critical challenge.

20. The project outlines potential strategies to address the challenge of sustaining the M&E system post-project. However, these strategies lack sufficient detail and firm commitments, leaving stakeholders uncertain about how they will be implemented or enforced in practice. To address this gap, the sustainability of the M&E system should be explicitly integrated into the project’s business case. This could include establishing a dedicated M&E unit within the project mechanisms, institutional road maps and capacity-building measures to ensure the system's continued functionality and impact post-project.

20. The project concludes by highlighting an enabling environment for long-term sustainability through partnerships with local governments, private sector actors and community groups. By institutionalizing these partnerships in the national context of Mali’s climate policy, it promotes mainstreaming climate-resilient development in Mali. However, as stated above, the absence of a clear M&E framework post-project remains a critical challenge to ensuring the continuity of key project outcomes, particularly in the maintenance of climate information systems and agroforestry initiatives.

20. In addition to creating new markets for CSA products and technologies, the project generates new business activities at the local and national level. The integration of VCA into the project’s market development strategy is seen as a promising approach for market participants. These activities not only stimulate economic growth but also shift incentives towards low-carbon and climate-resilient solutions. The challenge lies in ensuring that these gains are sustained beyond the project period, particularly after 2030, after the project concludes.

19. The IATF project addresses systemic barriers to low-carbon and climate-resilient development by aligning its activities with Mali’s national climate policies and strategies. It advances regulatory frameworks by embedding CSA practices in local governance through LLA plans, which are seen as a promising approach for scaling up CSA adoption at scale. The catalyst broader investments in low-emission and climate-resilient development. However, ensuring continued government and institutional commitment will be crucial to fully realizing these systemic changes.

10. The ITAP assesses the paradigm shift potential of the proposed project as medium to high.


### 1.4 Sustainable development potential

Scale: Medium to high

35. The project demonstrates significant potential for positive environmental externalities through the adoption of CSA and agroforestry practices. By incorporating technologies such as solar pumps and biodigesters, the project reduces reliance on fossil fuels, which helps mitigate climate change impacts. Additionally, it promotes biodiversity by integrating tree cover into agricultural landscapes, which provides habitats for various species. These activities contribute to global climate goals and align with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 1 (Climate action), 15 (Life on land) and 12 (Responsible


---
**Page 124**
---

consumption and production). However, sustaining these environmental benefits post-project will require consistent adoption by farmers and adequate technical and financial support from local and national institutions.

16. The project partially addresses the concerns regarding sustaining environmental benefits post-project through a strong funding foundation through training, policy alignment and initial financial mechanisms, the lack of detailed and formalized long-term strategies for institutional and financial sustainability remains a challenge after the project cycle has ended.

35. The project is poised to deliver significant social co-benefits by improving the health and well-being of both women and men and it achieves this by promoting access to clean energy solutions, increasing agricultural productivity through improved irrigation systems and water harvesting. Furthermore, capacity-building programs empower communities to adopt and restore more resilient livelihoods in the face of climate change. The project also addresses gender inequalities embedded within the project aim to address social inequities, fostering inclusion and participation of women and youth in decision-making processes (SDG 5: Gender equality). However, a challenge remains in ensuring equitable access to these benefits for marginalized groups, particularly women.

36. From an economic perspective, the project has the potential to transform local economies by creating jobs along the CSA and agroforestry value chains. Employment opportunities will be created through the installation of solar pumps and biodigesters are expected to enhance economic capacity. Strengthened local institutions and improved access to markets for smallholder farmers, especially women and youth. Additionally, the VSLAs expand financial inclusion in underserved rural areas. These efforts contribute to SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth). However, the project's long-term social and economic benefits post-project will depend on private sector engagement and the successful integration of financial mechanisms into existing market systems.

37. Furthermore, to maximize the economic and social benefits of renewable energy solutions like biodigesters while minimizing potential conflicts, the project should promote clear revenue-sharing arrangements between the project and local communities where revenues are appropriately distributed between biogas production and livestock feed to meet the needs of both sectors. This will ensure that all stakeholders benefit from the project's efforts in optimizing residue use and implementing sustainable practices.

38. The project’s private sector engagement strategy for promoting synergies is lacking the enhancement of market linkages post-project. To strengthen this strategy, the project should facilitate private sector partnerships through binding agreements, provide clearer financial terms and conditions, and develop a comprehensive business development plan for scaling these efforts within national frameworks.

29. The proposed project takes a proactive approach to gender equality and social inclusion, addressing barriers to women’s access to education, credit and access to resources. By improving women's access to training, financial services and leadership opportunities in CSA practices, the project aims to empower women as key stakeholders, enhancing inclusivity and cultural sensitivity within the project (SDG 5: Gender equality). However, ensuring sustained institutional and cultural support for gender-focused interventions remains a challenge after the project cycle has ended.

39. The TAP rates the sustainable development potential of the proposed project as medium to high.

### Country ownership

The project aligns closely with Mali’s national climate priorities, as reflected in its nationally determined contributions and the national adaptation programme of action. The national government has demonstrated strong commitment to climate resilience by prioritizing critical sectors for enhancing climate resilience, and the project activities are such that they can be implemented within existing institutional frameworks.

The project leverages Mali's existing enabling policy and institutional frameworks, involving key institutions like the Ministry of Environment and other sectoral ministries. By integrating these frameworks into the project design, it ensures alignment with national governance structures, which strengthens its institutional foundation. However, the absence of explicit provisions for long-term sustainability within the current institutional frameworks presents a potential gap in sustainability. Formalizing agreements to integrate these projects into existing institutional frameworks will promote an enabling environment created by the project and ensure its continuity beyond the project duration.

The accredited entity and the implementing entities have a track record of implementing climate-resilient initiatives in Mali and similar contexts. While the proponents'
