1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Score: Medium**

The proposed project employs a grant-based financial structure, which is appropriate given the significant upfront costs and long timelines needed to achieve results in CSA and agroforestry. The grant-based structure allows for flexibility in implementation timing as the project's structure supports both immediate implementation and, at some instances, long-term scalability. Mechanisms such as VSLAs and subsidies for solar irrigation systems ensure accessibility of water resources and energy sources, respectively, which can reduce costs for larger solar irrigation systems during deployment. However, the economic and financial analysis does not account for potential climate-related risks, such as droughts or floods, which could affect the project's financial soundness and the farmers' ability to repay loans. The project's vulnerability assessment also did not consider extreme weather events that could impact water availability.

The project demonstrates strong cost-effectiveness through its competitive emissions mitigation potential of 103 tCO2e/ha/year. However, the exclusion of extreme climate events in the EFA as stated in paragraph 50 above poses a critical limitation; one single slow onset or extreme event such as prolonged droughts or flooding could disrupt project implementation and impact the achievement of project results.

The project aims to leverage additional financial flow through partnerships with microfinance organizations, agribusiness investors and farmer contributions. Co-financing mechanisms, such as VSLAs and subsidies for solar irrigation systems, are in place to encourage private sector engagement. However, the EFA does not assess how extreme climate events might impact these co-financing mechanisms or the willingness of farmers to participate. Clear risk-sharing agreements and contingency measures could mitigate these concerns.

Best practices and innovative approaches are central to the project's design. The adoption of globally recognized CSA practices, such as compost application, contour farming and soil preservation techniques, is a key component of the project. The inclusion of subsidies for the payment schemes for scaling solar irrigation and digital platforms for market access highlights the project's innovative strategies for overcoming systemic barriers. However, the absence of


---
**Page 127**
---

strategy adapt their practices to extreme climate conditions may limit their effectiveness during periods of severe weather stress. Incorporating adaptive measures and regularly updating practices based on local climate trends would ensure that the project remains resilient under extreme conditions.

The project shows strong financial viability for farmers, with internal rates of return ranging from 16 per cent for shea butter to 246 per cent for vegetables across value chains. Increased profitability is expected through improved access to markets and adoption of agro-industry practices. However, sensitivity analysis do not sufficiently consider how extreme climate events (even a single event) could impact the project’s financial viability. This uncertainty presents a risk to the economic soundness of the project, especially for vulnerable farmers with limited capital or assets.

The ITAP considers the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project to be medium.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 15. The ITAP is aligned with Mali’s climate and development priorities, addressing key challenges such as food security, poverty reduction, and sustainable land management through CSA and agroforestry, mitigating GHG emissions through renewable energy adoption, positions it as a transformative intervention.

The theory of change and institutional arrangements are robust but need to be further developed. The project has identified some gaps in the explicit and inclusive assessment of extreme climate risks, security risks in the country context, and land use conflicts. Addressing these areas through a thorough risk reduction strategy is critical for ensuring long-term sustainability, particularly ensuring greater impact for Mali’s vulnerable populations, particularly women and youth.

Based on the analysis presented above, the ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding request under the following conditions precedent to the second disbursement under the funded activity agreement:

(a) Delivery to GCF by the accredited entity, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat;

(b) A comprehensive and detailed strategy to achieve institutional and financial sustainability of the project, including arrangements for land tenure and resource management. This involves formalizing agreements and partnerships with public and private stakeholders to secure their commitment, support and collaboration, ensuring continuous engagement and active stakeholder engagement even after the implementation period has concluded; and

(c) A strategy to preserve transboundary connectivity within agroforestry zones to ensure sustainable production across the project.

Furthermore, the ITAP recommends that, during the first two years of the project, the accredited entity:

(d) Establish clear mechanisms for tailoring CSA and agroforestry practices to current and future local conditions, leveraging localized knowledge hubs and stakeholder feedback;

(e) Institutionalize platforms for continuous stakeholder consultation, with a particular focus on women’s participation in decision-making processes; and

(f) Establish a dedicated gender and youth inclusion task force within the local coordination committees.

# Introduction

Mali is currently divided into 8 Regions, plus one capital (Bamako). The Regions are in turn subdivided into 12 Cercles and 703 Communes. Mali has enormous potential and abundant resources, but it faces major security, institutional, governance, and economic challenges, which are hampering progress towards poverty reduction and sustainable development. Development quality in the country area is also threatened by the effects of climate change, including drivers such as rising temperatures and erratic rainfall, resulting in hazards such as prolonged droughts, floods, heat waves, and desertification. These hazards have a direct impact on food security, water availability, soil salinity, crop damage and livestock illnesses etc. These impacts greatly affect vulnerable people which include women, children, the elderly, and those living with disabilities.

Regional and local researchers, as well as the international community, have agreed for more than a decade that the impacts of climate change are affecting the natural resources essential to livelihoods in Mali. The combination of these factors, coupled with existing social and economic problems is exacerbating the risks associated with forced migration and insecurity in the country. While the links between inter-community conflicts over access to and control of natural resources, the rise of violent extremism, and climate change are complex, they are not unrelated. The increasing number of conflicts between farmers and pastoralists (and their exploitation by various actors) illustrates the severity of these exacerbations by climate change and environmental degradation as well as other security challenges.

A large proportion of Mali's population (over 80%) works in agriculture and related sectors; and its agricultural sector is key for stimulating economic growth and food security. Low productivity with limited farmer access to productive inputs and technologies, underdeveloped agricultural markets, and weak rural infrastructure are major constraints on the agricultural sector that need to be addressed to build climate-resilient food systems in the country. These challenges are disproportionately distributed between climate-vulnerable farming communities and inland locations. The impacts of climate change are also unevenly distributed across regions. Vulnerability, extremes and change are already affecting the target regions, reducing agricultural productivity, increasing soil erosion, and depleting water resources. Climate change is also having a negative impact on fodder resources and escalating biodiversity loss. The impacts of climate change are felt disproportionately by those most vulnerable to its effects.

An innovative approach is therefore needed to anticipate interventions and catalyze a transformation of smallholder agricultural production in Mali, focusing on the sustainable use of agricultural and agroforestry practices that can increase resilience to climate change. This will require increases and adaptive learning for farmers and other value chain actors; while adapting and building resilience to climate change.

The UN Environment Programme (UNEP) aims to meet these objectives by sustainably increasing the agricultural and agroforestry production of vulnerable households to contribute to food diversification using innovative and climate-resilient techniques and practices; supporting the development of climate-resilient value chains in key regions, including through the promotion of market access; strengthening the enabling environment for smallholder farmers, as well as public and private agriculture-related institutions; and promoting gender equality. The assessment was carried out by a team of 12 experts from UNEP and other partners. A detailed analysis was carried out on gender inequalities in professional fields and in the rural economy across all regions of Mali. The assessment also included a study of stakeholders' access to and control over resources and benefits, particularly land rights; women's participation in decision-making and conflict management bodies, their access to formal education, extension, and


Source: Integrated Environmental Assessment of the Assessment Report UN Environment Programme, UNEP December 2012

### Financial Services, as Well as the Impact of GBV and the COVID Pandemic and the Presence or Absence of Male Heads in Families of Women

The gender assessment is provided in more detail following the introduction.

#### Status of Women

- **Political Representation**: In 2023, women accounted for 28.5% of members of parliament.
- **Local Councils**: Women made up 61.7% of local councilors compared to 38.3% men in 2023.

#### Women's Roles

- **Household Activities**: Women play a vital role in agriculture, environmental management and biodiversity in Mali (particularly agro-biodiversity), as they are primarily responsible for crops that are essential to nutrition.
- **Knowledge Transfer**: Women also have the knowledge and skills needed to manage small farms according to gender-related social codes. These roles include crop processing and selling forest resources.

#### Gender Differences

- **Agriculture**: In the agricultural sector, women are the main operators and processors of staple foods (maize, rice, sorghum, sorrel, monkey bread, shea butter, etc.) and are present throughout the agricultural chain.
- **Small-Scale Livestock Farming**: Women also play a significant role in small-scale livestock farming and are often the primary sellers of forest resources. They have specialized knowledge of forest plants which they use to increase their production and productivity.

#### Challenges

- **Limited Land Ownership**: In Mali, women own only 3% of land compared to men who own 97%. This means that most communities in Mali are governed by men.
- **Small-Scale Farming**: Women manage less than 10% of farms. Only 31% of farms in Mali are managed by women and these tend to be small.

#### Gender Disparities

- **Land Ownership**: Women own only 3% of land compared to 97% owned by men.
- **Farm Size**: Women's plots are also much smaller than those of men (0.5 ha vs 1.5 ha).

### 1. Access to technologies and information: Despite the equity efforts of support partners (national and international), women still face significant constraints in accessing these resources. The constraints to this access are:
- (i) land issues;
- (ii) methods of accessing land that do not encourage women's participation; 
- (iii) lack of access to credit, inputs, extension services, and other technologies and services.
The literacy rate of women is just 22% compared to 44% for men; low access to education and information technology also hinders women's ability to use these resources. Women have access to the telephone. However, they do not control it. It is the husband who buys the phone and pays the bill. The same applies to radio (AM/FM), television (TV or radio). In terms of time availability (daily, monthly, annually), the analysis shows that women invest more time than men, whatever the season. So men are more available than women.

### 2. Involvement in value chains: Gender equality is an essential dimension of inclusion agricultural production, processing, food distribution and resilient food systems. Women play an important role in agriculture and most value chains. However, their work often remains invisible or insufficiently supported.
Gender inequality affects the productivity and huts the economic performance of value chains. In Mali, women are present to varying degrees throughout the agricultural value chain and are involved in the processing and marketing of produce. In the agricultural sector, women are primarily responsible for growing food crops (maize, millet, sorghum) that increase their production and productivity (lack of access to control and ownership of land and inputs). Women also play a significant role in livestock rearing, poultry farming, and all the activities associated with processing and marketing agricultural products. Their role as processors is essential for food security at household level. Women are also heavily involved in growing food crops and market gardening for the family's subsistence.
The challenge of food security cannot be met without counting on their contribution to food production and processing. However, women's contributions remain invisible or insufficiently recognized by legislation; in practice, the influence of men dominates in terms of leadership. Decisions are generally taken by men in public, and women are only consulted when necessary.

*Source: Regional consultations and Allbarra Gender Analysis

1.3 Participation in decision-making bodies and conflict prevention/management: In Mali, social norms and the sexual division of labour largely exclude women from areas of public decision-making or conflict resolution. Article 156 of December 2013. The NGO Mali includes gender indicators in its action plan.

In COVID - 19 has further weakened the most vulnerable groups, especially young girls. In particular, young women who work near offices and in schools saw their incomes fall and found themselves trapped in their day-to-day activities, in most cases taking no appropriate measures to protect themselves. The configuration of Mali society meant that they were more likely than men to be exposed to the virus, which increased their greater risk of being contaminated and transmitting the disease to the rest of their family. In times of crisis, women are often the first to be affected by poverty, especially those living in deprived of their homes and whose means of subsistence have been undermined, the arrival of a pandemic has only exacerbated this situation. During the COVID-19 pandemic, women suffered more gender-based violence due to the general social stress combined with increasing tensions surrounding the fact that the family lived in unusual proximity.

As far as the level of information was concerned, many people, especially women, knew that there were health arrangements in place, but did not have precise and clear information about containment measures. The Ministry of Health has therefore developed a community care protocol. The telephone and local radio stations were effective means of communication for reaching out to the population.

Several actions were taken, including the involvement of women in community dialogue and the creation of income-generating activities to empower them. A number of measures have been taken, including the distribution of face masks and soap, and social distancing. WhatsApp groups (particularly in the Mopti region, with the organisation of E-forums for women) were created to provide information on COVID-19 prevention and income-generating activities (IGAs) to empower them. As a result, women have been able to assess the scale of the pandemic.

The scale of gender-based violence (GBV) has been exacerbated by the political, institutional and security crises that began in 2012, the persistence of inter-community conflicts, and the abrogation of the law on marriage and family. The age at which girls are married is also a female genital mutilation is the most widespread form of GBV in Mali (83% of women aged between 15 and 49) and 46.8% of girls were married before the age of 18. GEMMAP recorded 4,017 cases of FGM in 2019, compared to 3,900 in 2018.

These figures only partly reflect the real situation of GBV in the country, given the weakness of the legal framework and the lack of resources for its implementation. The fact that women are not always aware of their rights is also a factor. Unlike men (9%), and family pressure often prevents them from speaking out about the violence they suffer.

The National Action Plan on Gender Equality (NAPGE) was adopted in 2018, but the multi-sectoral and multi-party committee under the leadership of the MPEFP is still in the process of being adopted internally at the executive level. According to the Regional Directorate for Women's Affairs in Koulikoro, 35 cases of FGM were recorded in 2019.

Concerning the involvement of women in conflict resolution, conflict prevention and resolution issues in Mali remain under the almost exclusive management of men in the communities and are therefore not always taken into account. This is due to a lack of awareness on gender considerations, even though they have proven potential and capabilities in conflict resolution and prevention and more generally in peace-building. Since 2012, women have accounted for


---
**Page 139**
---

less than 10% of peace negotiations, and this figure has hardly changed since the adoption of resolution 1325 in 2000. The government has shown its political will to take account of the gender dimension in conflict prevention and management, for example through the provisions of the national strategy for reconciliation and social cohesion and its 2022-2028 action plan (priority 4: strengthening women’s participation in peace processes and security sector reform; priority 1.2: Strengthen the participation of women and young people in the peace process).

Although there is a clear political will to take the gender dimension into account in the reconciliation and social cohesion strategy, as well as in the national action plan for conflict prevention and resolution (NAPCR) at central level and local (CRCF, COFO, CCS), it is clear that women are poor or not at all represented in conflict prevention and resolution mechanisms. In order to ensure a more effective and sustainable women’s contribution, the IAT project will encourage a change in behaviour towards a culture of inclusiveness in formal and informal conflict resolution and prevention mechanisms at local level. This will be achieved through capacity building for women mediators and their empowerment opportunities. In addition to the contestation of local leaders, opinion leaders and religious figures, women’s participation is also hampered by the lack of knowledge about their role in reviving conflict management mechanisms exacerbating women's vulnerability. This programme aims at addressing these issues by providing training on gender equality and proven skills in social mediation and by strengthening their role in family and community units on issues of social cohesion and conflict management. Past experience, particularly with peace education, has shown that women are more likely to be involved in the prevention and impact on conflict prevention and resolution at community level, especially as they themselves can play a key role in preventing conflicts and promoting reconciliation.

The number of internally displaced people (IDPs) in the project area is 123,857 according to December 2015 and its dissemination in the IAT project areas should also be considered.


### 1.4 Displaced people: With the security situation, women and girls are becoming increasingly vulnerable. They are often victims of sexual violence (raped, beaten, tortured, kidnapped, abducted or forced) and are left to fend for themselves, mainly in the Magie and Tomboutou regions. The displacement is also due to campaigns against houses under construction. At the national level, for example, there are 123,857 displaced people according to December 2015. Of these displaced persons, of whom 224,124 are women, i.e., 54.34% are women.


### 1.5 Associations: The Regional Directorate for the Promotion of Women and Children and the FAP in the general area have listed 102 associations/groupings in Segou, 255 in Koulikoro, 251 associations in Mopti, 1,700 in Gao and 455 in Timbuktu. Many women’s associations are also present in the project areas. These associations are mainly women’s or mixed gender groups. Despite the lack of material and financial resources, these associations can mobilize women to participate in various activities such as income-generating activities, access to resource persons, leaders who can be put to good use in promoting women in value chains, climate security and the sustainable management of natural resources. They are also involved in civil action committees (CACs) and community action plans for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

### 1.6 Women’s allies and supporters: The coordinators of neighborhood and village chiefs, the High Council of Women, the women’s associations, the religious leaders, the mediators and communicators all support gender inclusion/gender equity and equality. The IAT project will have to take into account these actors in order to ensure a more effective and sustainable women's contribution. These actors can also play an important role in supporting people in the development of CACs and community action plans for climate change adaptation and mitigation. To do this, each of these actors will have to benefit from a reinforcement and support programme based on the needs identified above, so that they are in a position to fully

### 1.7 Project goals and objectives

This proposed IAT project will support climate-vulnerable smallholder agricultural production systems and rural livelihoods in Mali to increase climate resilience and low-emission development pathways. The project has four components: 1) increase adoption of improved and climate-smart agricultural practices, including the use of agroforestry; 2) improve connectivity of smallholders, women and youth to the agriculture input and output markets; 3) reduce greenhouse gas emissions through increased use of organic fertilizers, crop rotation, and manure management through biogas plants; 4) strengthen institutional and stakeholders' capacities for scaling up improved and climate-smart agroforestry and agriculture.

The project's paradigm shift goal is: IF smallholder farmers in highly vulnerable regions in Mali can access the right inputs at the right time, AND they adopt appropriate agricultural practices, THEN their food, nutrition and water security will be improved BECAUSE their adaptive capacity to climate change will increase. This will lead to increased market standards, markets and sustainable livelihoods, and that of public and private institutions, will be strengthened to respond to and reduce the climate change risk and impacts. The project will drive improvements in the following areas: 1) increasing access to improved agricultural inputs; improve localized climate-resilient agricultural yields; reduce the time, energy, and labor effort by food producers; increase the availability of water for irrigation; increase the control of agricultural inputs; and increase income. Long-term impacts will be achieved by increasing the use of organic fertilizers, crop rotation, and manure management through biogas plants to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Other long-term impacts will include enhanced access to climate-smart technologies to reduce climate-compromised (drought, heat seasonality, and flood) impacts on food production; increased resilience of smallholder farmers' livelihoods that raise yields and improve food security, and reduce emissions through improved livestock and fodder practices.

The proposed project will be implemented in five climate-vulnerable regions of Mali selected based on the climate risk assessment and stakeholder consultations. These regions include from north to south: Tombouctou, Gao, Mopti, Koulikoro, and Segou. In Tombouctou, Gao and Mopti, the project will be implemented in 12 communes (of which six are already funded by USAID). The Abtara will co-finance to implement some climate action and capacity-building activities in these three regions. In Koulikoro and Segou, the project will build on existing projects and programs of the Ministry of Environment and Sanitation within them 48 communes, in the Tombouctou, Gao, Mopti, Koulikoro and Segou regions on Mali based on the climate risk assessment and stakeholder consultations. The cities, circles, and communes beneficiaries will include smallholder farmers and their households, women's groups, entrepreneurs, private sector businesses, extension services members, and members of public institutions. The Ministry of Environment and Sanitation (MIES) (hereinafter referred to by its acronym) under the Ministry of Environment and Sanitation will serve as the Implementing Entity and Save the Children Mali (SCi Mali) will serve as the Executing Entity.

This annex outlines the gender assessment and action plan of the IAA T. The assessment presents how this project can address gender equality issues, identify and tackle the persisting gender barriers, and biases, and contribute towards improved gender equality.

2. Methodological approach

Our methodological approach was participatory at all stages, involving all stakeholders and in particular the SD Mali team leading the assignment. There were six (6) main phases in the implementation of the evaluation mission. These were briefly as follows: (i) scoping meeting; (ii) documentation review; (iii) data collection; (iv) analysis and synthesis; (v) production and submission of the gender report and action plan.

2.1 Assessment steps:

Stage 1: Scoping the study: a scoping meeting was held with the SD Mali team responsible for technical guidance and validation of the methodology and data collection tools. This meeting was an important step, in that it enabled firstly to gain a better understanding of SCI Mali’s assignment and its objectives, as well as the gender component in terms of reference. It also provided an ideal framework for: (i) introducing ourselves to the rest of the team; (ii) deepening our understanding of the objectives and expectations of the study in order to adapt a common work plan with predefined survey; (iii) providing us with all the documents needed to carry out the gender analysis and conduct the assignment.

Stage 2: Literature review: Another key stage in our approach was the literature review. Without being exhaustive, this stage covered all the national and international documents relevant to the issue of gender equality in agriculture. These included laws, decrees, regulations, international standards, reports on similar studies, the project document and all other documentation. Documents were also collected from other structures (technical services, AEDs) and other relevant stakeholders. The results of this stage are presented below.

Stage 3: Determination of data collection targets and development of the methodological guide for the work (including the guides for collecting data broken down by sex): For the actual evaluation mission, a series of meetings were held in various regions of Mali, targeted at regional consultations and identified key informants. Secondly, sex-disaggregated data collection questionnaires were drawn up.

Stage 4: Fieldwork: Kéita, Koukouni, Séguo, Mopi, Gao and Timbuktu: Meetings were held in these regions in the form of national and regional consultations, and the information gathered forms an integral part of this report. Additional information was gathered through individual interviews with key informants and focus group discussions.

Representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture and representatives from civil society organizations including women's groups, savings and loan associations and women representatives from NGOs, and the private sector.

3. Description of the reference situations:1. Gender issues and inequalities in Mali

Mali has a long and rich history of successive kingdoms and empires, which have exerted a strong influence in West Africa, and the country is known for its cultural diversity and its more than twenty main ethnic groups. The majority of Malians live under patriarchal hierarchies, with a few exceptions in the north. The main religions practiced in Mali are Islam (92.5%), Christianity (3.2%) and Animism (4.3%). Customary and traditional rights and the Muslim Sharia law coexist in Mali. In addition to these two legal systems, there is also the risk of misinterpretation of messages that prevent women from fully enjoying their rights. As a result, the legal framework for gender equality is not always respected. This can lead to customary laws or rules, which are much more conservative in terms of the roles, responsibilities and power relationships between men and women. Table 2 provides some country-level indicators about gender.

### Gender Development Index (GDI): This measures the average achievement of women and men on three fundamental dimensions of human development: health (measured by life expectancy at birth), education (measured by the expected length of schooling for boys and girls, and the average number of years of study for adults aged 25 and over) and control over economic resources. The GDI is calculated as a weighted average of three sub-indices. In 2019, the GDI was 0.388 for women, compared with 0.473 for men, giving a GDI of 0.621 and placing the country in Group B. By comparison, Burkina Faso and Niger have an HDI of 0.567 and 0.724 respectively.[1]

#### Multidimensional poverty index (MPI): The multidimensional poverty index determines the deprivations faced by people in the areas of education, health and standard of living. It provides a measure of the intensity of deprivation for each person. In 2019, 38% of people lived in multidimensional poverty and the intensity of this poverty (relative number of deprivations) was 4.5. The adjusted MPI is calculated as the proportion of the population living in multidimensional poverty, adjusted for the intensity of deprivation, is 0.376. Burkina Faso and Niger have MPIs of 0.519 and 0.590 respectively[2].

#### Gender Inequality Index (GII)[3]: This measures inequalities in the achievements of men and women on three fundamental dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market. The GII should enable analysis of policies and efforts. A high value indicates strong inequality between men and women. Mali's GII of 0.671 places it 158th out of 162 countries in the 2019 index. In 2019, 34% of women aged 15 to 49 years old had given birth before their 18th birthday and completed secondary education, compared with 16.4% of men. Out of every 100,000 live births in Mali, the number of deaths for children under five is 72. The adolescent fertility rate is 169.1 births per 1,000 women aged 15 to 19. The adult literacy rate is 82%, compared with 88% for men.[4]

### National, regional and international policies on gender equality and the environment, particularly those relating to women's rights, land rights, climate change, forest resources and agriculture.

At its 30th session in 1995, the present to the Malian Constitution of 25 February 1995 affirms the sovereign people's subscription to the Universal Declaration of Human Rights of 10 December 1948 and the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights. The principle of the primacy of universal human rights is enshrined in Article 3, which states that "the State guarantees to all its citizens some rights to citizens of both sexes without discrimination and proclaims in its preamble the defence of the rights of women and children as well as the cultural and linguistic diversity of the national community."

- The National Gender Policy: The National Gender Policy, with its six (6) Strategic Orientations and twenty (20) Strategic Axes, is based on a holistic, socio-economic and socio-cultural approach. The National Gender Policy is based on respect for universal rights while combining the values associated with a tolerant society that is open to the world and committed to a favourable change in traditions and customs towards greater justice, equity and equality.
- In addition, Mali has ratified, often with reservations, a number of national and international conventions and protocols related to the promotion and protection of women's rights, such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women since September 1982; the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol) since November 2000 and its Additional Protocol, as well as the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa.
- On 29 September 2015, governments adopted a new international development agenda, comprising a set of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) with the aim of eradicating poverty by 2030. The SDG Agenda is based on three pillars: (i) to end poverty and hunger; (ii) to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls; and (iii) to eradicate diseases and promote health for all. In this context, a number of concrete actions have been identified as critical conditions for achieving the MDGs, including: (i) undertake reforms in the land tenure system with a view to ensuring access to ownership and control of land and other forms of property, financial services, inheritance and natural resources in accordance with domestic law; (ii) strengthen the use of key technologies and information systems that are accessible by women and men alike for the empowerment of women; (iii) adopt and strengthen appropriate policies and enforceable legislation for the protection of equal rights to land ownership and access to resources at all levels.
- Mali also has a new reference framework for formulating, implementing and monitoring the various government policies and strategies that the country has developed in recent times, at both national and sectoral levels. This 2nd generation covers the period 2019–2035 and is based on the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by 2030, building on the country’s potential and resilience to promote inclusive development. The National Gender Policy was integrated into this framework. The Government Action Programme for the transition period was adopted on Monday 02 July 2021. All public policies will have to integrate gender, employment and climate change into the development of the country.
- At sector level, a national strategy to combat gender-based violence (GBV) was drawn up in 2019. This law also provides for the establishment of a special fund for women’s rights and elective and non-elective public posts. This law increased the percentage of women in appointments from 8% in 2014 to 15.73% in 2017. However, the minimum representation of 30% provided by this law has not been reached.
- The legal framework on gender equality is marked by important international and national conventions that Mali has ratified: the Conventions of the Rio de Janeiro convention (UNFCCC), CBD, CDD). other earlier conventions (Ramsar Convention, CITES) and is a party to numerous international and regional agreements relating to the management of natural resources.
- At the continental level, Mali took part in major initiatives such as the Great Green Wall project, the development of the NEPAD Environment Initiative, the Poverty-Environment Initiative, the Global Environment Facility (GEF), and the African Union’s Agenda 2063 for sustainable climate change under the aegis of the Conference of African Ministers of the Environment (CAME).

Law no. 95-02 of 18/09/96 on the principle of establishing and managing the domain of local authorities identifies the conditions under which these authorities may appropriate and manage parts of the national land domain, and sets out their responsibilities and rights in this area. This law governs the creation and management of local authority land, namely forest land, agricultural land, pastoral land and other types of land.

Civil society is also represented in consultation frameworks and decision-making bodies. SECONGO is the environmental leader for national NGOs and CAFO is the environmental leader for women's organisations and associations. These two organisations have participated in all the activities related to the development of forest law and are supported by Malian civil society as well as in line with the conclusions of the Rio Conference and Agenda 21.

The main laws and regulatory texts relating to forests and wildlife are 6 Laws, 18 Decrees and 6 Orders. The six key laws are:

- **Forestry** - Law no. 95-003 of 18 January 1995 on the organisation of timber exploitation, transport and trade.
- **Wildlife** - Law No. 95-004 of 18 January 1995 laying down the conditions for the management of forest resources.
- **Wildlife** - Law N°10-028 of 12 July 2010 determining the principles of management of the resources of the national forest estate.

### With regard to wildlife

Law No. 13/06 of 27 June 2018, setting the conditions for the management of wild fauna and its habitat.


#### Institutional framework

At institutional level, the Ministry of the Environment has set up six central departments:

- Direction Générale des Eaux et Forêts ;
- Direction Nationale de l'Assainissement, des Pollutions et des Nuisances ;
- Environnement et Développement Durable Agency ;
- Niger River Basin Agency ;
- Agence Nationale de Gestion des Stations d'Epuration du Mali.

In addition to these legal and institutional frameworks, there are:

- The National Forest Policy (PNP) adopted in 1986 with the following objectives:
    - Promotion of actions to adapt to the impacts of climate change (OPN2);
    - Promotion, extension and transfer of appropriate technologies as developed by research (OPN3);
    - Capacity building on climate change (OPN6).
- The National Programme for Poverty Eradication (PNPE) was adopted in 1998 and revised in 2018. This policy ensures the protection of natural resources including water, forest and land.

In most of the policies developed over the last ten years, the principles of equity and/or equality are announced. In strategies and/or programs, gender is specifically or indirectly integrated through the targeting of women, young people or mixed, marginalised, and vulnerable groups.


---
**Page 148**
---

The Forum d'Appui à l'Autornomisation de la Femme et à l'Épanouissement de l'Enfant (FAFÉ) is a fund initiated by UN Women as part of the implementation of the NQF in support of the Ministry for the Promotion of Women, Children and the Family. It is aimed at financing initiatives to develop women's entrepreneurship in Mali. Gender-responsive budgeting (GRB) is now an integral component of the national budgetary process. The gender assessment takes into account various development sectors with a view to creating opportunities in the fight against women’s poverty. In 2011, Mali adopted its National Gender-Sensitive Planning and Budgeting Strategy (NGSPBS), as part of the implementation of strategic guideline No. 3 of the National Gender Policy on good governance in public policies and reforms in the context of decentralisation and deconcentration.

### Results of the gender assessment

The consultations conducted at the national and sub-national level were essential for the design of the project in a way that is responsive to the needs of women as the target populations, and to identify the key climatic and non-climatic challenges and vulnerabilities faced by women in the target areas. The gender assessment synthesises information collected from all the consulted stakeholders on key gender aspects, including:

- Gender and social norms and division of labour
- Access to productive resources and decision-making power at household and community level
- Decision making power at household and community level
- Impact of climate change, particularly drought, on the pre-existing gender inequalities
- Coping strategies used by men and women in order for women and girls to overcome climate change impacts
- Existing policies, understanding and adoption of gender mainstreaming for social protection and climate adaptation activities.

#### 4.1. Division of labour between men and women and decision-making models

The roles and responsibilities of men and women are specific to each culture. They can also vary from one region to another within the same country. In some cultures, men and women, as well as girls and boys, may engage in activities, but most of the time their responsibilities are different. The domestic production process obeys the rules of land sharing and the division of tasks between men and women. For example, in the Dogon culture, the division of labour is: men do agricultural work while women take care of household chores.

The social or sexual division of labour also obliges women to take care of housework, the education and health of their children, the daily chore of supplying drinking water and firewood, the upkeep of the family home, the preparation of meals, the collection of firewood, the planting and so on. All the labour and resources available to the extended family are mobilised to cultivate the common fields. In the Dogon culture, for example, in the regions of Bandiagara, Kidal, Ségou, Mopti, Timbuktu and Gao regions, a large part of the week (generally five or six days): Christians do not go to the fields on Sundays and Mondays but Friday, but family members also do not go to work in the fields. The women are responsible for taking care of the household while men reserve rest time and work in the individual fields, especially for the women, on the plots of land granted by their husbands or fathers. In addition to these tasks, they have to collect firewood to feed the family, pay each man's first marriage expenses, buy collective equipment and solve other


---
**Page 149**
---

collective problems. This agricultural economy, which is not very mechanised, requires a lot of human labour.

Women are also involved in the agricultural value chain and participate in the processing and marketing of the products produced. However, they are not paid, nor do they control the income from this production. In addition to primary care and household tasks, women, with the help of their daughters, collect water and firewood for cooking and heating, which is a major time-consuming activity, income, which they manage and control, even if they have to inform the men21. Men are involved in agricultural activities such as ploughing, sowing, weeding, harvesting, and other income-generating tasks and control all income linked to agricultural production. The movement of men in conventional agriculture is also influenced by cultural constraints for women, such as economic restrictions, fewer meals and a double workload.

The assessment also revealed that in the target regions, the social division of labour results in an arduous burden on women and girls. This is due to the fact that women are often excluded from traditions and customs. It should be noted, however, that it is increasingly common to see men collecting water and firewood, where the increase in income has led to these changes aimed at protecting their children’s education. In addition, some men have started to participate more actively in income-generating activities such as livestock rearing or small-scale trade. This excludes women and girls from taking part in agricultural, livestock and income-generating activities.

Women are also involved in the production of food crops:

• Land: Women and men, girls and boys plant the seed; after the fields have been prepared, the men and boys are responsible for weeding. At harvest time, everyone is involved, but at distribution, women and girls collect the produce while the men and boys keep the seeds to replant them in their own fields.

In Mégpi, women are much more involved in the production of groundnuts, cowpeas,

• millet, sorghum, maize, beans, yams, cassava, sweet potatoes, bananas, plantains, and

sorghum. Pottery is an activity reserved exclusively for women, who use the earth as a resource.

• In Koulikoro: women produce groundnuts, sesame and red "dà" (hibiscus)

• In Séguo: women produce groundnuts, dà, okra, red dà, sesame, especially in Bia, and cassava

• In Gao: women are heavily involved in rice production

The men are more involved in rice production and increasingly in leguminous crops and some fruit trees.

• Pasture / livestock: in the traditional environment of all the regions analysed, livestock farming is a major activity. The animals reared by women include goats, sheep, cattle, donkeys or mules, which they rear for their own consumption or to sell them at markets. Women also rear cattle for the Ramadan festivities, either individually or as part of a family cooperative, in stalls or on pastures. In some cases, women are involved in the sale and purchase of animals wholesale, some of whom come from outside the area. As for the women, they rear small ruminants (goat and sheep), and are increasingly improving their family or bush rearing (dahie) to sell them at markets. The cattle pens are usually owned by men, but in some cases, women, who inform their husbands before using them, and the same applies to organic manure production. In addition, women have started to participate more actively in the sale of certain partners, such as PRAAPs; women are increasingly involved in cattle fattening. Traditionally, cattle pens are the property of the whole family, and roles are shared between men and women. The young woman is responsible for the whole process of milking and its management. The young


---
**Page 150**
---

men raise livestock, equal or some poultry for themselves. The young boys act as shepherds for their families and/or for other farmers in return for wages in kind or in cash. Men are responsible for veterinary care of all livestock.

• Water points / reservoirs / Fishing: Women use water points and reservoirs for household consumption and fishing. They fish in rivers, lakes, and ponds. Some women also participate in fisheries where enough water is available. Some women also farm fish in their farm ponds, often with the support of projects. The women and girls are productively engaged in the processing of fish.

• Market gardening: Women and girls, men and boys take part in market gardening. It's mostly done by women and girls who sell their produce at markets or to middlemen. Young boys help their mothers on the market-gardening sites. Marketing is carried out by the women themselves with the support of some organizations. In most cases where women are involved in these groups, the men often act as administrative secretaries (keeping administrative documents). In general, the women are supported by certain development partners who provide them with seeds and other inputs for their market gardening activities.

• Forests: women and girls are involved in collecting firewood for household consumption all year round. They also collect fruits, nuts, and leaves from trees and shrubs on the household's plot. With increasing insecurity due to conflict, men are getting invovled in collecting firewood and other forest products. Women and girls collect a variety of forest products such as shea butter and néré in Mopti; shea butter, néré and Zâbou in Ségou and Koulikoro; leaves such as Fakohouyé, Joulou, doum fruit and water lilies in Timbuktu; henna in Bananara and Koulikoro. Women are also involved in the production of honey.

• Indigenous Populations and Peoples (IPP) level: land management methods, for example, are based on traditional practices that have been adapted to modern conditions. Political reforms undertaken at national level since the advent of democracy in 1992 have had major repercussions on certain traditional charteances, which totally exclude women, even though older women were involved in the decision-making process up until the event of the death of the legitimat chief, passed in the absence of consensus, by electoral means under the supervision of a commission appointed by the government to represent all ethnic peoples and commodity chains. The division of labour is as follows:

- Men: livestock production (cattle, sheep and goat farming [food, animal care], bourgou production in Mali, Timbuktu and Gao; fattening industry; beekeeping [sale of honey]; market gardening [production and sale of produce]. In Timbuktu, women also participate in the production of honey.

- Women: market gardening (production and sale of produce), sheep/goat and poultry fattening (feeding and watering);

- Young boys: honey production, market gardening (labour), fattening (grazing animals, watering); poultry (eggdons).

# Young girls: helping the family in the market gardening and fattening sectors.

IATF will engage with local men, women and youth based on their current involvement in the value chain and use their knowledge and experience to expand the value chains.


## Access and control of resources

Men and women, boys and young people can access and use all resources, but only with the agreement and/or permission of the heads of household. Women in particular, through their groups, can easily obtain plots of land for their agricultural activities, with the condition of electing a representative village chief/secretary chairman/woman and with the support of certain development partners.

### Control of the land

Women have only indirect control over certain socials. It is the responsibility of heads of families to manage landowners. Women have only indirect access to land. They generally benefit from plots of land acquired through a third party (usually a man: husband, head of lineage, other relative) and which are not registered in their names.

### Control of grazing

In Mopti, for example, in the flood zone, the management of the bounjouroutes is shared between men and women. The latter have the responsibility of passage of the animals for grazing. It should be noted that their animals are the first to go out to graze. The sale of household animals is generally the responsibility of the men. When it comes to selling their own livestock, women often sell them at a lower price than men.

### Forest resources

As for the women, they have control over harvested products such as gum arabic, néré, chèb, zaban, certain leaves, and acacia pods, which are very profitable to sell. In fact, each woman has her own tree or trees that she harvests from regularly. The sale of these pods is insufficient to build a value chain. In the agro-pastoral regions covered by the gender assessment, women have access to other resources such as firewood, water, and wild fruits, which are even anarchic by others. These resources are exploited in part by the community (management of communal areas), but also for personal use in agriculture, animal husbandry, handicrafts and traditional medicine, and play a particularly important role in satisfying their daily food, energy and medical needs, and more specifically by the women and young people


---
**Page 152**
---

who supply the families with firewood and other forest products. Thanks to forest by-products, based on fruits and leaves, women also carry out income-generating activities (IGAs).

The water resources/water are managed by the water masters, who are the bozos, and the ponds by the villagers.

### Facilities:
In rural areas, less than 20% of women have access to equipment. According to the report of the Ministry of Agriculture, only 15% of farmers own tractors in Mali. Mainly, access to equipment has a considerable impact on productivity and agricultural production. Given the relatively high cost of access, equipment is generally the property of the farm. Women's low income levels make it difficult for them to purchase or rent equipment. They do not have their own equipment and therefore have limited access to it. Of the women farmers who own equipment, 45% own a tractor, while only 10% own a plow. The type of equipment owned by women is exclusively men. Whatever the type of equipment, women's farms are always under-equipped compared to those of men.

Women's lack of equipment is a significant factor in their productivity. On the farm, they can only have access to the equipment when work has been completed on the other plots, which often leads to delays and losses.

#### 4.3. Access to and control of profits
In terms of access to and control over benefits, the analysis reveals that only men have access to benefits and income and decide who can have access to them. Women have control over their own income but are not able to make decisions about how this money is spent or how it will be used by their husbands beforehand.

The use of resources is decided by the heads of household according to the results of the assessment.

#### 4.4. Access to information and technologies related to natural resource management
In the villages, it is usually the village chief who is the first to be informed, then he informs the heads of families, who in turn inform the members of their families, including the women. Information is also shared through town criers*. Men are also informed through village meetings, radio, television, and newspapers. The use of mobile phones, which are used for communication services (water supply, forestry, animal husbandry), talks in griot* and "word of mouth" are also sources of information for men. Some women get information related to natural resource management from radio and television. In the health sector, women have access to information through health centres, men, women, girls and boys have access to information via meetings, radio, television, traditional healers, and sometimes training courses. In the livestock sector, the head of the family is informed by the shepherd in the event of illness and by the veterinary service for vaccination.

The access to technologies and services helps both men and women to improve farming methods and techniques, increase productivity and income, improve living standards and raise the social status of women. However, only 10% of rural women have access to these technologies and international NGOs*, have little access to technologies and services. The key constraints for women's access to technologies are: i) lack of education, which limits their ability to encourage women farmers to invest; ii) illiteracy among women, which limits their access to technologies and services; iii) lack of access to financial resources; and iv) customs and traditions

* Persons employed to make public announcement in the small village towns.

# Gender and value chains

Gender equality is an essential dimension of inclusive agricultural and economic growth, food and nutrition security and resilient food systems. Women play an important role in agriculture and in most cases are responsible for feeding their families.

In Mali, women are present to varying degrees throughout the agricultural value chain, participating in the processing and marketing of farm produce. In the agricultural sector, women are present through all stages of production, from planting seeds to harvesting crops (lack of access to land, lack of production and productivity (lack of access to control and ownership of land and means of production, etc.). They are generally involved in small-scale livestock farming and all the activities involving the processing and marketing of farm produce. However, their contribution is not always valued or accounted for. Women are mainly involved in growing food crops and marketed through informal channels (e.g., markets). The value chain analysis shows that women do not benefit from the economic benefits generated by agricultural production without counting on their contribution to food production or seeking to improve their access to land.

Also, despite the non-discriminatory texts, in practice, the influence of men dominates in terms of leadership and decision-making. Women's voices are often ignored or only considered when necessary. The results of the gender and value chain analysis are shown in Table 3 below:

Opportunities for women and young people to benefit from technical capacity-building in agriculture and agroforestry

With regard to farming and forestry in the 5 regions, it should be noted that women and young people benefit sporadically from technical capacity building sessions. This is due to a lack of staff at the service level, the shortage of staff in the technical services of the State's Agriculture and Water Resources Department (DASAWA), and the low number of women and young people on farming and agroforestry techniques. According to the results of the survey carried out by the DASAWA during the agricultural campaign in the Mopti region: "The number of staff is 148, but only 20 are women and young people." The proportion of women and young people among the total population is therefore of 46%. There are not enough staff to actively carry out the State's urgent and priority actions. This is also true for the region's 135 communes.

The number of plant health control officers is 13.


Access for women and young people to market activities and infrastructure linked to agriculture and agroforestry

It should also be noted that capacity building is generally carried out at the commune level, which is not always in line with the needs of these areas. They are carried out with limited numbers of women and young people who do not have the necessary skills or knowledge.

These capacity-building sessions last 2 to 3 days maximum. The participants in the capacity-building sessions give feedback to the remaining women and young people, but this feedback is not very good at passing on the information received during these sessions.


Access for women and young people to market activities and infrastructure linked to agriculture and agroforestry

Women and young people will be given access to market activities and infrastructure related to agriculture and agroforestry through:

- Promotion of the development of women's and young people's entrepreneurship projects in all lines of the various value chains.
- Prioritising the installation of women and young people on developed sites, particularly those that are not covered by the National Land Law and the Agricultural Land Law.

Constraints, opportunities and increased employment options and improved employment opportunities within agricultural value chains for women and young people

Proposals for better employment of women and young people in value chains

You will need :

- Facilitate their access to and control over resources such as land, infrastructure and inputs;
- Promote and develop women’s and young people’s entrepreneurship by setting up new enterprises or expanding existing ones through instant agro-businesses based on innovation (use of green energy) by women and young people specialised in their areas of activity and operating in the value chains;
- Provide these young companies with better ongoing training, access to markets, credit and other resources; facilitate their access to resources and funding, and simplified, real business plans;
- Facilitate communication between these young companies, input suppliers and potential buyers.


Constraints and opportunities associated with the increased adoption of modern smart agricultural and agrotechnological technologies by women and young people

The constraints are many and varied. As a large proportion (more than 50%) of farmers in this age group have no formal education, they lack skills to adopt agricultural and agro-technological innovations. This is due to the fact that these technologies are often complex and require knowledge on the techniques and, above all, lack of access to improved seeds adapted to their specific conditions. These technologies also need to be adopted more widely. In addition to these constraints, there is a low level of literacy among women (literacy rate: 57%) and insufficient knowledge about environmental issues.

As far as opportunities are concerned, the five regions have a wide range of agro-technological innovations in their production systems ranging from basic systems for the production of cereals and horticultural products from the south to the north, via the centre of the country. In addition to social and traditional constraints, factual constraints, in particular access to productive resources (land, goods, agricultural services, financial resources), are also holding back the emergence of women in the decision-making power.

Where are the men and women among the producers, wholesalers and processors?

In the case of cereals, men are more than 90% present at all levels, unlike market garden produce, where women are more than 95% visible. When it comes to fruit production, young people are more present in the field than men. In the case of citrus fruits, women and young people are present both as producers and processors.

Who controls the marketing channels?

When it comes to marketing channels, women are almost in majority when it comes to selling their produce at local markets or through cooperatives. As for market carters, they are mostly men.

Access to equipment and infrastructure for post-harvest management, processing, etc

According to the report Projet National Genre des Secteurs de l'Agriculture et du Développement Rural - Male access to equipment has a considerable advantage over women's access. This is due to the high cost of access; equipment in general is the property of the farm. Women's low level of education and their lack of knowledge about agricultural machines therefore have limited access to it. Of the women who own equipment, only 10% are able to use it independently without help from men or children. The other types of equipment are owned exclusively by farmers.

On the other hand, women have more access to post-harvest management compared with men's farms. The equipment on women's farms consists mainly of simple tools such as a knife and a basket for harvesting and productivity. On their farms, women can only dig the equipment when work is done in the fields or during harvest.

Post-harvest management: Women do not have control over family production, as it is the men who produce (in some cases), store and remove bundles of harvested products from the field. In monogamous households, women are more involved in decision-making. On the other hand, in polygamous households, it is usually the first woman to join the household who makes decisions about food production. The second wife wants to take in terms of food security. In agricultural households, it is usually the husband who decides what will be produced and how much. He can also decide which members of the family and to needy neighbours towards whom they feel a certain responsibility.

Controlled by men: Women are not involved in decisions about the quantity of food that should be stored or consumed daily. They do not know when to buy more food, nor when to sell it. The decision is made by the husband who knows how much food has come from the woman's own resources (income from the sale of livestock products or from parallel activities). She only decides if he asks her opinion.

# Analysis results

## Access to and control over resources and income generation and young people

Under customary law, women are excluded from land ownership, even though they play a major role in land use. Owning certain assets, such as land and houses, can confer a degree of financial and social autonomy, and can increase their autonomy, their level of income and even their purchasing power. In the event of marital breakdown, women have no means for protecting them in the event of marital breakdown. According to FAQ statistics, between 2013 and 2015, 48% of married women were victims of domestic violence; more, women who generate income do not have full access to these resources. In the case of land ownership, for example, if a woman owns land, they would produce 20-30% more food... " FAO at work 2010 (2011)Although women are responsible for most agricultural activities in rural areas, Kouloukouno regions is made up of different ethnic groups spread throughout the territory. These groups are structured and organized by a strong social structure, such as mother and wives' associations. Women have assigned roles that determine their access to productive resources, their participation in decision-making processes and their ability to generate income. They are responsible for accompanying their family or husband in the exploitation and management of land and other natural resources. This is particularly true when it comes to women who own land jointly with others, which limits their access to resources in the same way as men. The same applies to young people.

However, it is important to point out that in rural areas, the community or the men temporarily hand over small plots of land to women, especially married women, for them to cultivate and sell the produce. This practice helps strengthen the ingredients of the condiment and generating a little income, which can be taken at any time. According to the EDGR VI 2018 report, 39.9% of young people aged between 15-24 years old are married or in a relationship alone. Those who own land jointly, i.e., with other people, are estimated at around 67%.

The young people participate in the family farm and part of the income from their activities is used to pay for school fees, buy food, pay rent, etc... as well as the cost of the young man's wedding or the young woman's dowry, or part of this money could be given to the young man to set up a business or go on an adventure.

## Participation of women and young people in decision-making

According to information gathered in the Séguo and Mopti regions, decisions are made by men. Women participate only as advisors at village council meetings, where they can give their opinions. Family decisions are taken by family councils, which are composed of men. Women have a consultative role and issue opinions, even if the decision often concerns them.

Despite the non-discriminatory texts, in practice, the influence of men over women is very strong. In fact, most decisions are generally taken by men in public and women are only consulted informally or not at all.

## Legal status of women and young people

The Malian Constitution clearly sets out the principle of equality and non-discrimination between men and women. It guarantees that they have equal rights and duties. Discrimination based on social origin, colour, religion, language, political opinion, ethnic or national origin is prohibited. Mali has ratified, often without reservation, several regional and international conventions and treaties relating to the protection and promotion of women's


---
**Page 159**

# 4.6. Power and decision-making.

In Mal, social norms and the sexual division of labour largely exclude women from public decision-making and conflict resolution. The representation* of women in all the institutions saw a timid increase between 2015 and 2018. In 2018, women held only 34% of seats in the National Assembly, while women party leaders accounted for just 2%. Similarly, female farm managers have less access to farmers' associations than male counterparts, which is a major obstacle to their participation in decision-making.

The proportion of women councillors was 8.3% in the 2009 elections, i.e. 1 percentage points higher than in 2004 and 4.3 percentage points higher than in 1999.

In 2020, a law was passed to comply with the provisions of Law No. 2015-052, which obliges political parties to include one-third women on proposed electoral lists. This obligation was met, and out of 79,238 candidates, there were 26,436 women, i.e. 33.4%.

*Source: EDIP VI 2018 report
*Source: Report on the representation of women in positions of responsibility in the Public Administration_2015
*Source: Election report/Verifrein, 2016

## Access for women and young people to information about climate change, the environment, agriculture and agroforestry

Information on climate, the environment and agriculture is disseminated and accessed through a variety of channels, including radio, TV, newspapers and the internet. Radio and TV, newspapers and the internet are generally accessible in urban areas but less so in rural areas. The means for smart phones with internet and also the source of information about climate change, the environment, agriculture and agroforestry is "SÉNÉKAL", but this is not widely used by the population.

In 2018, 54% of women and 63% of men said they had heard of SÉNÉKAL. In fact, 39% of women do not use any of these three media - newspapers, radio or television - to obtain information about climate change, the environment, agriculture and agroforestry. The most widely used medium: 47% of women and 54% of men said they listened to the radio at least once a week.

In Koulikoro and Segou regions, 36.6% and 43.8% of women respectively and 29.1% and 30.8% of men respectively are exposed to all three media at least once a week. In the same areas, 28.9% and 35.7% of women and 30.6% and 40.0% of men respectively are exposed to radio only for urban areas and 46.1% for rural areas. For men in the same areas, 28.9% and 37.4% are exposed to radio only for urban areas and 50.0% for rural areas.

In the light of these data, we can deduce that women's and young people's access to information on the climate, environment, agriculture and agroforestry is less than that of men. Moreover, young people, especially women, do not always have access to the means of communication which are the main source of information about climate change and the measures taken by governments to combat it. This lack of access makes women more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) identifies women as a priority group for climate change as women, young people, the elderly, the disabled, orphans and widows.


---
**Page 160**
---

Transition (TAT) has twenty-eight (28) women out of one hundred and forty-seven (147 ) members, i.e., 19%. The current transitional government has only six women out of 32 members, i.e. 20.6%. Out of fifteen (15) governors, only one is a woman (6.7%).

The causes of women's low representation in decision-making bodies are: (i) political will, (ii) negative social perception of women politicians, (iii) women’s low political maturity, their low level of education and lack of experience, (iv) the absence of women in the civil service, which does not grant economic or development resources at all levels. With regard to decision-making, according to the 2015 survey conducted by the UNDP, 48% of women participate in decisions about their family’s finances; 30% participate in the decision for major household purchases and 20% for their children's education. Only 6% of women participate in decisions on elections and 63% declared not to participate in any of the three decisions mentioned above.

The 2.36% representation rate* of women in conflict management bodies (CSA, DDRM, MOC, CRES) is also very low compared with other countries in the region. The efforts made during the transitional justice process, efforts to take gender into account are visible, in particular at the level of Truth, Justice and Reconciliation Commission, which has created a sub-committee on gender issues. However, women’s representation remains very weak: only 10% of women out of four pillars of the CJUR's work. The CJUR has also been the most representative peace mechanism in terms of gender, with 25% of women in its composition and 37% for the appointment of women to the CSA in 2020. This consideration of gender is also reflected in the large number of statements made by women (50%)**.

According to the analysis of the gender dimension of the situation, it should be noted that although older women are highly regarded, their role is generally limited to mediating family disputes, passing on knowledge and skills up to a certain age. In the case of land management, women temporarily farm small plots of land, growing livestock, farmers; women have the right to use income from the sale of milk. In both cases, they have little say in the management of public affairs and conflicts.

In the areas covered by the gender analysis, even when women are involved in exploiting and developing the land (market gardening and reforestation), they cannot own it, even though they have a right to use it. Women also have no access to credit or loans from banks. They have ideas and ideas to put forward for better management. At community level, there are many village groups, often led by men. In the case of land management, women can only manage small plots of land in customary cities in traditional institutions where decisions are taken. Until the outbreak of communal violence, these groups were mixed, made up mainly of Dogon and Peulh women.

Despite the fact that the project has been implemented for more than two years (since 2017), the IATT project, the power relationship has not changed overall, and women's priorities relating to the management of natural resources or conflicts are still little taken into account in public decisions and local governance. Women have no access to land titles or deeds, which is a major obstacle in terms of access to land management and ownership, which remains a highly discriminatory area for women. The lack of land tenure security is also due to the fact that women are often victims as a result of the insecurity, accentuating women's dependence on their men in their families to protect them from threats. Women have no access to credit or loans from banks. They can only join armed groups, for protection against other armed groups or the FDS, where they do not trust. Where male family members are absent due to conflict or migration, women have to provide for their families. Many have been forced into prostitution and begging in order to meet their basic needs and economic activities. They have demonstrated their resilience by finding endogenous solutions to meet their needs. At the same time, women's real reproductive roles and their contribution to economic

### 4.7. Availability and use of gender indicators

To improve the profitability of the work of rural women active in key production sectors (agriculture,
livestock, fisheries and the environment), PNG-Mail has included gender indicators in its action
plan:

- Percentage of women with access to developed land,
- Percentage of women and young people who have benefited from funding from the
  National Rural Finance Fund,
- Percentage of women who have applied modern techniques for the production, processing
  and sustainable conservation of agricultural, livestock and fishery products,
- Percentage of women trained and supervised in sustainable plant production and
  regeneration techniques,
- Productivity rate of women who have benefited from inputs in areas at high risk of natural
  disasters,
- Women's rate of access to financial resources and factors of production.

These indicators set out in the NGP-Mail should serve as a reference for the various stakeholders,
particularly the IAAT project.

### 4.8. Rights and legal status

#### 4.8.1. How women and men are treated in customary and formal legal codes and by judicial
systems

Discussions in groups and with key informants show that women and men are viewed differently
by customary and formal legal codes, as well as by judicial systems. Details of these differences can
be found in Table 4.

31


---
**Page 162**
---

Table 4. How women and men are treated in customary and formal legal codes and by judicial systems

| Questions | Answers |
| --- | --- |
| How women and men are viewed and treated by: | Formal legal codes |
| Customary law | - There is a difference between men and women in the legal code. |
|  | - According to the marriage code, the man is the head of the family. |
|  | - It's the man who chooses where to live. |
|  | - The child takes his father's surname. |
|  | - In the case of inheritance, women do not have the same right as land and men. Even though the law provides for equal rights, it has developed to be allocated to women and young people. |
|  | - Women are considered minors. |
|  | - There is a difference in access to employment, due to women's low level of education. |
| Religious inheritance | - Religious traditions determine inheritance differently. |
| Customary inheritance | - Women themselves are an inheritance. |
| Religious heirs | The wife receives a third of the inheritance. |
| Formal legal code | The man does not benefit from the advantages of the death of his wife, and he cannot inherit the property of his deceased wife. |
| Employment | There is a difference in access to employment, due to women's low level of education. |

As can be seen from Table 5, formal legal codes treat men and women in the same way, without distinction, unlike customary codes, which treat men and women differently. As for the expiation of wrongs and legal representation, the legal provisions prevail and no distinction is made between men and women. However, there are some differences when it comes to divorce: if a woman applies for a divorce, if the judge or magistrate is a man, he tends to incriminate the woman in order to make her pay the price of the divorce she applied for a divorce46. In this respect, it is also worth noting that formal legal codes strictly codify religious codes, so that when the woman concerned is a Muslim, for example, Islamic law prevails.

### 4.8.2 Assessment of documents issued by the State, such as identity cards, registration certificates and voter cards

- **Identity cards:** in accordance with current legislation, the conditions for obtaining identity cards at the police station are different from those required to obtain a voter card. In some areas, in most cases, the birth certificate used to issue identity cards are held by men, and women have to fight hard to obtain them. The men equate this document with their own identity and refuse to give it to their wives. In some cases, women do not have the resources to obtain identity documents, and often it is the procedures for obtaining cards that they are unfamiliar with.
- **Voter cards:** in many areas, women are reluctant to register to vote due to illiteracy, socio-cultural constraints, refusal by husbands, etc.
