## 4.11. Gender and COVID - 19

The COVID-19 pandemic, which was officially declared in Mali in March 2020, has claimed 461 deaths, including 138 women in the Mopti region<sup>**45**</sup>. COVID-19 has further weakened the most vulnerable groups of society. Women and girls are particularly affected by this pandemic because they work in markets, near offices and in schools so their incomes fall and were trapped in their day-to-day activities, in most cases taking no appropriate measures to protect themselves. Women's daily informal income is often derived from selling goods at local markets or working as domestic workers. The configuration of Malian society meant that it was they who were in constant contact with other members of the family, including children and elderly people, which made them more vulnerable to spreading the disease to the rest of their family. In times of conflict, the pandemic has only increased the vulnerability of women who have been displaced or living in camps. The gendered nature of violence that has been underlined and which already suffer from the arrival of a pandemic on the scale of COVID-19.

A study commissioned by the UNFPA highlighted the fact that cohabitation could have led to acts of violence within households. Inactivity and lack of income have also been identified as factors leading to GBV in Mali. It should be noted that men's dominant position within the household, due

<sup>**45**</sup> Based on discussions during regional consultations
<sup>**46**</sup> Source: DRPSAP - Mopti


---
**Page 165**
---

to their financial situation, has been affected and has caused stress and irritation**. The closure of international borders and market restrictions have had a major impact on informal traders. Also, with the pandemic, women suffered more gender-based violence due to general social stress combined with increasing tensions surrounding the fact that the family lived in unusual proximity, in addition to the lack of access to information and services. Women also experienced difficulties at work on which many women had previously depended for support were now weakened due to reduced physical presence at work and the need to stay home to care for children and elderly relatives. Women relied heavily on traditional and miracle healers and medicines, and rumours spread more quickly than official information from the relevant authorities in whom communities had no confidence. The lack of access to water and sanitation services was a major problem for women when they were carrying out domestic work such as cooking or fetching water. Curfews and restrictions on movement also made it difficult for women to access these services.

In remote areas, they did not have access either to good information or to materials enabling them to protect themselves against disease. Women's work schedules were increased by the need to look after children and elderly relatives at home. The lack of access to water and sanitation services on health kits (masks, soap, bleach, hand-washing buckets, etc.). With the outbreak of the COVID-19 pandemic, women experienced a number of difficulties in accessing essential services: (i) since the outbreak of COVID-19, particularly health services; GBV management services and education services; (ii) schools were closed in order to control the spread of COVID-19. As a result, children had no access to school meals or other forms of support that they would normally receive during this period; (iii) due to the closure of markets and shops, women found it difficult to be able to buy food for their families as well as essential items such as soap and water; (iv) due to the lack of access to information on how to protect themselves against COVID-19, many women did not know what measures they should take in order to protect themselves. This was particularly true for women who were living with disabilities or had limited mobility.

The needs and interests of women and girls (and people with disabilities) are neither represented nor specifically taken into account in the planning and response to the pandemic. In terms of government responses, it is clear that women's voices have not been heard regarding the interventions relating to COVID-19, in particular on the economic, health, sanitation and hygiene measures put in place by governments. When it comes to access to information and communication, many people, especially women, knew that there were health measures in place but did not know what they should do or how to protect themselves against COVID-19 and health. They were not aware of the care facilities, let alone the core protection. The telephone and local radio stations were effective means of communication for reaching the most vulnerable women who had limited access to information through other channels such as social media, including the involvement of women in community dialogue via social networks and WhatsApp groups, and the creation of a specific WhatsApp group for women with disabilities. This would have allowed women to be able to assess the scale of COVID-19 and become more resilient to this pandemic.

### 4.12. Violence against women, combating female circumcision

Analysis of the EDSVM data shows that the Malian population is lax and insensitive when it comes to domestic violence. There is still no specific law on violence against women (domestic violence) in Mali. The lack of a legal framework for the protection of women is one of the rationales of these acts of violence and the social pressure on the victims explain the widespread impunity enjoyed by the perpetrators.

Exclusive to female circumcision, the age range between 15 and 49. In terms of combating the practice, the National Programme to Combat Female Genital Mutilation (NPFGM) is in place but it has not yet legislated against female genital mutilation.

The scale of gender-based violence (GBV) has been exacerbated by the political, institutional and security situation in Mali. The GBV is also linked to the presence of armed groups and the activities of armed groups and violent extremism. According to the MICS 2015, female genital mutilation is the most widespread form of GBV in Mali (37% of women aged between 15 and 49) and 48.6% of girls who have been subjected to female genital mutilation are under 15 years old.

The data presented here refers only partially reflect the real situation of GBV in the country, given the weakness of the judicial system and impunity. Women have very little access to justice services (4%), unlike men (70%). The number of women who have been subjected to female genital mutilation have suffered a draft special law drawn up and validated in 2017 by national multi-sectoral and multi-party committee under the leadership of the MPEFF is still in the process of being adopted entirely at executive level.

### 4.13. Conflict prevention and management mechanisms

As far as women's involvement in conflict resolution is concerned, conflict resolution and prevention mechanisms are still dominated almost exclusively by men in the communities and are therefore highly discriminatory for women, even beyond any religious or cultural considerations, despite the fact that they have proven potential and capabilities in conflict resolution and prevention. Women represent only 10% of mediators in Mali, compared to more than 90% of men. They account for less than 10% of peace negotiators, and this figure has hardly changed since the adoption of resolution 1325 on women, peace and security in 2000.

The promotion of women's participation in conflict prevention and management mechanisms will promote behavioral change towards a culture of inclusiveness in formal and informal conflict resolution and prevention. This is linked to the local level as well as improved access for women to mobile and resilient economic activities and opportunities. It also contributes to the contestation of local leaders, opinion leaders and religious leaders, the exclusion and discrimination against women.

The lack of involvement of women in conflict prevention mechanisms exacerbates women's vulnerability. IAAT presents an opportunity to reverse this trend by relying on women's participation at all levels of decision-making. Women play a key role in family and community units in matters of social cohesion and conflict management. Past experience, particularly with peace nuts in Mali, has shown that it is women who are mobilised and involved in the resolution of conflicts at the local level as well as at the national level, especially as they themselves can play an active role in triggering these conflicts.

The translation of Law No 10/2015 of December 2015 and the dissemination in the NAT project areas should also be considered.


---
**Page 167**
---

In all the areas covered by the gender analysis, prevention and management mechanisms were found to exist.

### Legislative provisions

- **The State judicial system:** it operates according to legal rules; it is the law, and is not very active in prevention.
- **Land Co-operatives (CoFs):** these are an emanation of the State (cf. Agricultural Land Law, LFA, N°2017-601 of 11 April 2017); they are set up at the level of all communes. Village CoFs are not set up but village chiefs are members of communal CoFs. The commune is responsible for their operation and management (the commune has a known responsibility for their operation). Women and young people are poorly represented in these CoFs. But with the support of certain development partners, this trend is changing.
- **Departmental reconciliation committees:** these are set up by the departmental directorate of the national reconciliation support mission (MARN) within the ministry responsible for national reconciliation. These committees have a dual role: to provide technical assistance and to train reconciliation teams in all regions with the following missions: to inform and raise awareness about conflict resolution mechanisms; to promote the use of these mechanisms, conflict management mechanisms; to prevent, mediate and manage intra- and inter- community conflicts; and to support reconciliation at local level. As of 31.12.2020, the following committees were operational: in the region of Centre-Val de Loire (4); in the region of Hauts-de-France (5).
- **Women and young people are poorly or not at all represented in the PRRAs in the regions of Centre-Val de Loire and Hauts-de-France.**
- **The RCGs are set up by the PRIRAs in the municipalities. Their purpose is to prevent and resolve, as close as possible to the complex realities on the ground, sources of conflict and the dynamics that lead to them. The RCGs are set up where there has been a commune already a functioning reconciliation mechanism in the area. There is also a lack of women's participation in these committees.**
- **The watchdog committees:** which are usually set up by certain development actors, are not operational. Women have said that they are represented on these committees. For example, in the region of Centre-Val de Loire, the commune of Saint-Pierre-des-Corps has a committee.
- **Local authorities (mayors and other elected representatives):** born out of the political nature of local government, they are not always trained to manage conflicts or mediate; the conflict resolution actions of this player are often condemned by the judiciary.
- **Village committees:** these are set up by the commune directorate, and most of them function well in their local facilities. They include a surveillance water point management committee, dispute management committee.

As for the successes and failures of these schemes, they are varied

The main success lies in the amicable settlement of conflicts at village level (action by the village chiefdom, with the village chief being a member of the communal CoFs). The shortcomings noted were: lack of awareness among women about their rights; lack of understanding of the roles of the members of the watch committees, with communities wondering why the watch committees? The consequences of failure are generally the cause of social disintegration and family poverty.

# 4.14. The situation of displaced persons

With the security situation, women and girls are becoming increasingly vulnerable. Many women have become heads of households (the male active being killed, abducted or fled) and are left to fend for themselves. Displaced are camped in camps in large numbers, without any construction. At national level, for example, there are 412,387 displaced returnees and 120,296 internally displaced persons (IDPs). The proportion of women among the IDPs is around 50% of women*. This category of vulnerable people should be taken into account in the IAAT project.

## 4.17. Constraints faced by women in pursuing their practical and strategic gender interests

The obstacles facing women identified by the analysis are:

- The coexistence of modern and customary law, which enshrines the hierarchy of the sexes and gender stereotypes (social roles attributed to each sex);
- Female illiteracy;
- Women's lack of access to sources of information on climate change and the measures that need to be taken;
- Low awareness among women’s associations of the need to adapt to the effects of climate change;
- Women’s poor knowledge of sustainable management and rational use of natural resources;
- Women’s low resilience to climate change;
- The low representation of women in conflict prevention and management frameworks and mechanisms;
- Customs and traditions;
- Socio-cultural and religious constraints;
- Women's lack of self-confidence /Women's fear of commitment.

## 5. Recommendations for the IAAT project

The best and most effective ways to empower women and girls include:

1. Increasing access to resources needed for climate adaptation and mitigation: The gender assessment shows that women in the project locations have less access to finance, technology, and information on climate change and its associated risks. IAAT needs to support them to increase women’s access to income by increasing their involvement in VSLAs, linking them to financial institutions, training them on financial management skills, and providing them with microfinance loans. In addition, the IAAT should also strongly advocate community leaders, including elected representatives, traditional authorities, and religious leaders, for greater recognition of women's access to and control of resources and benefits by bringing them into the CACs process. Gender equality must be implied while selecting beneficiaries and distributing project resources (e.g., seeds, tools, inputs such as fertilizers, pesticides, livestock, trees/seeds/breeds, etc.) and agro-ecological inputs (e.g., planning materials and equipment).

2. Creating space for women: gender assessment shows that women have low decision-making power in the household and limited access to resources needed for climate adaptation and mitigation. In addition, women's involvement in entrepreneurship is very low. IAAT’s capacity-building activities should focus on increasing women’s participation in market-based activities (both market and climate information), and building entrepreneurship skills for the selected businesses (such as value addition and post-harvest management, market gardens, agro-forestry products, etc.). Creating space for women in business environments across the

**Results of group work during national and regional consultations**


---
**Page 171**
---

3. Linking with the private sector: our assessment indicates that men are more engaged with the private sector service providers such as agriculture product collectors and aggregators and input suppliers (e.g., seed, feed, animal health services, and tools/services). Women's engagement in these activities is limited. Therefore, involving the private sector service provider can increase their participation in the existing market and new value chains. This will also help in increasing women's economic empowerment by integrating them into the value chains as key actors.

4. Access to extension services: Women’s access to extension services as well as their involvement in agricultural extension is limited. IAFI can develop a gender-sensitive CSA curriculum to integrate into the existing agriculture extension system (for both public and private sector service providers) that focuses on improving input use, production and marketing in agriculture and agroforestry sectors. Currently, women’s involvement in agriculture extension services is very low in Mali due to their low education rate and limited access to information and communication technologies. To ensure the training and capacity building of extension service providers, women should be included as much as possible in these activities. In addition, IAFI can also train female extension officers in Mali's cultural setting where women are less engaged with men extension officers.

5. Empowering women in early warnings and climate action: In Mali, women are placed at greater risk through a lack of timely and relevant information about climate risks and lack of equal access to information services and technologies. Moreover, women’s voices are often not heard or taken into account when decisions on climate change adaptation and mitigation are made. Increasing women's access to early warning systems and climate information services can empower them in making informed decisions for their families and communities. IAFI can support women's access to existing early warning systems and climate information services, and build their capacity to use this information for climate change adaptation and mitigation decisions at their farms and communities.

#### 7. Gender Assessment in Project Implementation

IAAT will engage in further consultations with community members across all 12 circles during the project's initial planning phase. These consultations and in-depth gender assessment aim to ensure that the interests and needs of women, men, girls, and boys are fully integrated during the project implementation process. The assessment will identify and address gender-based vulnerabilities and marginalization—such as gender, education level, head of household status, marital status, disabilities, age, and other social factors—that intersect and magnify vulnerability to climate change impacts. This assessment will also identify opportunities for interventions that address gender and climate change adaptation and mitigation needs. Additionally, the assessment will help address root causes of vulnerability, supporting more sustainable and impactful gender outcomes. This assessment will support in generating the baseline for gender integration in IAAT's activities, monitoring project outcomes during the project period and impact evaluation.
